# Property Case Briefs

*AI-drafted study aid. Check every brief against the opinion.*

## Jacque v. Steenberg Homes (Wisconsin Supreme Court, 1997)

**Facts**
- In the fall of 1993, a neighbor of the Jacques purchased a mobile home from Steenberg Homes, with delivery included.
- Steenberg Homes determined the easiest delivery route was across the Jacques' land.
- Steenberg Homes asked the Jacques multiple times for permission to cross their land.
- The Jacques refused permission each time, due to a prior adverse possession loss.
- On February 15, 1994, Steenberg Homes decided to deliver the mobile home across the Jacques' land despite their refusals.
- Mr. Jacque confronted Steenberg employees who were planning to cross his land and reiterated that permission was denied.
- Steenberg's assistant manager came to the Jacques' home, where the Jacques showed proof of ownership and again refused permission.
- The assistant manager asked Mr. Jacque how much money it would take, but Mr. Jacque stated it was not about money and told them to use the road.
- After leaving the Jacques' home, Steenberg's assistant manager allegedly told employees to "get the home in there any way you can" and to block the road to conceal their actions.
- Steenberg employees used a "bobcat" to cut a path through the Jacques' snow-covered field and hauled the mobile home across their land.
- A neighbor informed the Jacques that Steenberg had crossed their land.
- Mr. Jacque called the sheriff's department, and an officer issued a $30 citation to Steenberg's assistant manager.

**Procedural History**
- The Jacques sued Steenberg Homes in circuit court for intentional trespass, seeking compensatory and punitive damages.
- Steenberg Homes conceded intentional trespass but argued no compensatory damages were proven and punitive damages could not be awarded without them.
- The jury awarded the Jacques $1 in nominal damages and $100,000 in punitive damages.
- The circuit court set aside the $100,000 punitive damage award.
- The court of appeals affirmed the circuit court's decision, feeling bound by precedent that nominal damages cannot support punitive damages.
- The Jacques appealed to the Wisconsin Supreme Court.

**Issue**
Can an award of nominal damages for an intentional trespass to land support an award of punitive damages?

**Holding**
Plaintiff (Jacques) wins. When nominal damages are awarded for an intentional trespass to land, punitive damages may be awarded in the discretion of the jury. Reversed and remanded.

**Rules**
- **Punitive Damages (General Rule).** Punitive damages cannot be awarded if only nominal compensatory damages are found.
- **Rejected: Punitive Damages (Intentional Trespass Exception).** The general rule that nominal damages cannot support punitive damages does not apply when the tort is an intentional trespass to land.
- **Right to Exclude.** The private landowner's right to exclude others from their land is one of the most essential rights of property.
- **Intentional Trespass Harm.** In intentional trespass to land, actual harm occurs even if immeasurable in dollars, as the legal right to exclude is violated.
- **Prospective Application (Sunbursting).** A judicial holding that departs from past precedent is generally applied retroactively, but prospective application (sunbursting) may be used to mitigate hardships when retroactive application would be inequitable, especially when there has been reliance on an overruled decision by a substantial number of persons and considerable harm could result.
- **Punitive Damages (Excessiveness Factors).** When determining if a punitive damage award violates the Due Process Clause, courts consider: (1) the degree of reprehensibility of the conduct; (2) the disparity between the harm or potential harm suffered and the punitive damage award; and (3) the difference between the remedy and comparable civil or criminal penalties.

**Reasoning**
- The court recognized that the general rule requiring compensatory damages to support punitive damages (from *Barnard v. Cohen*) had never been squarely addressed in the context of intentional trespass to land.
- The court found the rationale for the general rule (that society has little interest in deterring harmless conduct) inapposite for intentional trespass to land.
    - **Individual Interest:** The right to exclude others from one's land is a fundamental property right, recognized by both state and federal courts.
        - This right is "hollow" if the legal system provides insufficient means to protect it.
        - A nominal damage award (like a "halfpenny") does not adequately protect this right.
    - **Actual Harm:** The law infers some damage from every direct entry upon another's land, meaning actual harm occurs in every trespass, even if not measurable in dollars.
    - **Potential for Harm:** A series of intentional trespasses can lead to loss of ownership through adverse possession, demonstrating the potential for significant harm.
- **Societal Interest:** Society has an interest in punishing and deterring intentional trespassers beyond protecting individual landowners.
    - **Integrity of the Legal System:** Landowners should have confidence that wrongdoers will be punished, reducing the likelihood of "self-help" remedies.
    - **Deterrence:** A $30 forfeiture and $1 nominal damage award are insufficient to deter future intentional trespasses by companies like Steenberg Homes, who might otherwise find it more profitable to trespass.
        - Punitive damages must exceed the profit from illegal activity to be effective.
    - **Prosecution of Claims:** Allowing punitive damages incentivizes plaintiffs to pursue claims involving egregious conduct that might otherwise go unpunished.
- The court found that Steenberg's "reliance" on the *Barnard* rule was misleading because they only conceded intentional trespass after overwhelming evidence was presented.
    - Applying the new rule to Steenberg was justified to reward the Jacques for challenging the unsound old rule.
- The court found the $100,000 punitive damage award was not excessive based on the *Gore* factors.
    - **Reprehensibility:** Steenberg's conduct was highly reprehensible due to its indifference to the law, repeated refusals, and deceitful actions.
    - **Disparity:** While the ratio of nominal to punitive damages was large (1:100,000), a fixed multiplier is not appropriate when nominal damages do not reflect the actual harm of violating a fundamental right.
    - **Comparable Penalties:** The $30 forfeiture for simple trespass was largely irrelevant because it did not contemplate the egregious nature of Steenberg's actions and was insufficient to deter future conduct.

**Dissent ([Judge])**
None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is crucial for understanding the **right to exclude** as a fundamental "stick in the bundle" of property rights, echoing the sentiment in *Jacque v. Steenberg Homes*. It demonstrates that even without measurable economic harm, the violation of this right can warrant significant punitive damages. It connects to the concept of **trespass introductory concepts** by emphasizing the importance of protecting property boundaries and the state's role in endorsing the private citizen's right to exclude. The court's discussion of adverse possession also links it to **acquisition and claim scope**.
2. This case raises the question of how courts balance the need for deterrence and vindication of rights against concerns about excessive punitive damage awards, especially when actual damages are minimal. What if the trespass had been accidental but still caused emotional distress? Would the outcome be the same?

**Takeaway:** The right to exclude others from one's land is so fundamental that its intentional violation, even without measurable economic harm, can justify substantial punitive damages to deter future misconduct and uphold the integrity of the legal system.

## Hinman v. Pacific Air Transport (United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit, 1936)

**Facts**
- Appellants owned 72.5 acres of land in Burbank, California.
- Appellants claimed ownership of the airspace above their land to an altitude of at least 150 feet.
- Appellees, commercial airlines, operated aircraft through this airspace at altitudes as low as 5 feet above the surface.
- Appellants notified appellees to stop trespassing.
- Appellees continued to operate aircraft through the airspace.
- Appellees' aircraft followed two defined courses over appellants' land.

**Procedural History**
- Appellants filed a bill against Pacific Air Transport and another against United Air Lines Transport Corporation.
- The trial court sustained motions to dismiss the original bills.
- Appellants filed amended bills.
- The trial court sustained motions to dismiss the first amended bills.
- Appellants filed second amended bills (or first amended in one case).
- The trial court sustained motions to dismiss these bills.
- Appellants appealed the decrees sustaining the motions to dismiss.

**Issue**
Can a landowner claim absolute ownership of the airspace above their property to an indefinite height, and sue for trespass when aircraft fly through it without causing actual damage or interference with the landowner's use?

**Holding**
Defendant (appellees) wins. A landowner does not have absolute ownership of the airspace above their property to an indefinite height, and aircraft flying through it without causing actual damage or interference with the landowner's use does not constitute trespass. Affirmed.

**Rules**
- **Ad Coelum Doctrine (Rejected).** The ancient maxim "cujus est solum ejus est usque ad coelum et ad inferos" (whoever owns the soil, owns up to the sky and down to the depths) is not the law and was never intended to be taken literally.
- **Property Rights in Airspace.** The essence of property is dominion over it, and property must be capable of exclusive possession.
- **Air as Property.** The air, like the sea, is by its nature incapable of private ownership, except insofar as one may actually use it.
- **Extent of Airspace Ownership.** A landowner owns as much of the space above the ground as they can occupy or make use of in connection with the enjoyment of their land, and this right varies with their needs and is coextensive with them, but only so long as they use it. All that lies beyond belongs to the world.
- **Trespass to Airspace.** Any use of airspace by others that is injurious to the landowner's land, or constitutes an actual interference with their possession or beneficial use thereof, would be a trespass for which there would be a remedy.
- **Easement by Prescription (Airspace).** It is not legally possible to obtain an easement by prescription through the airspace above another's land.
- **Damages for Trespass.** If a legal injury is proven but the extent of the injury is not shown, nominal damages only can be recovered.

**Reasoning**
- The **ad coelum doctrine** is rejected because it is a figurative phrase that would lead to absurd results if taken literally.
    - The doctrine was invented when use of space above land was confined to narrow limits and meant that the owner could use overlying space to the extent they were able, and no one could interfere with that use.
    - A literal construction would mean ownership of infinity, which is impossible.
- **Ownership of airspace** is limited to what is actually used or occupied in connection with the enjoyment of the land.
    - Title to airspace unconnected with the use of land is inconceivable and not known to the law.
    - The right to airspace is not fixed but varies with needs and is coextensive with them.
    - A landowner's title to the air is paramount only to the extent that no one can acquire a right to the space above him that will limit him in whatever use he can make of it as part of his enjoyment of the land.
- It would be **impracticable and lead to confusion** if landowners could claim definite, unused spaces in the air to protect contemplated future use.
    - Such a rule would be a departure from human experience and reason.
    - It would be difficult for courts to protect varying claims and enforce rights like ejectment.
    - This rule is not necessary to protect constitutional rights in property.
- **Flying through airspace is not inherently a trespass.**
    - It is a lawful act unless it causes actual injury to the landowner's possession.
    - Appellants did not allege facts showing actual or substantial damage, only a naked conclusion.
    - Therefore, the complaint did not state a case for injunctive relief or more than nominal damages.
- An **easement by prescription** cannot be obtained for airspace.
    - The primary thing being used by planes is the air itself, which is generally held not subject to prescriptive easements.

**Dissent (Mathews)**
None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case significantly limits the traditional ad coelum doctrine, establishing that a landowner's rights in airspace are not absolute but are tied to actual use and enjoyment of the surface. It connects to *Jacque v. Steenberg Homes* by showing that while the right to exclude is fundamental, it is not limitless, especially when the "trespass" occurs far above the surface and causes no actual harm. It also contrasts with cases like *Edwards v. Sims*, which dealt with subsurface rights, by highlighting the different considerations for air rights.
2. This case raises the question of how high "actual use" extends, and what constitutes "actual interference" in the context of modern technology. What if the planes were flying so low that they created significant noise or vibrations, even if they didn't physically touch the property?

**Takeaway:** A landowner's property rights in airspace are limited to the portion they can actually use and occupy in connection with their land, and mere flight through unused airspace without actual damage is not a trespass.

## Hendricks v. Stalnaker (Supreme Court of Appeals of West Virginia, 1989)

**Facts**
- In 1984, the Hendrickses bought 2.95 acres adjacent to Stalnaker's property for a home or trailer development.
- In 1985, Stalnaker built a home on his 10-acre tract and had two water wells dowsed. One well produced poor quality water due to a former strip mine. The other was near the Hendrickses' property.
- On December 31, 1985, Mr. Hendricks met with a sanitarian to find locations for a water well and septic system. The Health Department requires 100 feet between wells and septic systems. The only suitable location for a septic system on the Hendrickses' land was near Stalnaker's property.
- On January 13, 1986, the Hendrickses contacted the sanitarian to complete their septic system permit application.
- On January 13, 1986, Stalnaker called the sanitarian and learned about the Hendrickses' proposed septic system.
- On January 14, 1986, Stalnaker contacted a well driller, obtained a permit, and began drilling the second well near the Hendrickses' property.
- On January 15, 1986, the sanitarian informed Mr. Hendricks that no septic system permit could be issued because his proposed absorption field was within 100 feet of Stalnaker's new water well.
- The well was completed on January 25, 1986, but not connected to Stalnaker's home until January 1987.
- In January 1987, Mr. Hendricks installed a septic system without a permit, leaving it inoperative pending the lawsuit.

**Procedural History**
- The Hendrickses sued Stalnaker in the Circuit Court of Lewis County, seeking to declare the well a private nuisance, abate it, and obtain damages.
- In a bifurcated trial, the jury found the well was a private nuisance, and the judge ordered its abatement. The jury found for Stalnaker on damages.
- Stalnaker appealed to the Supreme Court of Appeals of West Virginia.

**Issue**
- Is a water well a private nuisance if it prevents a neighbor from installing a septic system, when both uses are necessary for residential land use and neither party has a practical alternative?

**Holding**
- Defendant Stalnaker wins. The water well is not an unreasonable use of land and therefore does not constitute a private nuisance. Reversed.

**Rules**
- **Private Nuisance.** A private nuisance is a substantial and unreasonable interference with the private use and enjoyment of another’s land.
- **Intentional Interference.** An interference is intentional when the actor knows or should know that the conduct is causing a substantial and unreasonable interference.
- **Unreasonable Interference (Balancing Test).** An intentional interference is unreasonable when the gravity of the harm outweighs the social value of the activity alleged to cause the harm.
    - Factors for gravity of harm include: extent and character of harm, social value of the invaded use, suitability of the invaded use to the locality, and burden on the harmed person to avoid harm.
    - Factors for utility of conduct include: social value of the primary purpose of the conduct, suitability of the conduct to the locality, and impracticability of preventing the invasion.

**Reasoning**
- The Hendrickses' inability to operate a septic system is a substantial interference with their land use.
- Stalnaker's installation of the well was intentional, but not malicious; he sought an adequate water supply and found no alternative.
- The critical question is whether the interference (the well) was unreasonable, determined by balancing the landowners' interests.
    - Both a water well and a septic system are necessary for residential use of the land.
    - Both uses burden adjacent property: a well requires a 100-foot non-interference zone, and a septic system's 100-foot safety zone may intrude on adjacent property. A septic system places a more invasive burden due to potential drainage.
    - Both parties lack inexpensive and practical alternatives for their respective uses.
    - The balancing of interests is at least equal, or slightly favors the water well, because both are necessary and neither party has a clear alternative.
- The Hendrickses did not show that the balancing of interests favored their septic system.
- The evidence does not demonstrate that the water well is an unreasonable use of land.
- When material facts are undisputed and only one inference can be drawn, the question becomes one of law for the court. Here, the trial court should have held as a matter of law that the well was not a private nuisance.

**Dissent ([Judge])**
- None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case illustrates the balancing test for private nuisance, particularly when two essential land uses conflict. It connects to **Jost v. Dairyland Power Cooperative** and **Campbell v. Seaman** by showing how courts weigh the utility of the defendant's conduct against the harm to the plaintiff's use and enjoyment of land. Unlike those cases where the defendant's activity was clearly harmful (smell, fumes), here both parties' desired uses are beneficial and necessary, leading to a more even balance.
2. What if Stalnaker had drilled the well solely to spite the Hendrickses, knowing it would prevent their septic system, even if he had another viable water source? Would that change the outcome under the "malicious or indecent conduct" factor mentioned by the court?

**Takeaway:** When two necessary and reasonable land uses conflict, and neither party has a practical alternative, a court applying a nuisance balancing test may find neither use to be unreasonable.

## Jost v. Dairyland Power Cooperative (Wisconsin Supreme Court, 1969)

**Facts**
- Dairyland Power Cooperative operated a power plant that emitted sulfur fumes into the atmosphere.
- The Josts and other neighboring farmers complained about the sulfur fumes for several years.
- The sulfur fumes caused damage to the farmers' alfalfa crops and other vegetation.
- The farmers sued Dairyland Power Cooperative for damages.

**Procedural History**
- Trial court found Dairyland Power Cooperative liable for nuisance and awarded damages for crop loss and diminution in market value for one plaintiff.
- The trial judge changed the jury's "no" answer to the "substantial damage" question to "yes."
- Dairyland Power Cooperative appealed.

**Issue**
- Can a defendant in a nuisance action for damages avoid liability by showing due care or that the social utility of its conduct outweighs the harm to the plaintiffs?

**Holding**
- Plaintiff farmers win. A defendant in a nuisance action for damages cannot avoid liability by showing due care or that the social utility of its conduct outweighs the harm to the plaintiffs. Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded for a new trial on the issue of diminution of market value for all plaintiffs.

**Rules**
- **Nuisance.** A nuisance is a field of tort liability that refers to the interests invaded and the harm inflicted, rather than a particular type of tortious conduct.
- **Negligence and Nuisance.** Negligence is merely one type of conduct that may give rise to a nuisance.
- **Due Care in Nuisance Actions.** Freedom from negligence is no defense if the consequences of continued conduct nevertheless cause substantial injury to a claimant.
- **Balancing Utility vs. Harm in Nuisance Damages.** In a suit for damages for nuisance, the doctrine of comparative injury (balancing the utility of the offending conduct against the gravity of the injury inflicted) is not applied in Wisconsin.
- **Substantial Injury.** Substantial injury is defined as tangible injury or a discomfort perceptible to the senses of ordinary people.
- **Permanent Nuisance Damages.** When a nuisance is continuing and permanent, damages for diminution in market value are appropriate, and future recovery for specific items of damage on a year-by-year basis is generally not permitted unless the level of nuisance increases.

**Reasoning**
- The case was properly tried on a theory of nuisance, not negligence, as made clear by plaintiffs' counsel from the outset.
- The trial judge correctly changed the jury's answer regarding substantial damage because the evidence showed tangible injury to property (alfalfa crops and vegetation) that was more than nominal.
- The court properly excluded evidence of Dairyland's due care because **freedom from negligence is not a defense to a nuisance action** where substantial injury occurs.
- The court properly excluded evidence of the social and economic utility of the power plant because **Wisconsin law does not permit balancing the utility of the offending conduct against the gravity of the injury in damage suits for nuisance**.
    - To allow a socially useful enterprise to injure others without compensation would be a taking of property without due process.
    - The rule from *Pennoyer v. Allen* (1883) remains applicable: the interruption of enjoyment and destruction of comfort furnishes the ground of action, and it is no defense that the business was conducted reasonably.
- The court erred in finding no diminution in market value for all plaintiffs, as the continuing nature of the nuisance and the undisputed damage to vegetation indicated a permanent injury.
    - Damages for a permanent nuisance should be reflected in a diminution of market value.

**Dissent ([Judge])**
- None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is important for understanding the distinction between nuisance and negligence, and particularly for the rule that **due care is not a defense to a nuisance claim for damages**. It connects to *Jost v. Dairyland Power Cooperative* by reinforcing the idea that property owners have a right to be free from substantial interference, even if the interfering activity is otherwise lawful and conducted with care. It also contrasts with cases where balancing of equities might be considered, such as in injunction requests (though the court here explicitly states that issue is not before them).
2. What if the power plant was the only source of electricity for a large region, and shutting it down would cause widespread hardship? Would the court's reasoning still hold for an injunction request, or would the "comparative injury" doctrine apply then?

**Takeaway:** In Wisconsin, a defendant cannot escape liability for damages caused by a nuisance by arguing they used due care or that their activity has greater social utility than the harm it causes.

## Campbell v. Seaman (New York Court of Appeals, 1876)

**Facts**
- Around 1849, the plaintiffs, Samuel B. Campbell et al., acquired 30-40 acres of land in Castleton, New York.
- From 1857 to 1859, the plaintiffs built an expensive dwelling-house on their land and improved the land with grading, terracing, roads, walks, and planting ornamental and useful trees and shrubs.
- The defendant, Nathan N. Seaman, owned adjoining land which he had used as a brick-yard for some years.
- The defendant's brick-yard was about 1,320 feet south of the plaintiffs' dwelling-house and 567 feet south of their woods.
- In 1869 and 1870, the defendant burned bricks using anthracite coal.
- During the burning process, sulphurous acid gas was generated, which escaped from the kiln during the last two days of burning.
- When the wind blew from the south, this gas was carried over the plaintiffs' land.
- The gas killed the foliage on the plaintiffs' white and yellow pines and Norway spruce, and after repeated attacks, killed 100 to 150 valuable pine and spruce trees, and injured their grape vines and plum trees.
- The defendant's premises had been known and used as a brick-yard for over twenty-five years, but the use of anthracite coal for brick burning was not continuous for twenty years. From 1840 to 1853, no bricks were burned. From 1853 to 1857, bricks were burned, then discontinued until 1867. From 1857 to 1867, the land was used for agriculture.
- The plaintiffs objected to the brick burning before bringing suit.

**Procedural History**
- Plaintiffs brought an action to recover damages and to restrain the continuance of the alleged nuisance.
- A referee found in favor of the plaintiffs, awarding damages and an injunction.
- The General Term of the Supreme Court affirmed the judgment.
- Defendant appealed to the New York Court of Appeals.

**Issue**
Can a landowner be enjoined from operating a lawful business on their property if it causes substantial and irreparable injury to a neighbor's property, even if the business existed before the neighbor improved their land?

**Holding**
Plaintiff (landowner) wins. A landowner can be enjoined from operating a lawful business on their property if it causes substantial and irreparable injury to a neighbor's property, even if the business existed before the neighbor improved their land. Affirmed.

**Rules**
- **Nuisance.** Every person is bound to make a reasonable use of their property so as to occasion no unnecessary damage or annoyance to their neighbor. An unreasonable, unwarrantable, or unlawful use of property that produces material annoyance, inconvenience, discomfort, or hurt to a neighbor constitutes a nuisance.
- **Reasonable Use.** What constitutes a reasonable use of one's property depends on the circumstances of each case; a use lawful and reasonable in one locality may be unlawful and unreasonable in another.
- **Irreparable Injury.** An injury is irreparable if the damaged property cannot be replaced, or if money damages cannot adequately compensate for the loss of unique items.
- **Injunction for Nuisance.** An injunction can be rightfully demanded to prevent irreparable injury, interminable litigation, and a multiplicity of suits.
- **Injunction Discretion.** The discretion of the court in granting or refusing an injunction is not arbitrary and can be corrected on appeal if improperly exercised.
- **Prior Use (Rejected).** It does not matter that the alleged nuisance-causing activity existed before the plaintiff acquired or improved their land.
- **Prescriptive Right to Nuisance (Rejected).** A prescriptive right to continue a nuisance can only be acquired by continuous use for twenty consecutive years.
- **Acquiescence (Rejected).** Acquiescence short of twenty years will not bar a complaint of nuisance unless the plaintiff's conduct induced the defendant to incur large expenditures, forming an estoppel.
- **Damnum Absque Injuria (Rejected).** While a reasonable and lawful use of land may cause some inconvenience or damage that is *damnum absque injuria*, one cannot place anything on their land that the law would pronounce a nuisance and thereby compel a neighbor to leave their land vacant or restrict its use.

**Reasoning**
- The defendant's brick burning was a nuisance because the sulphurous acid gas caused tangible and appreciable injury to the plaintiffs' property, killing trees and damaging other vegetation.
- The injury was substantial and irreparable because the destroyed ornamental trees and vines could not be replaced, and their value was difficult to estimate in monetary terms.
- An injunction was appropriate to prevent interminable litigation and a multiplicity of suits, as the injury was recurring with each brick burning.
- The fact that the nuisance was not continual, but only occasional (when the wind blew from the south), did not preclude an injunction, as the damage was significant.
- The defendant's prior use of the land as a brick-yard did not give him a right to continue the nuisance, as one cannot establish a nuisance on vacant land and thereby control a neighbor's future use.
- The defendant had not acquired a prescriptive right because the use of anthracite coal, which caused the damage, had not been continuous for twenty years.
- The plaintiffs did not acquiesce in the nuisance; their conduct did not induce the defendant to incur large expenses that would create an estoppel.
- The damage to the plaintiffs was large and substantial, while it was not clear that restraining the defendant would cause him equally large damage, as he could potentially make bricks elsewhere or use his land for other profitable purposes.
- The court rejected the argument that the brick-making business was useful and necessary, stating that similar decisions in England had not caused embarrassment to the industry, and suitable locations for brick-making could be found without harming neighbors.

**Dissent ([Judge])**
None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is a foundational nuisance case, establishing that a lawful business can be enjoined if it causes substantial harm to a neighbor's property. It connects to *Jost v. Dairyland Power Cooperative* by illustrating the court's willingness to grant injunctive relief for nuisance, even against economically productive activities, when the harm is significant. It also contrasts with cases like *Adams v. Cleveland-Cliffs Iron Co.* which might focus more on the "reasonableness" of the use in an industrial context, whereas here the court emphasizes the tangible injury to the plaintiff's enjoyment of their property. The discussion of "irreparable injury" and "multiplicity of suits" is key for understanding when equitable remedies like injunctions are granted over mere damages.
2. What if the defendant could prove that their land was uniquely suited for brick-making and that an injunction would effectively destroy their business, leading to a much greater economic loss for them than the plaintiffs' damages? Would the court still grant an injunction, or would it lean towards damages?

**Takeaway:** A lawful business can be a private nuisance if it causes substantial and irreparable harm to a neighbor's property, warranting an injunction even if the business predates the neighbor's improvements.

## Adams v. Cleveland-Cliffs Iron Co. (Michigan Court of Appeals, 1999)

**Facts**
- The Empire Mine, operated by Cleveland-Cliffs Iron Company and Empire Mining Partnership, engaged in blasting, extracting, and processing iron ore 24 hours a day, year-round.
- Plaintiffs, 55 individuals living near the mine in Palmer, complained of dust, noise, and vibrations emanating from the mine.
- Plaintiffs alleged the blasting caused tremors, and dust accumulated inside and outside their homes, leading to masonry cracks, window replacement, plumbing and sewer leaks, carpet and drape replacement, and increased cleaning and repainting.
- Many plaintiffs testified that noise and vibrations caused shock, nervousness, and sleeplessness.
- Several plaintiffs claimed these conditions diminished their home values, making some unmarketable.
- Emissions from the mine consistently remained within applicable air-quality standards, but the amount of particulate matter accumulating over Palmer was four times greater than in surrounding communities.
- Plaintiffs sued for damages under theories of trespass and nuisance.
- The trial court instructed the jury that an unauthorized intrusion onto land, including emissions, dust, vibration, or noise, could constitute a trespass.
- The jury found for 52 plaintiffs on the trespass claim, awarding $599,199, but could not reach a verdict on the nuisance claim.
- The trial court denied the defendants' motions for a new trial or judgment notwithstanding the verdict.

**Procedural History**
- Plaintiffs sued defendants in Marquette Circuit Court for trespass and nuisance.
- The jury found for 52 plaintiffs on the trespass claim and awarded damages; it was hung on the nuisance claim.
- The trial court denied defendants' post-trial motions.
- Defendants appealed to the Michigan Court of Appeals.

**Issue**
Can a landowner recover for trespass in Michigan for intrusions of airborne particulate, noise, or vibrations?

**Holding**
Defendant (Cleveland-Cliffs Iron Co.) wins. Recovery for trespass to land in Michigan requires proof of an unauthorized direct or immediate intrusion of a physical, tangible object. Reversed and remanded.

**Rules**
- **Trespass.** An invasion of a landholder’s interest in the exclusive possession of the land.
- **Nuisance.** An interference with a landholder’s use and enjoyment of the land.
- **Traditional Trespass Elements.** Recovery for trespass requires an unauthorized direct or immediate intrusion of a physical, tangible object onto land over which a plaintiff has a right of exclusive control.
- **Trespass Damages.** Once a trespass is proved, a plaintiff is entitled to at least nominal damages, even without proof of other injury.
- **Nuisance Damages.** Recovery for nuisance traditionally requires proof of actual and substantial injury and balancing the disturbance against the social utility of its cause.
- **Rejected: Modern Trespass View.** The court rejects the view that trespass can include indirect, intangible invasions that interfere with exclusive possessory interests, often requiring actual and substantial damages and balancing social utility.
- **Tangible Object Requirement.** Noise, vibrations, and dust are generally considered intangible and thus not actionable in trespass. Dust particles do not normally occupy land in a meaningful sense.
- **Direct Intrusion Requirement.** A direct or immediate invasion for trespass is one accomplished by any means the offender knew or reasonably should have known would result in the physical invasion of the plaintiff’s land.

**Reasoning**
- The court aims to preserve the separate identities of trespass and nuisance.
- **Trespass** protects the right to exclusive possession, while **nuisance** protects the right to use and enjoyment.
- The traditional view of trespass required a direct or immediate invasion by a physical, tangible object.
- Intangible irritants like dust, smoke, noise, and vibrations were historically addressed under nuisance law.
- The "modern view of trespass," which allows recovery for intangible intrusions and requires actual damages, effectively blurs the line between trespass and nuisance.
- This blurring undermines the traditional principle of presumed damages in trespass, which is essential to protecting the right to exclude.
- Requiring actual and substantial damages and balancing them against social utility is appropriate for nuisance, not trespass.
- The court finds no Michigan precedent that unambiguously eliminates the traditional distinction between trespass and nuisance.
- The court rejects the argument that *Adkins v. Thomas Solvent Co.* implicitly adopted the modern view of trespass, noting that *Adkins* was a nuisance case and its reference to modern trespass was dicta to illustrate a point about nuisance.
- Noise and vibrations are clearly not tangible objects and cannot give rise to a trespass action.
- Dust, while physically composed of particles, does not normally present itself as a significant physical intrusion and is generally considered intangible for trespass purposes.
- The trial court's instruction, allowing trespass for intangible irritants and requiring proof of damages, erroneously conflated trespass with nuisance.
- The proper remedy for noise, vibrations, or ambient dust that interferes with use and enjoyment is an action for nuisance, requiring proof of significant harm from unreasonable interference.

**Dissent ([Judge])** / **Concurrence ([Judge])**
None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is crucial for understanding the distinction between **trespass** and **nuisance**, particularly regarding intangible invasions. It contrasts with cases like *Jost v. Dairyland Power Cooperative* and *Campbell v. Seaman*, which dealt with similar types of invasions but often under nuisance theories or with different jurisdictional approaches to the trespass/nuisance boundary. It reinforces the idea that the "bundle of sticks" includes distinct rights of exclusion (trespass) and use/enjoyment (nuisance).
2. [CHECK: Is the court's definition of "direct" intrusion, adopting the Restatement's "substantial certainty" standard, a liberalization or a strict adherence to the traditional directness requirement? How does this interact with the tangibility requirement?]

**Takeaway:** In Michigan, trespass requires a direct, physical, and tangible intrusion, while intangible interferences like noise, vibrations, and ambient dust are properly addressed under nuisance law, which requires proof of actual and substantial harm.

---

## Spur Industries Inc. v. Del E. Webb Development Co. (Arizona Supreme Court, 1972)

**Facts**
- Around 1911, farming began in the area west of Phoenix, Arizona.
- In 1954, the retirement community of Youngtown was started about 1.5 miles north of Olive Avenue.
- In 1956, Spur's predecessors developed cattle feedlots about 0.5 miles south of Olive Avenue.
- By 1959, there were 25 cattle feeding operations within a 7-mile radius of Spur's location.
- In May 1959, Del Webb began planning Sun City, a new urban development, and purchased 20,000 acres of farmland for this purpose.
- In September 1959, Del Webb started constructing a golf course, and Spur's predecessors began leveling ground for more feedlot area.
- In 1960, Spur purchased the feedlot property and expanded its operations.
- In January 1960, Del Webb began selling homes in Sun City, with the first unit completed about 2.5 miles north of Spur's feedlot.
- By May 1960, Del Webb had 450-500 houses completed or under construction.
- Del Webb continued developing Sun City southward, closer to the feedlot.
- By 1963, sales resistance due to feedlot odors became a serious problem for Del Webb in the southwestern portion of its development.
- By December 1967, Del Webb's property extended south to Olive Avenue, and Spur's feedlot was within 500 feet north of Olive Avenue.
- Del Webb filed a complaint alleging that over 1,300 lots were unfit for residential development due to Spur's feedlot operation.
- At the time of the suit, Spur was feeding 20,000 to 30,000 head of cattle.

**Procedural History**
- Del Webb sued Spur Industries, seeking to enjoin the feedlot operation as a public nuisance.
- The trial court found Spur's operation to be a nuisance and permanently enjoined it.
- Spur appealed the injunction.
- Del Webb cross-appealed.

**Issue**
1. Can a lawful cattle feedlot operation be enjoined as a nuisance when a nearby residential area develops around it?
2. If the nuisance can be enjoined, must the developer who created the residential area indemnify the feedlot operator for moving or ceasing operations?

**Holding**
- Del Webb (developer) wins in part, Spur (feedlot operator) wins in part.
- The feedlot operation can be enjoined as a public nuisance, but the developer who brought residents to the nuisance must indemnify the feedlot operator for the cost of moving or shutting down.
- Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded.

**Rules**
- **Public Nuisance.** A public nuisance affects the rights enjoyed by citizens as part of the public, impacting a considerable number of people or an entire community.
- **Private Nuisance.** A private nuisance affects a single individual or a small number of persons in the enjoyment of private rights.
- **Statutory Public Nuisance.** Conditions in populous areas that constitute breeding places for disease-carrying insects are specifically declared public nuisances dangerous to public health.
- **Locality Rule.** Whether a lawful occupation constitutes a nuisance depends significantly on the locality and surroundings; what is a nuisance in a populous area may be unobjectionable elsewhere.
- **Standing for Public Nuisance.** A private party can sue to enjoin a public nuisance if they can show special injury.
- **Equity Powers.** Courts of equity may go further to give or withhold relief in furtherance of the public interest than when only private interests are involved.
- **Coming to the Nuisance (Rejected as absolute bar).** A residential landowner may not get relief if they knowingly moved into an area reserved for industrial or agricultural endeavors and were damaged thereby. *However, this doctrine is not an absolute bar when public interest is involved.*
- **Balancing Equities.** The law of nuisance is elastic and requires what is fair and reasonable under all circumstances, especially when considering public interest and economic development.

**Reasoning**
- **Spur's operation is an enjoinable public nuisance:**
    - The feedlot produced massive amounts of manure, causing flies and odor that annoyed residents of Sun City.
    - This affected a considerable number of people, making it a public nuisance.
    - Arizona statute § 36-601(A)(1) declares conditions that breed disease-carrying insects in populous areas to be public nuisances.
    - The locality rule dictates that a business can become a nuisance if it affects a populous neighborhood.
    - Del Webb had standing to sue because it suffered special injury (loss of sales) due to the nuisance.
- **Del Webb must indemnify Spur:**
    - While the "coming to the nuisance" doctrine would normally bar relief for Webb if it were the only injured party, the public interest in protecting Sun City residents from the nuisance is paramount.
    - Spur's business was lawful and established in an agricultural area before Sun City developed.
    - Del Webb knowingly developed residential properties closer to the feedlot, taking advantage of lower land values in a rural area.
    - It would be inequitable to force Spur to bear the entire cost of moving when Del Webb created the situation that made the injunction necessary.
    - The court uses its equitable powers to balance the public's right to be free from nuisance with the fairness to a lawful business forced to relocate due to encroaching development.
    - This remedy is limited to cases where a developer foreseeably brings a population into a previously agricultural/industrial area, necessitating an injunction against a lawful business.

**Dissent ([Judge])**
- None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is a classic example of a **nuisance** claim and introduces the concept of "coming to the nuisance." It shows how courts balance competing land uses and public welfare. It connects to **Jost v. Dairyland Power Cooperative** and **Campbell v. Seaman** by illustrating how lawful activities can become nuisances due to their impact on neighbors, but it adds a unique equitable remedy not seen in those cases. It also contrasts with **Boomer v. Atlantic Cement Co.** (cited in the opinion), where the court allowed damages instead of an injunction, whereas here an injunction is granted but with compensation.
2. This case raises the question of who should bear the cost of land-use conflicts when one party's lawful activity becomes problematic due to another's development. What if Spur had expanded its operations significantly *after* Sun City began developing nearby? Would the indemnification still be appropriate?

**Takeaway:** A lawful business can be enjoined as a public nuisance even if the plaintiff "came to the nuisance," but equity may require the plaintiff to indemnify the defendant if the plaintiff created the conditions that made the injunction necessary.

---

## Pardee v. Camden Lumber Co. (Supreme Court of Appeals of West Virginia, 1911)

**Facts**
- The plaintiffs, Barton Pardee and others, owned land with standing timber.
- The defendants, Camden Lumber Company and others, were cutting timber on the plaintiffs' land.
- The plaintiffs filed a bill for an injunction to prevent the cutting of timber.
- An action of ejectment to determine title to the land was pending between the parties.
- The trial court dissolved the injunction.
- A jury verdict in the ejectment action was rendered in favor of the defendants, but no judgment was entered on the verdict.

**Procedural History**
- The Circuit Court of Webster County dissolved an injunction against the defendants.
- The plaintiffs appealed to the Supreme Court of Appeals of West Virginia.
- Reversed and Remanded.

**Issue**
- Can a landowner obtain an injunction to prevent the cutting of timber on their land when the title is in dispute, without alleging the trespasser's insolvency or other circumstances making a legal remedy inadequate?

**Holding**
- The plaintiffs (landowners) win. An owner of land with standing timber can obtain an injunction to prevent its cutting, even if the trespasser is solvent, because the legal remedy of damages is inadequate for the destruction of growing timber. Reversed and Remanded.

**Rules**
- **Injunction for Injury to Growing Timber.** The wrongful destruction of growing timber, which is "part and parcel of the land on which it stands," is an injury to the land itself that is not adequately remediable by an action at law.
- **Protection of Owner of Standing Timber.** An owner of land has a legal right to keep timber in its natural state until they choose to alter it, and equity will protect this right.
- **Insolvency Not Required for Timber Injunction.** To obtain an injunction to prevent timber trespass, it is not necessary to allege the trespasser's insolvency or any other circumstance rendering an action at law futile.
- **Injunction Pending Title Dispute.** When land title is in dispute and an ejectment action is pending or imminent, the claimant out of possession may enjoin the other party from cutting timber until the title question is determined by the law court.
- **Verdict Not Judgment.** A jury verdict in a law action, not yet carried into judgment, does not justify the dissolution of an injunction.
- Rejected: **Insolvency Requirement for Timber Injunction.** To obtain an injunction against timber cutting, the plaintiff must aver good title, trespass by the defendant, and the defendant's insolvency or other circumstances rendering a damages action futile.

**Reasoning**
- The court re-examined the long-standing rule requiring proof of insolvency for an injunction against timber cutting due to public and professional dissatisfaction and the increased value of timber.
- Overruling the prior rule does not disturb property rights but rather conserves and protects them by providing a more complete remedy for property owners.
- The prior rule was based on the supposed adequacy of legal remedies for timber cutting, which the court now finds unsound.
- Standing timber is part of the real estate and has no legal equivalent in nature or value; cutting it changes its legal nature and incidents.
- Legal remedies (damages or detinue) are inadequate because they cannot restore the timber to its natural state or replace it with an equivalent.
- The general policy of the law guarantees an owner the right to possession, dominion, and immunity from injury, especially for property that cannot be substantially replaced.
- The court analogizes to specific performance for unique personal property (heirlooms, limited stock) and real estate, where damages are inadequate.
- The distinction between contractual rights concerning real estate (always subject to equitable cognizance) and trespasses thereon (historically denied equitable relief without insolvency) is inconsistent.
- The wrong done by a tenant (waste) and a stranger (trespass) to timber is of equal gravity, yet historically, injunctions were granted against tenants but not strangers, based on a flawed theory of legal remedy adequacy.
- The historical view of timber as an encumbrance rather than a valuable asset led to the erroneous rule, but conditions have changed, making timber scarce and valuable.
- The rule is contrary to the weight of authority in other jurisdictions.
- A jury verdict alone, without a judgment, is not a final adjudication of title and does not justify dissolving the injunction.

**Dissent (Brannon, Judge)**
- None. (Concurrence, but not a dissent)

**Concurrence (Brannon, Judge)**
- Agrees with the majority's decision to overrule prior decisions denying injunctions against timber cutting.
- Believes the rule of those decisions is "so bad that it ought not to stand."
- States that an erroneous decision should be overruled if its continued operation does more harm than its reversal.
- The overruled decisions have been "doing mischief all the time," and overruling them will avoid that mischief without harm, especially since it relates only to remedy.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is important because it significantly expands the availability of injunctive relief for trespass to land, particularly for the destruction of natural resources like timber. It connects to cases like *Jacque v. Steenberg Homes* by emphasizing the owner's right to exclude and the inadequacy of damages for certain property interests, even when the harm is not "irreparable" in the traditional sense of the trespasser being insolvent. It also highlights the evolving nature of property law in response to changing societal values and economic conditions, similar to how nuisance law evolved in cases like *Jost v. Dairyland Power Cooperative* or *Spur Industries Inc. v. Del E. Webb Development Co.*
2. This case raises the question of how courts should balance the principle of *stare decisis* with the need to correct what they perceive as erroneous or outdated legal rules, especially when those rules impact property rights and remedies.

**Takeaway:** Courts may grant injunctive relief for trespass to land, even without a showing of the trespasser's insolvency, when the injury involves the destruction of unique or irreplaceable natural resources like standing timber, as damages are an inadequate remedy.

---

## Pierson v. Post (New York Supreme Court of Judicature, 1805)

**Facts**
- Post was hunting a fox with his dogs and hounds on "wild and uninhabited, unpossessed and waste land."
- Post was in view of the fox and actively pursuing it.
- Pierson, knowing the fox was being hunted by Post, killed the fox and carried it away.

**Procedural History**
- Post sued Pierson in a justice's court for trespass on the case.
- The justice's court rendered a verdict for Post (the plaintiff below).
- Pierson (the defendant below) sued out a certiorari, assigning as error that the declaration was not sufficient in law to maintain an action.

**Issue**
Can a hunter acquire a property right in a wild animal by merely pursuing it, such that another person who intercepts and kills the animal is liable for trespass?

**Holding**
Pierson (the now plaintiff) wins. Mere pursuit of a wild animal does not create a property right in the pursuer. Reversed.

**Rules**
- **Occupancy.** Property in wild animals (ferae naturae) is acquired by occupancy only.
- **Rejected: Mere Pursuit.** Mere pursuit of a wild animal, even with hounds and in view, does not vest property or right in the huntsman.
- **Rejected: Wounding without Capture.** Wounding a wild animal is not sufficient to establish property rights unless the animal is actually taken.
- **Occupancy (Majority Definition).** Occupancy requires actual corporal possession, or at least mortal wounding, circumventing, or ensnaring the animal to deprive it of its natural liberty and bring it within the pursuer's certain control.

**Reasoning**
- The court relies on ancient legal writers (Justinian, Fleta, Bracton, Puffendorf, Grotius) who define occupancy as requiring actual physical possession or control.
- Mere pursuit, even if continuous, does not constitute occupancy because it does not deprive the animal of its natural liberty.
- The court acknowledges that mortal wounding, or securing an animal with nets or snares, could be considered occupancy because it demonstrates an unequivocal intention to appropriate the animal and deprives it of its natural liberty.
- The facts of this case involve "mere pursuit" and do not meet the definition of occupancy.
- Adopting a rule that mere pursuit creates a property right would lead to uncertainty, quarrels, and litigation, which the court seeks to avoid for the sake of "certainty, and preserving peace and order in society."
- Pierson's act, though "uncourteous or unkind," caused no legal injury for which a remedy could be applied.

**Dissent (Livingston, J.)**
- The question should be decided by sportsmen, not ancient legal texts, as they would understand the customs of hunting.
- The court should consider the public policy of encouraging the destruction of "noxious beasts" like foxes.
- Allowing an interloper to take a fox after a long chase would discourage hunting and thus reduce the destruction of such pests.
- Property in wild animals can be acquired without bodily touch or manucaption, provided the pursuer has a reasonable prospect of taking the animal and has demonstrated an intention to convert it to his own use.
- The dissent embraces Barbeyrac's view that actual bodily seizure is not always necessary for possession.
- The pursuit in this case, with "dogs and hounds of imperial stature," would have "inevitably and speedily terminated in corporal possession," thus conferring a right to the fox.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is foundational for understanding the **rule of capture** in property law, particularly for wild animals. It establishes a bright-line rule for when property rights attach to unowned resources. It contrasts with cases like *Keeble v. Hickeringill*, which focused on malicious interference with trade, rather than the property right itself.
2. What if Post had shot the fox, but it ran off and Pierson found it still alive but mortally wounded? Would that be "occupancy" under the majority's rule?

**Takeaway:** To acquire a property right in a wild animal, one must not merely pursue it, but must actually capture it or at least deprive it of its natural liberty.

---

## Goddard v. Winchell (Iowa Supreme Court, 1892)

**Facts**
- Since 1857, John Goddard owned land in Winnebago County, Iowa.
- On May 2, 1890, an aerolite weighing about 66 pounds fell onto Goddard's land and buried itself three feet deep.
- The next day, Peter Hoagland dug up the aerolite with a spade in the presence of Goddard's tenant, Elickson.
- Hoagland took the aerolite to his house and claimed ownership.
- On May 5, 1890, Hoagland sold the aerolite to H.V. Winchell for $105.
- Winchell took possession of the aerolite.

**Procedural History**
- The district court found for the plaintiff, Goddard.
- The defendant, Winchell, appealed.

**Issue**
- Does an aerolite that falls from the sky and buries itself in land belong to the landowner or to the person who discovers and digs it up?

**Holding**
- Plaintiff (Goddard) wins. An aerolite that falls and becomes embedded in the soil becomes part of the land and belongs to the landowner. Affirmed.

**Rules**
- **Quicquid plantatur solo, solo cedit (Whatever is affixed to the soil belongs to the soil).** This rule means that anything permanently attached to the land becomes part of the realty.
- **Rejected: Title by Occupancy.** This rule states that occupancy is taking possession of things that belonged to nobody, and movables found on the surface of the earth and unclaimed by any owner belong to the first occupant or finder.
- **Rejected: Finder's Keepers (Lost or Abandoned Property).** The finder of lost or abandoned property is the owner against all the world except the former owner.

**Reasoning**
- The aerolite became a part of the soil because it came to its position through natural causes and was embedded three feet deep.
- It was a "natural deposit" and not a "movable thing 'on the earth'" but "in the earth."
- The aerolite's substance, a stone, was not foreign or unnatural to the soil.
- The rule of "title by occupancy" applies to movables found "on the surface of the earth or in the sea," not to things embedded in the earth.
- The aerolite was never lost or abandoned, so the rules for lost or abandoned property do not apply.
- The court analogizes the aerolite to other natural deposits like boulders and drift left by glaciers, which belong to the landowner.
- The fact that the aerolite may have scientific value does not change its ownership; the landowner is as capable of contributing to scientific advancement as the finder.
- The court acknowledges the uniqueness of the case and the lack of exact precedent but finds its conclusion most analogous to generally accepted rules of law.

**Dissent ([Judge])** / **Concurrence ([Judge])**
- None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is a classic example of the **accession** doctrine, where something becomes part of a larger, more valuable thing. It contrasts with cases like *Pierson v. Post* or *Keeble v. Hickeringill* which deal with the capture of wild animals (ferae naturae) and the concept of first possession for unowned things. Here, the court determines the aerolite was not "unowned" once it became embedded in the land. It also touches on the distinction between chattels and realty, a theme seen in cases involving fixtures.
2. What if the aerolite had just bounced off the surface and was lying on top of the grass? Would it still belong to Goddard, or would it be considered a "movable" subject to the finder's rule?

**Takeaway:** Things that become naturally embedded in the land are considered part of the realty and belong to the landowner, not the finder.

---

## Baker v. Howard County Hunt (Court of Appeals of Maryland, 1936)

**Facts**
- In 1924, Dr. Laurence H. Baker and Rebekah W. Baker acquired a farm in Howard County, Maryland.
- From 1926 to 1929, Dr. Baker conducted experiments on rabbits on the farm and also raised hogs and chickens.
- In 1930, the Howard County Hunt, an unincorporated association, was formed. Philip Bowen, the huntsman, furnished and hunted a pack of hounds for the club.
- In 1931, Dr. Baker noticed fox hounds and riders on his farm but paid little attention as he had few animals.
- By 1933, Dr. Baker's rabbit stock had greatly increased.
- On January 14, 1933, fox hounds overran the Bakers' farm, and Mrs. Baker was bitten while trying to drive them off.
- Following this incident, Mr. T. Stockton Matthews, president of the club, sent a letter to Dr. Baker expressing regret for the annoyance and damage.
- Dr. Baker complained that no real effort was made to keep the hounds off his property, and they continued to trample crops, break hot frames, disturb animals, and annoy Mrs. Baker.
- On one occasion in February 1936, Dr. Baker shot several hounds that were in his chicken yard, frightening his chickens.
- On March 9, 1936, Dr. and Mrs. Baker filed a bill of complaint against the Howard County Hunt and Philip Bowen.

**Procedural History**
- The Bakers filed a bill of complaint in equity seeking an injunction.
- The trial court dismissed the bill.
- The Bakers appealed.

**Issue**
- Can a landowner obtain an injunction against repeated trespasses by fox hounds and hunters that interfere with the peaceful enjoyment of their property, even if the trespasses are not continuous?

**Holding**
- The Bakers (appellants) win. Equity can provide injunctive relief against a series of trespasses that seriously interfere with a landowner's right to peaceful enjoyment of their property. Reversed and remanded.

**Rules**
- **Trespass by Animals.** While the owner of a dog not believed to be dangerous is not liable for its trespasses of its own volition, they are liable if they take it where they know it will likely damage property, or permit it to stray with that knowledge.
- **Injunction for Repeated Trespasses.** Equity will grant an injunction to prevent repeated trespasses that cause irreparable injury, where full and adequate relief cannot be granted at law, where the trespass goes to the destruction of the property as enjoyed, or to prevent a multiplicity of suits.
- **Adequacy of Legal Remedy.** To preclude equitable relief, the legal remedy must be fully adequate and complete, as practical and efficient as the remedy in equity.
- **Self-Defense of Property.** A landowner may kill a dog in defense of their person or property where circumstances justify a reasonable belief that such a measure is necessary to protect against harm.
- **Rejected: Fox Hunting Rights.** The rights of fox hunters are subordinate to the rights of landowners. There is no principle of law that justifies trespassing over the lands of others for fox hunting.

**Reasoning**
- The evidence showed that the defendants' hounds repeatedly overran the Bakers' farm and caused substantial injury.
- The defendants made no serious effort to prevent the trespasses, despite being warned.
- The resulting damage was of a character for which the Bakers could not obtain adequate relief at law.
    - The injuries were partly intangible and incapable of measurement, such as the interruption of Dr. Baker's rabbit experiments and interference with their plan to use the farm as a bird refuge.
    - The threat of continued trespasses interfered with their reasonable enjoyment of the property.
    - The expense and annoyance of prosecuting numerous actions at law for trifling damages made the legal remedy inadequate.
- The Bakers' act of shooting the hounds was justified under the circumstances, as the hounds were in the chicken yard frightening chickens, and thus did not bar equitable relief under the "clean hands" doctrine.
    - A statute permits killing any dog seen pursuing, worrying, wounding, or killing poultry or livestock.
- The court rejected the argument that the defendants' mere expression of an intention not to repeat a trespass would bar equitable relief, especially when their past conduct showed a contrary intention.
- The common law rule regarding liability for a single straying dog's trespasses does not apply to a pack of 15-20 hounds, which can cause substantial damage.

**Dissent ([Judge])** / **Concurrence ([Judge])**
None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case illustrates the limits of the **owner sovereignty** principle when faced with repeated invasions of property. It connects to cases like *Jacque v. Steenberg Homes* by emphasizing the importance of the right to exclude, but here, the remedy sought is an injunction rather than just damages, highlighting the inadequacy of legal remedies for certain types of trespass. It also touches on the common law rule regarding animal trespass, which is often distinguished from human trespass.
2. What if the fox hunting club offered to pay a yearly fee to the Bakers for the right to cross their land, but the Bakers still refused? Would the court still grant an injunction, or would it consider a liability rule (damages) more appropriate?

**Takeaway:** Equity will grant an injunction to protect a landowner's right to peaceful enjoyment against repeated trespasses, especially when legal remedies are inadequate.

---

## Johnson v. M'Intosh (Supreme Court of the United States, 1823)

**Facts**
- On May 23, 1609, King James I granted a charter to the Virginia Company, giving them land in North America.
- At the time of the charter, various independent Indian tribes occupied and possessed the territory in full sovereignty.
- In 1624, the Virginia Company's charter was dissolved, and its rights reverted to the English Crown.
- On July 5, 1773, chiefs of the Illinois Indians sold a large tract of land to William Murray and others (plaintiffs' predecessors).
- On October 18, 1775, chiefs of the Piankeshaw Indians sold another large tract of land to Louis Viviat and others (plaintiffs' predecessors).
- The purchasers under these deeds were British subjects and entered the land, becoming seized as the law required.
- On May 6, 1776, Virginia declared independence from Great Britain.
- On December 20, 1783, Virginia authorized its delegates to convey its claim to the territory north-west of the Ohio River to the United States.
- On March 1, 1784, Virginia's delegates conveyed this territory to the United States.
- On July 20, 1818, the United States sold and conveyed a portion of this land to William McIntosh (defendant).
- The land granted to McIntosh was within the tracts previously conveyed by the Piankeshaw Indians to Viviat and others.
- McIntosh entered and possessed the land under his patent.
- Thomas Johnson, one of the original grantees from the Piankeshaw Indians, died in 1819, devising his share to Joshua Johnson and Thomas J. Graham (the lessors of the plaintiff).
- Neither the original grantees from the Indians nor their successors ever obtained actual possession of the lands due to the American Revolution and subsequent disputes.

**Procedural History**
- Plaintiffs brought an action of ejectment in the District Court of Illinois.
- The District Court entered judgment for the defendant.
- Plaintiffs appealed to the Supreme Court of the United States.

**Issue**
Can a title to land derived from a purchase directly from Native American tribes be recognized in U.S. courts, when the U.S. government later granted the same land to another party?

**Holding**
Defendant wins. A title to lands under grant to private individuals made by Indian tribes cannot be recognized in the courts of the United States. Affirmed.

**Rules**
- **Discovery Doctrine.** The principle adopted by European nations was that discovery gave title to the government by whose subjects or authority it was made, against all other European governments, which title could be consummated by possession.
- **Exclusive Right of Acquisition.** The exclusion of all other Europeans gave the discovering nation the sole right to acquire soil from the natives and establish settlements.
- **Indian Right of Occupancy.** While European nations asserted ultimate dominion, they admitted the natives to be rightful occupants of the soil with a legal claim to retain possession and use it.
- **Limited Indian Sovereignty.** The rights of original inhabitants to complete sovereignty and to dispose of the soil at will were diminished by the principle that discovery gave exclusive title to the discoverers.
- **Governmental Pre-emption.** The exclusive right to extinguish the Indian title of occupancy, either by purchase or conquest, resides in the discovering sovereign (and its successors).
- **Incompatible Titles.** An absolute title to lands cannot exist at the same time in different persons or governments; the absolute title of the crown (and thus the U.S.) is subject only to the Indian right of occupancy, which the crown has the exclusive right to extinguish.
- **Rejected: Individual Purchase from Indians.** Private individuals cannot acquire a valid title to land directly from Indian tribes that can be sustained in U.S. courts against a title derived from the government.

**Reasoning**
- European nations, upon discovering the continent, established a principle among themselves: discovery gave title to the discovering government against other European governments.
    - This principle gave the discovering nation the exclusive right to acquire land from the natives.
    - While natives were recognized as rightful occupants, their power to dispose of the soil was denied.
    - The ultimate dominion was asserted by the discoverers, who could grant the soil subject to the Indian right of occupancy.
- This principle has been universally recognized and applied by all European powers claiming territory in America, including England.
    - English charters consistently granted land "not actually possessed by any Christian prince or people," asserting a right to take possession despite native occupancy.
    - The entire country was granted by the Crown while occupied by Indians, and these grants conveyed soil and dominion.
- The United States acceded to this rule upon independence.
    - The treaty ending the Revolutionary War transferred Great Britain's governmental powers and territorial rights to the U.S.
    - States, like Virginia, affirmed their exclusive right of pre-emption from Indians and annulled private purchases.
    - The U.S. has consistently exercised the exclusive right to extinguish Indian title and grant the soil, even for lands occupied by numerous tribes.
- The court cannot question the validity of the title derived from conquest and discovery.
    - The British government, whose rights passed to the U.S., asserted title to all lands occupied by Indians within its chartered limits and the exclusive right to extinguish their titles.
    - This title has been maintained by force and is the foundation of property for the mass of the community.
- The nature of the Indian tribes (fierce savages, hunters) made it impossible to govern them as a distinct people or incorporate them.
    - This necessitated a different rule than that applied to conquered civilized nations.
    - The rule that Indians are merely occupants, incapable of transferring absolute title, became indispensable to the system under which the country was settled.
- The proclamation of 1763 by the King of Great Britain further supports the defendant's title.
    - It reserved lands for Indians and forbade private purchases, reflecting the Crown's exclusive right to grant or reserve lands.
    - This power was never denied by the colonies to the Crown.
- Even if individuals could extinguish Indian title, they would only acquire that title, which is dependent on Indian laws and subject to their will.
    - The U.S. courts cannot protect such a title if the tribe chooses to annul it.
    - The tribes whose title the plaintiffs claim ceded the lands to the U.S. without reservation, implying they considered prior grants invalid.

**Dissent ([Judge])**
None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is foundational for understanding the **discovery doctrine** and its impact on Native American land rights in U.S. property law. It establishes the principle of **governmental pre-emption** over Indian lands, meaning only the sovereign (first the European colonizers, then the U.S. government) could extinguish Native American title. It contrasts sharply with a pure **first possession** theory, as it denies the full property rights of the original inhabitants based on their "savage" character and the needs of the "civilized" conquerors. It sets the stage for later cases dealing with tribal sovereignty and land claims.
2. The opinion acknowledges the "natural right" of Indians to their lands but dismisses it in favor of established legal principles based on conquest and necessity. This raises questions about the moral legitimacy of the legal framework established. What if the court had decided to apply "abstract justice" instead of the "principles which our own government has adopted"?

**Takeaway:** The U.S. legal system does not recognize private land titles acquired directly from Native American tribes, as the sovereign holds the exclusive right to extinguish aboriginal title.

---

## International News Service v. Associated Press (Supreme Court of the United States, 1918)

**Facts**
- Associated Press (AP) is a cooperative organization that gathers news globally and distributes it to its member newspapers for publication. Members pay assessments for this service and agree to use the news exclusively for their specified newspaper, not furnishing it to non-members before publication. Members also provide local news to AP.
- International News Service (INS) is a for-profit corporation that gathers and sells news to its client newspapers.
- INS systematically took news from AP's bulletin boards and early editions of AP member newspapers.
- INS then sold this news, either verbatim or rewritten, to its own clients, enabling them to compete with AP members in prompt news publication.
- AP filed suit to stop INS's practices, including bribing employees, inducing contract violations, and copying news from bulletins and early editions.
- The District Court granted a preliminary injunction against bribing and inducing contract violations but refused to enjoin the copying of news from bulletins and early editions, stating it was an issue of first impression.
- The Circuit Court of Appeals affirmed the injunction and modified it to also enjoin the bodily taking of AP's news until its commercial value had passed.

**Procedural History**
- AP sued INS in District Court.
- District Court granted a preliminary injunction in part, denying it for the copying of news from bulletins and early editions.
- Both parties appealed to the Circuit Court of Appeals.
- Circuit Court of Appeals affirmed the injunction and modified it to include the copying of news from bulletins and early editions.
- INS appealed to the Supreme Court.

**Issue**
Can a news-gathering organization be restrained from taking news from a competitor's publicly available bulletins and early newspaper editions and selling it to its own clients for profit?

**Holding**
Plaintiff (AP) wins. A news-gathering organization has a quasi-property right in the news it gathers, as against a competitor, even after initial publication, and the systematic appropriation of that news by a competitor for commercial gain constitutes unfair competition. Affirmed.

**Rules**
- **Property in News (Rejected).** News, as information about current events, is generally not subject to absolute property rights at common law or under copyright law.
- **Copyright Law.** News articles, as literary productions, can be copyrighted, but the news element itself (the information) is not copyrightable.
- **Unfair Competition.** When competitors are engaged in the business of gathering and distributing news, each party has a duty to conduct its business so as not to unnecessarily or unfairly injure that of the other.
- **Quasi-Property Right.** As between competitors in the business of gathering and distributing news, the news itself, gathered at pains and expense, is considered quasi-property.
- **Misappropriation.** The systematic appropriation of a competitor's quasi-property (news) for commercial gain, where the appropriator reaps where it has not sown, constitutes unfair competition.
- **Abandonment.** Publication of news for limited purposes (e.g., to newspaper readers) does not constitute abandonment of the news to the public for all purposes, especially not for commercial appropriation by a competitor.
- **Unclean Hands.** The practice of using a rival's news as "tips" for independent investigation and verification, if commonly practiced by both parties and not alleged to be unlawful, does not constitute unclean hands sufficient to bar relief.

**Reasoning**
- The case concerns unfair competition between two news-gathering organizations, not the public's right to news.
- News, while not subject to absolute property rights against the public after publication, is "stock in trade" for news agencies, acquired through significant enterprise, organization, skill, labor, and money.
- As between competitors, news must be regarded as "quasi-property" because both parties seek to profit from it.
- INS's actions amount to reaping where it has not sown, diverting profit from AP who incurred the expense of gathering the news.
- This conduct is an unauthorized interference with AP's legitimate business at the point where profit is to be reaped.
- The value of news depends on its prompt transmission and freshness; allowing competitors to appropriate it immediately after initial publication would undermine the business model of news gathering.
- AP's publication of news through bulletins and early editions is for the benefit of its readers, not an abandonment of its commercial value to competitors.
- The "unclean hands" defense fails because using news as "tips" for independent investigation is a recognized practice by both parties and does not equate to the bodily appropriation of news.
- The court's decision does not grant AP a monopoly on news gathering or distribution, but rather postpones a competitor's participation in the distribution of news it did not gather, to prevent unfair advantage.

**Dissent (Brandeis)**
- News, as knowledge, truths, conceptions, and ideas, generally becomes free for common use after voluntary communication to others, unless it involves creation, invention, or discovery protected by copyright or patent law.
- The knowledge sought to be protected here is not of a kind that the law has historically recognized as property.
- The "ticker" cases, relied upon by the majority, are distinguishable because they involved breaches of contract or trust regarding confidential information, which is not present here.
- Cases protecting literary property are inapplicable because news lacks the creative element required for such protection and general publication (like in newspapers) would extinguish any common-law property rights.
- Unfair competition, as traditionally understood, involves fraud, force, or other illegal means, such as "passing off" one's goods as another's. Mere appropriation of another's uncopyrighted product without compensation, even if unseemly, is not legally unfair competition.
- The means by which INS obtained the news (publicly available papers and bulletins) were lawful, and there was no misrepresentation or breach of trust.
- Courts are ill-equipped to create new property rights in news, as this involves complex policy considerations best left to the legislature, which has previously declined to grant such protection.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is foundational for the concept of **quasi-property** and **misappropriation** in intellectual property law, extending property-like protection beyond traditional categories like copyright and patent. It connects to the idea of **labor and investment** as a justification for property rights, similar to Pierson v. Post (though in a different context), but here applied to intangible information. It also contrasts with cases like Moore v. Regents of the University of California, where the court was reluctant to extend property rights to human biological material, highlighting the judiciary's role in defining the scope of property.
2. The tension between protecting investment in news gathering and promoting the free flow of information is a key theme.
