- **"Concerted Family Effort."** This concept involves mutual sacrifice, effort, and contribution of both parties as part of a larger, long-range plan intended to benefit the family as a whole, including financial contributions, increased household responsibilities, shared emotional burdens, and postponement of personal educational or career pursuits by the non-student spouse.
- **Rejected: Degree as Alimony Factor.** An advanced degree is not properly considered as a factor in awarding alimony because alimony is for support, while compensation for a degree is based on equitable recognition of contributions, and alimony can be terminated upon remarriage.
- **Rejected: Degree as "Property" in the Classic Sense.** The physical or metaphysical definition of a degree as "property" is not decisive; courts must focus on the most equitable solution for dissolving the marriage and dividing holdings.
- **Valuation of Equitable Claim for Advanced Degree.** Valuation involves a two-step analysis:
    1. Examination of the sacrifices, efforts, and contributions of the non-student spouse toward attainment of the degree.
    2. Determination of the most equitable remedy or means of compensating the non-student spouse under the facts of the case, considering:
        - The length of the marriage after the degree was obtained.
        - The sources and extent of financial support given to the degree holder during the years in school.
        - The overall division of the parties’ marital property.
- **Rejected: Present Value Method for Degree Valuation.** The present value method, which attempts to estimate future earnings attributable to the degree, is not an appropriate means to evaluate the equitable claim because it misconstrues the underlying premise that compensation is for contributions, not for an interest in the degree itself.
- **Focus of Compensation.** The focus of an award involving an advanced degree is to reimburse the non-student spouse for unrewarded sacrifices, efforts, and contributions toward attainment of the degree, not for "loss of expectations" or the degree's potential income.

**Reasoning**
- **Fairness and Equity:** Michigan law requires a fair and equitable distribution of marital assets, and equitable considerations form the basis for recognizing a claim for compensation for an advanced degree.
- **Concerted Family Effort:**
    - Kathryn postponed her education, moved with Miles, and worked full-time, providing approximately 80% of the couple's financial support while Miles attended law school.
    - Kathryn bore primary responsibility for household tasks.
    - Both parties experienced the stress of law school, with Miles blaming his behavior on stress and Kathryn trying to avoid agitating him.
    - These facts demonstrate a concerted family effort, making Miles's law degree the product of mutual sacrifice and contribution.
- **Characterization as Marital Asset/Equitable Claim:**
    - The court rejects treating the degree as a factor for alimony because alimony is for support, while this compensation is for recognized contributions. Alimony can also be terminated upon remarriage, which would unfairly jeopardize the non-student spouse's right.
    - The court rejects the idea that a degree cannot be considered because it lacks traditional property attributes (like transferability or inheritable value). The focus is on equitable distribution, not strict property definitions.
    - The non-student spouse's interest is an "equitable claim" arising from the concerted family effort, not a pecuniary interest in the degree itself.
- **Valuation:**
    - The trial court's valuation was unclear and failed to account for relevant considerations.
    - The present value method (estimating future earnings) is inappropriate because it implies a pecuniary interest in the degree, which the court rejects.
    - The proper focus is on compensating the non-student spouse for their sacrifices, efforts, and contributions.
    - Relevant factors for revaluation include: the short duration of the marriage after the degree, Kathryn's significant financial and non-pecuniary support, and the fact that she had to pursue her own education independently.
    - Miles is responsible for the student loans that financed his education.
- **Other Property Distribution:**
    - The court found no error in the distribution of automobiles, considering the source of funds and remaining debt.
    - Kathryn's $10,000 certificate of deposit was properly excluded as it was acquired before marriage and held jointly with her grandfather.
    - Miscellaneous household items were properly awarded to the party in possession.

**Dissent ([Judge])** / **Concurrence ([Judge])**
None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is important for understanding how courts may address non-traditional "assets" in divorce proceedings, particularly when one spouse's career advancement is a direct result of the other's sacrifices. It connects to the broader theme of **personhood_dignity** and **labor_or_investment** as justifications for property rights, even when the "property" doesn't fit traditional definitions. It contrasts with cases like *Moore v. Regents of the University of California* where the court was unwilling to recognize a property interest in human cells, highlighting the different contexts (divorce vs. medical research) and the equitable considerations at play.
2. This case raises the question of how to quantify "sacrifices, efforts, and contributions" in a way that is consistent and fair across different divorce cases, especially when non-pecuniary contributions are involved.

**Takeaway:** While a professional degree itself may not be traditional "property," a non-degree-earning spouse's significant contributions to its attainment during marriage can create an equitable claim for compensation upon divorce.

---

## Blackett v. Olanoff (Massachusetts Supreme Judicial Court, 1977)

**Facts**
- Landlords leased a building for residential purposes.
- Landlords leased a nearby building to others for use as a bar and cocktail lounge (lounge).
- The lounge generated loud music and disturbances into the early morning hours.
- Residential tenants complained to the landlords about the noise.
- The landlords made some attempts to remedy the problem but were unsuccessful.
- Residential tenants vacated their apartments within a reasonable time.

**Procedural History**
- Landlords sued tenants for rent in Municipal Court of the City of Boston.
- Cases were transferred to the Housing Court of the City of Boston.
- The Housing Court judge found for the tenants, ruling that the landlords breached the implied warranty of quiet enjoyment.
- Landlords appealed.
- The appeals were transferred to the Massachusetts Supreme Judicial Court.
- The Massachusetts Supreme Judicial Court affirmed the judgments.

**Issue**
Can landlords be held responsible for a breach of the implied warranty of quiet enjoyment due to noise from another tenant's commercial establishment, when the landlords had the ability to control the noise?

**Holding**
Defendant tenants win. Landlords breached the implied warranty of quiet enjoyment because they introduced a commercial activity that disturbed residential tenants and had the ability to control the objectionable conditions but failed to do so. Affirmed.

**Rules**
- **Implied Warranty of Quiet Enjoyment.** A landlord's conduct, not their intent, is controlling in determining a breach of the implied warranty of quiet enjoyment.
- **Constructive Eviction.** A tenant is constructively evicted when the landlord's actions or inactions, or what they permit to be done, result in the tenant being substantially deprived of the quiet enjoyment of their leased premises for a substantial time, and the tenant vacates within a reasonable time.
- **Landlord Control Over Nuisance.** A landlord may be held responsible for a nuisance created by one tenant that affects another if the landlord has the right and ability to control the objectionable conditions.
- Rejected: **Landlord Intent for Breach.** A landlord must perform some act with the intent of depriving the tenant of the enjoyment and occupation of the whole or part of the leased premises.

**Reasoning**
- The judge was warranted in finding that the landlords had control over the conditions.
    - The landlords introduced a commercial activity into a residential area.
    - The lounge's lease explicitly required entertainment to be conducted so it would not disturb residents.
    - The landlords complained to the lounge tenants, and the noise would temporarily abate.
- The disturbing condition was a natural and probable consequence of the landlords permitting the lounge to operate.
- The landlords had the right to control the objectionable noise from the lounge.
- This situation differs from traditional cases where a landlord is not responsible for one residential tenant annoying another.
    - Here, the landlords created the situation by leasing to the lounge.
    - The landlords had the ability to control the conditions.
- The court declines to maintain a distinction between landlord nonfeasance and malfeasance when the landlord creates the situation and has the right to control the objectionable conditions.

**Dissent ([Judge])**
None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is important for understanding the **implied warranty of quiet enjoyment** and **constructive eviction**, particularly how it applies when a landlord's actions regarding one tenant impact another. It expands the landlord's responsibility beyond direct actions to include situations where they have control over a nuisance created by another tenant. It connects to cases like *Javins v. First National Realty Corp.* by showing a judicial willingness to imply protections for tenants, though *Javins* specifically addresses the implied warranty of habitability.
2. What if the lounge's lease did not contain a clause about not disturbing residents? Would the landlords still be deemed to have control over the noise?

**Takeaway:** Landlords can be held liable for constructive eviction due to noise from another tenant if they have the power to control the noise and fail to do so, even if they did not intend to disturb the tenant.

---

## Gotlieb v. Taco Bell Corp. (United States District Court, E.D. New York, 1994)

**Facts**
- On August 15, 1991, Gotlieb and Blaymore (landlords) and Taco Bell (tenant) entered into a 20-year commercial ground lease to establish a Taco Bell restaurant.
- The lease required Taco Bell to diligently obtain necessary permits and approvals within a six-month "permitting period" ending February 15, 1992.
- If permits were not obtained, Taco Bell could cancel the lease.
- In September 1991, community groups began opposing the restaurant's construction.
- Taco Bell attempted to address community opposition but did not file its permit application until February 14, 1992.
- On February 14, 1992, Taco Bell served a written repudiation of the lease.
- On February 14, 1992, the landlords rejected Taco Bell's repudiation.
- In June 1992, the landlords initiated this action.
- District Judge Spatt granted summary judgment for the landlords, finding Taco Bell failed to meet the lease conditions, the lease remained in effect, and Taco Bell was liable.
- In October 1993, the landlords met with representatives from Rite-Aid drug stores to discuss renting the premises for their sole benefit.
- On November 3, 1993, the landlords submitted a written proposal with explicit lease terms to Rite-Aid.

**Procedural History**
- Landlords sued Taco Bell for breach of a commercial ground lease.
- District Judge Spatt granted summary judgment for the landlords on liability and referred the case to a Magistrate Judge for a trial on damages.
- The parties consented to proceed before the Magistrate Judge for all purposes.
- The Magistrate Judge held a trial on damages.

**Issue**
Can a landlord's actions to re-let a property for their own benefit, after rejecting a tenant's repudiation, constitute an acceptance of the surrender by operation of law, thereby terminating the lease and limiting the tenant's liability for future rent?

**Holding**
Plaintiff landlords win in part. The landlords' actions to re-let the property for their sole benefit constituted an acceptance of the tenant's surrender by operation of law, terminating the lease as of November 1993, and limiting the tenant's liability to accrued rent and other damages up to that date. Affirmed in part.

**Rules**
- **Landlord's Options Upon Tenant Repudiation.** When a lessee attempts to repudiate a lease prior to its expiration, a lessor may reject the repudiation (tenant remains liable), notify the tenant of re-letting for the tenant's benefit (tenant liable for deficiency), or accept the repudiation and re-let for its own benefit (lessee generally relieved of further liability).
- **No Commercial Landlord Duty to Mitigate Damages.** A commercial lessor has no obligation to mitigate damages when a tenant repudiates a lease.
- **Acceptance of Surrender by Operation of Law.** Termination of an estate by repudiation or surrender may be effected by operation of law when the parties do some act inconsistent with the landlord-tenant relationship, implying an intent to deem the lease terminated.
- **Conduct Inconsistent with Landlord-Tenant Relationship.** Conduct by a landlord that falls short of actual re-letting but indicates the landlord's intent to terminate the lease and use the premises for their own benefit can imply acceptance of surrender.
- **Outward Refusal to Accept Repudiation.** An outward refusal to accept repudiation does not bar a finding that subsequent conduct creates an acceptance by operation of law.
- **Mere Attempts to Re-let.** Mere attempts to re-let are insufficient to establish an acceptance by operation of law.
- **Effect of Accepted Surrender on Future Rent.** An acceptance of a surrender operates to discharge the tenant from all liability for rent in the future.
- **Acceleration Clauses.** In the absence of an acceleration clause in the lease, an action for future rent cannot be maintained.
- **Liquidated Damages.** To recover liquidated damages, the amount must bear a reasonable relation to the probable loss, and the actual loss must be incapable or difficult of precise estimation.
- **Prevailing Party for Attorney's Fees.** A "prevailing party" is one who succeeds on "any significant issue in litigation which achieves some of the benefit the parties sought in bringing suit."
- **Attorney's Fees in Landlord-Tenant Cases.** Lease covenants providing for attorney's fees and costs are valid if reasonable and not intended as a penalty.
- **Lodestar Method for Attorney's Fees.** New York courts use the "lodestar" method (hours reasonably expended multiplied by reasonable hourly rate) to determine attorney's fees in landlord-tenant cases.

**Reasoning**
- The landlords' actions of negotiating and making a written offer to lease the premises to Rite-Aid for their sole benefit demonstrated an intent to accept Taco Bell's surrender.
    - This conduct was inconsistent with the tenant's interest in the property.
    - Plaintiff Gotlieb's testimony confirmed his decision to deal directly with Rite-Aid, "let the chips fall where they may," because they were incurring expenses and "going absolutely nowhere."
    - This went beyond "mere attempts to re-let."
- Therefore, the lease was terminated by operation of law as of November 1993.
- Taco Bell is liable for accrued rent and additional rent up to November 1993 because the lease remained in effect until that time.
- The landlords are not entitled to future rent because the lease was terminated by their acceptance of surrender.
    - An accepted surrender discharges the tenant from future rent liability.
    - The lease also lacked an acceleration clause for future rent.
    - Awarding future rent would result in a windfall, as the landlords were negotiating a new lease at a higher rent.
- The landlords are entitled to the value of the unbuilt structure because Taco Bell failed to diligently obtain permits, and the lease provided that title to improvements would vest in the landlord upon termination.
- The landlords are "prevailing parties" for attorney's fees because they obtained summary judgment on liability and recovered significant damages, even if not all damages sought.
- The court reduced the requested attorney's fees due to duplicative and unreasonable billing entries.

**Dissent ([Judge])** / **Concurrence ([Judge])**
None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case illustrates the concept of **surrender by operation of law** in commercial leases, where a landlord's actions, even if contrary to their stated intent, can be deemed an acceptance of a tenant's repudiation. It connects to the broader theme of **leases_reforms_transfers** by showing how landlord-tenant relationships can be terminated outside of explicit agreement, impacting the tenant's ongoing liability. It also touches on the **freedom_of_contract** stick by analyzing the lease's specific clauses regarding acceleration and attorney's fees.
2. What if the landlords had simply listed the property for rent but had not entered into specific negotiations with Rite-Aid? Would that still constitute an acceptance of surrender by operation of law, or would it be considered a "mere attempt to re-let"?

**Takeaway:** A landlord's affirmative actions to re-let a property for their own benefit, even after explicitly rejecting a tenant's repudiation, can constitute an acceptance of surrender by operation of law, thereby terminating the lease and limiting the tenant's liability for future rent.

---

## Medico-Dental Building Co. v. Horton & Converse (Supreme Court of California, 1942)

**Facts**
- In 1925, Horton & Converse (defendant) began occupying space in the Medico-Dental Building as a drug store under a lease.
- On July 1, 1934, defendant entered into a new lease for the same space for 16 years and 4 months with the original lessor.
- This lease stipulated that the premises would be used "as a drug store and for no other business or purpose" and that the lessor agreed "not to lease or sublease any part or portion of the Medico-Dental Building to any other person, firm or corporation for the purpose of maintaining a drug store or selling drugs or ampoules."
- Plaintiff, Medico-Dental Building Company, became the successor in interest to the original lessor.
- On December 30, 1937, plaintiff leased the entire ninth floor of the building to Dr. Boonshaft, a physician, for three years, starting April 15, 1938.
- Dr. Boonshaft's lease stated the premises were "to be used solely as offices for the practice of medicine and dentistry" and that he would not "maintain therein or thereon, nor permit to be maintained therein or thereon, a drug store or drug dispensary, nor will lessee compound or dispense, or permit to be compounded or dispensed, drugs or ampoules except in connection with the regular course of treatment of lessee’s own patients." It also prohibited signs with "Pharmacy," "Drug Store," or "Dispensary."
- Dr. Boonshaft took possession on April 15, 1938, and operated the Boonshaft Medical Group, which provided medical treatment to registered groups and lodge members for a monthly fee, including some drugs, with additional charges for others.
- Dr. Boonshaft maintained a drug room where drugs were sold and prescriptions filled for patients of the Medical Group.
- Dr. Boonshaft obtained a pharmacy license on May 10, 1938.
- Until June 25, 1938, Dr. Boonshaft bought drugs from defendant's store in the building.
- On June 25, 1938, Dr. Boonshaft began buying drugs wholesale from defendant's wholesale department at another location due to sales tax objections.
- In May 1938, a drug store, registered as a pharmacy, was opened on the ninth floor, selling drugs and compounding prescriptions for a charge.
- On July 8, 1938, a sales tax permit was issued to Dr. Boonshaft.
- In the last week of July 1938, defendant learned of Dr. Boonshaft's drug store operation.
- On August 3, 1938, defendant notified plaintiff in writing that Dr. Boonshaft's practice breached its lease and demanded plaintiff stop the drug sales and prescription compounding.
- Between August 3 and August 31, 1938, Dr. Boonshaft's drug store continued to operate.
- Plaintiff's agents conferred about defendant's objection but did not communicate the discussions to defendant.
- On August 8, 1938, at a meeting in defendant's office, plaintiff advised defendant it would "take the matter up further and see what it could do about it and advise defendant."
- On August 19, 1938, plaintiff's attorney informed defendant that "no arrangements could be made with Dr. Boonshaft and plaintiff could not do anything with him."
- Defendant's president responded that it would vacate the premises to avoid waiving its exclusive right. Plaintiff's attorney replied, "Well, use your own judgment about that."
- Plaintiff took no further action.
- The next day, defendant closed its store, put a sign directing customers to other locations, and piled empty packing boxes in its leased premises.
- On August 24, 1938, defendant sent plaintiff a written notice of rescission.
- Defendant vacated the store on August 31, 1938.

**Procedural History**
- Plaintiff sued defendant for unpaid rent, electricity charges, and renovation costs.
- The trial court found in favor of the defendant, except for the electricity charge, concluding that plaintiff breached the restrictive covenant in the lease.
- Plaintiff appealed.

**Issue**
Can a tenant rescind a lease and stop paying rent if the landlord breaches a restrictive covenant that was a material part of the lease agreement?

**Holding**
Defendant (lessee) wins. A tenant can rescind a lease and stop paying rent if the landlord breaches a restrictive covenant that goes to the whole consideration of the lease, as such covenants are dependent. Affirmed.

**Rules**
- **Lease as Contract.** Leases have a dual character as both a conveyance and a contract, and contractual features should be interpreted according to general contract rules to give effect to the parties' intentions.
- **Dependent Covenants.** Covenants in leases are dependent if they run to the entire consideration of the contract, meaning the breach of one covenant defeats the entire object of the other party in entering the lease.
- **Independent Covenants.** Rejected: The rule that all covenants in leases are independent.
- **Material Breach.** A breach of a contractual right that is so dominant or pervasive as to frustrate the purpose of the undertaking justifies rescission.
- **Waiver.** Waiver may be shown by conduct that is inconsistent with the intent to enforce a right, inducing a reasonable belief that the right has been relinquished.

**Reasoning**
- The restrictive covenant was a dependent covenant because it went to the whole consideration of the lease.
    - The lease itself indicated the parties' intention for interdependence, particularly with the restrictive covenant and the rent payment appearing in a rider and the lessee being limited to a drug store use.
    - The exclusive right to operate a drug store was vital to defendant's business, which catered to doctors and dentists in the building.
    - Plaintiff knew the building tenants were the "chief source" of defendant's business.
    - The court rejected the plaintiff's argument that all covenants in leases are independent, distinguishing prior cases.
- Plaintiff breached the restrictive covenant by leasing to Dr. Boonshaft for the purpose of maintaining a drug store and by acquiescing in Dr. Boonshaft's conduct.
    - The trial court reasonably inferred from Dr. Boonshaft's lease provisions and subsequent conduct that plaintiff intended to give him the right to operate a drug store for his patients.
    - The provision in Dr. Boonshaft's lease prohibiting "Pharmacy," "Drug Store," or "Dispensary" signs suggested that a drug store, even if restricted, was contemplated.
    - Plaintiff's property manager admitted that Dr. Boonshaft was violating his lease.
    - The evidence showed Dr. Boonshaft was operating a drug store, with a registered pharmacist, selling drugs, and compounding prescriptions.
    - The fact that Dr. Boonshaft could legally operate a drug store as a physician with a pharmacist did not satisfy plaintiff's contractual obligation to protect defendant from competition.
    - Technical subterfuges are disregarded; the substance of the situation determines the issue.
- The breach was substantial and went to the whole of the consideration.
    - The exclusive nature of defendant's pharmacy, its limited patronage source, and its long tenancy established the materiality of the breach.
    - The lease would not have been executed without the restrictive covenant.
    - The potential loss of business from Dr. Boonshaft's drug store was a significant financial impact, not easily measurable in damages.
    - Defendant was not required to prove the exact pecuniary detriment.
- Defendant did not waive the breach.
    - Defendant promptly protested the breach and allowed plaintiff time to resolve the issue.
    - Plaintiff's final communication that it "could not do anything" with Dr. Boonshaft forced defendant to choose between waiving the breach or vacating.
    - The trial court found that defendant did not assist Dr. Boonshaft or have prior knowledge of the competitive activity.

**Dissent ([Judge])** / **Concurrence ([Judge])**
None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is important for understanding the **contractual nature of leases** and the concept of **dependent covenants**. It contrasts with the traditional common law view that lease covenants are independent, as seen in cases like *Berg v. Wiley*, where a landlord's breach of a covenant might not excuse a tenant's obligation to pay rent. Here, the court emphasizes the parties' intent and the materiality of the covenant to the entire consideration, allowing the tenant to rescind.
2. [CHECK: Is the distinction between "leasing for the prohibited purpose" and "acquiescence in the conduct" clear enough, or does the court treat them as essentially the same breach?]

**Takeaway:** Leases are not merely conveyances but also contracts, and a landlord's breach of a material, dependent covenant can justify a tenant's rescission and termination of rent obligations.

---

## Javins v. First National Realty Corp. (United States Court of Appeals, District of Columbia Circuit, 1970)

**Facts**
- Appellants rented apartments in Clifton Terrace, a three-building apartment complex in Northwest Washington, D.C., from First National Realty Corporation.
- First National Realty Corporation filed separate actions seeking possession of the apartments because the appellants had not paid rent for April.
- Appellants admitted they had not paid rent but alleged numerous violations of the Housing Regulations of the District of Columbia as a defense.
- Appellants offered to prove approximately 1500 housing code violations in the building, some directly affecting their premises and others indirectly, all arising since the term of the lease commenced.

**Procedural History**
- The Landlord and Tenant Branch of the District of Columbia Court of General Sessions ruled proof of housing code violations inadmissible as a defense to an eviction action for nonpayment of rent and entered judgment for the landlord.
- The District of Columbia Court of Appeals upheld this ruling.
- The United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit granted appellants' petitions for leave to appeal.
- The United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit reversed and remanded.

**Issue**
Can housing code violations that arise during the term of a lease be used as a defense to an eviction action for nonpayment of rent?

**Holding**
The tenants win. A warranty of habitability, measured by the standards in the Housing Regulations for the District of Columbia, is implied by operation of law into leases of urban dwelling units, and breach of this warranty gives rise to the usual remedies for breach of contract. Reversed and remanded.

**Rules**
- **Traditional Leasehold Law.** Historically, a lease was considered a conveyance of an interest in land, and courts applied real property rules, often absolving landlords of repair obligations unless explicitly covenanted.
- **Modern Contract Law.** Modern contract law recognizes implied warranties of quality in sales of goods and services, protecting buyers' legitimate expectations.
- **Implied Warranty of Habitability.** A warranty of habitability, measured by housing code standards, is implied by operation of law into leases of urban dwelling units.
- **Contractual Interdependence.** Under contract principles, a tenant's obligation to pay rent is dependent upon the landlord's performance of their obligations, including the implied warranty of habitability.
- **Waiver of Statutory Duties.** Duties imposed by housing regulations cannot be waived or shifted by agreement if the regulations specifically place the duty upon the lessor.

**Reasoning**
- The traditional view of a lease as a conveyance of land is outdated for urban dwelling units.
    - In an agrarian society, the land was paramount, and tenants could make repairs.
    - Modern urban tenants seek shelter and a "package of goods and services," not just bare possession of land.
    - Urban tenants often lack the skills, resources, or long-term interest to make repairs, especially in complex multiple dwellings.
- Modern contract principles, including implied warranties, are more appropriate for interpreting urban dwelling leases.
    - Buyers of goods and services rely on suppliers for quality, and implied warranties protect these expectations.
    - This principle has extended to sales of new homes and other non-sales transactions.
    - The landlord is a commercial businessman with greater capacity and incentive to maintain the property, and the tenant relies on the landlord's skill.
- The District of Columbia Housing Regulations require an implied warranty of habitability.
    - The Housing Regulations provide a comprehensive scheme for housing standards, maintenance, and penalties.
    - Previous court decisions (Whetzel, Kanelos, Brown) established that these regulations create privately enforceable rights and duties for landlords.
    - The regulations reflect a legislative policy judgment that landlords have a duty to maintain habitable conditions, rendering the old common law rule obsolete.
    - The law existing at the time of contract formation is deemed a part of the contract, implying the housing code into leases.
- Breach of the implied warranty of habitability affects the tenant's obligation to pay rent.
    - The tenant's obligation to pay rent is dependent on the landlord's performance of the warranty.
    - The finder of fact must determine if violations existed and what portion of rent obligation was suspended.
    - If the entire obligation is extinguished, the eviction action fails; if partial, the tenant must pay the due amount to avoid eviction.
    - The court may require tenants to pay rent into the court registry during litigation to protect both parties.

**Dissent (ROBB)** / **Concurrence (ROBB)**
Concurs in the result and in Parts IV-B and V of the opinion.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is foundational for understanding the shift from property law to contract law in landlord-tenant relationships, particularly for residential leases. It directly challenges the common law doctrine of *caveat emptor* in this context. It connects to cases like *Berg v. Wiley* by limiting a landlord's self-help remedies when a tenant has a valid defense based on habitability. It also relates to *Medio-Dental Building Co. v. Horton & Converse* in its discussion of applying contract principles to leases.
2. What if a tenant intentionally causes the housing code violations? The court states that the landlord can defend by proving the damage was caused by the tenant's wrongful action.

**Takeaway:** Residential leases are contracts that include an implied warranty of habitability, meaning landlords must maintain premises according to housing codes, and tenants can use breach of this warranty as a defense against nonpayment of rent.

---

## Mullendore Theatres Inc. v. Growth Realty Investors Co. (Washington Court of Appeals, 1984)

**Facts**
- In 1969, Conner Theatres Corporation leased part of the Jones Building and provided a $22,500 security deposit.
- The lease stated that the deposit would be applied to damages if the tenant defaulted, or returned to the tenant at the lease's expiration if there was no default.
- The lease also stated that all covenants would run with the land.
- Before 1974, the original landlord transferred the property to North Pacific World Trade Center, Ltd.
- In 1974, Conner assigned its leasehold interest to Mullendore Theatres, Inc., and the security deposit was reduced to $6,000.
- In 1975, Growth Realty acquired the property when North Pacific defaulted on a note and deed of trust.
- Growth later sold the property to the City of Tacoma.
- The City was concerned about the potential $6,000 liability for the security deposit and wanted to reduce the purchase price by that amount.
- Growth agreed to indemnify the City for any liability related to the security deposit.
- In 1980, Mullendore negotiated a new lease with the City and released any claims against the City for the security deposit, but reserved claims against others.
- Mullendore then sued Growth for the return of the security deposit.

**Procedural History**
- The trial court held that the covenant to refund the security deposit ran with the land and bound Growth through the indemnification clause.
- Growth appealed.

**Issue**
- Does a landlord's covenant to refund a tenant's security deposit run with the land, obligating a successor landlord to refund it, when the lease permits but does not require the deposit to be used for the benefit of the leased property?

**Holding**
- Defendant (Growth Realty Investors Co.) wins. A landlord's covenant to refund a security deposit does not run with the land if the lease does not restrict the use of the deposit for the benefit of the property. Reversed.

**Rules**
- **Covenant Running with the Land.** A lease covenant runs with the land only if it "touches or concerns" the land.
- **Touch and Concern Test.** To touch or concern the land, a covenant must be so related to the land as to enhance its value and confer a benefit upon it.
- **Collateral and Personal Obligation.** If a covenant does not touch or concern the land, it is a collateral and personal obligation of the original lessor.
- **Promise to Pay Money.** For a promise to pay money to be a running covenant, it must restrict the use of the funds to the benefit of the property.
- **Intent to Run.** Intent alone is not sufficient to make a covenant run with the land if its nature is personal.

**Reasoning**
- The covenant to refund the security deposit did not "touch or concern" the land.
    - The landlord was not required to spend the deposit for repairs, maintenance, or any other purpose related to the property.
    - The landlord was not required to transfer the deposit to successors.
    - The covenant was not directly related to the property itself.
- Because the covenant did not touch and concern the land, it could not run with the land, despite the lease's language stating that all covenants would run.
    - Intent is not enough to transform a personal covenant into a running covenant.
- The City had no obligation to return the security deposit.
    - Growth's agreement to indemnify the City did not create a new liability for Growth; it only protected the City from potential liability.

**Dissent ([Judge])**
- None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case illustrates the "touch and concern" test for covenants running with the land, a concept also relevant to easements and equitable servitudes. It emphasizes that a covenant must directly affect the physical use or value of the land, not just be a personal financial obligation, to bind successors. This contrasts with cases where covenants for maintenance assessments (like in *Rodruck*) are found to run because they directly benefit the property.
2. [CHECK: Is the indemnification agreement between Growth and the City relevant to the "touch and concern" analysis, or only to Growth's potential liability if the covenant *had* run?]

**Takeaway:** A covenant to refund a security deposit is generally considered a personal obligation and does not run with the land unless its terms specifically tie the deposit's use to the benefit or improvement of the property.

---

## Jaber v. Miller (Arkansas Supreme Court, 1951)

**Facts**
- In 1945, Jaber leased a business building from its owner for a five-year term, from March 1, 1946, to March 1, 1951, with a monthly rent of $200. The lease provided for termination if the premises were destroyed by fire.
- Jaber operated a rug shop in the building until 1949.
- In 1949, Jaber sold his merchandise and transferred his lease to Norber & Son via an instrument titled "Contract and Assignment." This instrument stated Jaber "hereby transfers and assigns" the lease for the remainder of the term. Norber & Son paid Jaber $700 cash and executed five promissory notes for $700 each. Norber & Son also agreed to pay the owner $200 monthly rent, and Jaber reserved a right to retake possession if Norber & Son failed to pay rent or the notes. The instrument did not address destruction by fire.
- Later, Miller obtained a transfer of the lease from Norber & Son.
- Miller, unable to pay the $700 notes, arranged with Jaber to divide the payments into monthly installments of $175. Miller and the Norbers executed fourteen new promissory notes for $175 each, which Jaber accepted in place of the unpaid original notes.
- On December 3, 1949, the building was destroyed by fire.
- Miller then contended that Jaber's transfer to Norber & Son was a sublease, and thus the notes represented rent, which terminated with the destruction of the building.

**Procedural History**
- Miller sued Jaber to cancel the fourteen promissory notes.
- The chancellor concluded the notes were rental payments and should be canceled.
- Jaber appealed.

**Issue**
- Should the court determine whether a transfer of a leasehold is an assignment or a sublease based on the parties' intent, or based on whether the entire term was transferred?

**Holding**
- Appellant (Jaber) wins. The transfer from Jaber to Norber & Son was an assignment, and the notes were deferred payments for the assignment, not rent. Reversed.

**Rules**
- **Common Law Assignment/Sublease Distinction.** If a lessee transfers their estate for the entire remainder of the term, it is an assignment, regardless of form or intent. If the lessee transfers their estate for less than the entire term, even for a day less, it is a sublease, regardless of form or intent.
- **Rejected: Common Law Assignment/Sublease Distinction.** The court rejects the common law rule that distinguishes between an assignment and a sublease based solely on whether the entire term is transferred.
- **Modern Assignment/Sublease Distinction.** The intention of the parties governs in determining whether an instrument is an assignment or a sublease.
- **Right of Re-entry (Massachusetts Rule).** *(Rejected by this court)* If a sublessor reserves a right of re-entry for nonpayment of rent, this is a sufficient reversionary estate to make the instrument a sublease.

**Reasoning**
- The common law distinction between assignment and sublease is arbitrary and based on feudal property law, where each person held land by tenure and owed duties to an overlord.
    - If a tenant transferred their entire term, they parted with their interest, and the transferee held directly from the original lessor.
    - The intention of the parties was irrelevant; the sole question was whether the first lessee retained a reversion.
- The common law rule can lead to injustice for both the subtenant (double liability for rent) and the sublessor (merger preventing collection of increased rent).
- A majority of American courts have adopted the English doctrine, but some have tried to soften its harshness by:
    - Following the parties' intention in disputes between sublessor and sublessee.
    - Finding a "reversion" in what the parties intended as a sublease, such as a reserved right of re-entry (the Massachusetts rule).
- The court is not compelled to adhere to an unjust rule that was logical only in feudalism.
    - The rule is a "trap" for laymen and less skilled lawyers, leading to hardship and injustice by frustrating the parties' intent.
    - It is "revolting" to maintain a rule when its original grounds have vanished.
- The court adopts the rule that the intention of the parties governs.
    - The duration of the primary term compared to the sublease length may be a factor, but not the sole consideration.
- In this case, the parties clearly intended an assignment.
    - The document is titled "Contract and Assignment."
    - All language is that of an assignment.
    - The consideration was for the sale of the lease, not rent.
    - The deferred payments were evidenced by promissory notes, not typical for a lease.
- The assignment did not provide for fire contingency, which is unfortunate for Miller, but the court will not supply a provision not demanded by the assignees.

**Dissent ([Judge])** / **Concurrence ([Judge])**
- None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is important because it rejects a long-standing common law rule based on feudal concepts in favor of a more modern approach centered on the parties' intent. It connects to the theme of how property law evolves to meet contemporary needs, similar to how courts might interpret other ancient doctrines. It contrasts with cases that strictly adhere to formalistic rules, like those sometimes seen in **Johnson v. M'Intosh** regarding discovery.
2. What if the "assignment" had reserved a right of re-entry for the last day of the term? Would the court still prioritize intent, or would the formalistic common law rule creep back in?

**Takeaway:** Courts may abandon outdated common law property rules in favor of principles that reflect the parties' intent and modern commercial realities.

---

## Nahrstedt v. Lakeside Village Condominium Association (Supreme Court of California, 1994)

**Facts**
- In April 1978, the developer of Lakeside Village, a 530-unit condominium complex, recorded a declaration of covenants, conditions, and restrictions (CC&Rs) that included a pet restriction stating, "No animals (which shall mean dogs and cats), livestock, reptiles or poultry shall be kept in any unit."
- In January 1988, Natore Nahrstedt purchased a condominium unit in Lakeside Village and moved in with her three cats.
- The Lakeside Village Condominium Association (Association) learned of Nahrstedt's cats and demanded their removal, assessing fines against her for continued violation of the pet restriction.
- Nahrstedt alleged that she kept her cats indoors, they were "noiseless," and "created no nuisance." She also alleged she did not know of the pet restriction when she bought her condominium.

**Procedural History**
- Nahrstedt sued the Association, its officers, and employees, seeking to invalidate the assessments, enjoin future assessments, and declare the pet restriction "unreasonable" as applied to indoor cats. She also sought damages for invasion of privacy and emotional distress.
- The trial court sustained the Association's demurrer to the complaint and dismissed Nahrstedt's complaint.
- The Court of Appeal reversed the trial court's judgment, concluding that Nahrstedt's complaint stated a claim for declaratory relief because the reasonableness of a use restriction depended on the facts of a particular homeowner's case.
- The Association petitioned for review to the Supreme Court of California.

**Issue**
Can a condominium owner prevent enforcement of a pet restriction contained in the recorded declaration of a common interest development by showing it is unreasonable as applied to their specific circumstances?

**Holding**
The Association wins. A pet restriction contained in a recorded declaration of a common interest development is enforceable unless it is arbitrary, violates a fundamental public policy, or imposes burdens on the affected land that substantially outweigh the restriction's benefits to the development as a whole. Reversed.

**Rules**
- **Common Interest Development Act (California Civil Code § 1354(a)).** Covenants and restrictions in the recorded declaration of a common interest development are enforceable equitable servitudes, unless unreasonable.
- **Equitable Servitudes.** Courts will enforce a promise about the use of land even without privity of contract, especially for uniform building restrictions under a general plan.
- **Presumption of Validity.** Use restrictions contained in a recorded declaration of a common interest development are presumed reasonable.
- **Test for Unreasonableness.** A recorded use restriction is "unreasonable" and thus unenforceable if it:
    - Is arbitrary (bears no rational relationship to the protection, preservation, operation, or purpose of the affected land).
    - Violates a fundamental public policy.
    - Imposes burdens on the use of affected land that are so disproportionate to the restriction's beneficial effects that it should not be enforced.
- **Focus of Unreasonableness Test.** The reasonableness or unreasonableness of a condominium use restriction subject to § 1354 is determined by reference to the common interest development as a whole, not by reference to facts specific to the objecting homeowner.
- **Rejected: Case-by-case reasonableness.** The reasonableness of a recorded use restriction does not depend on the conduct of a particular condominium owner.

**Reasoning**
- The Legislature, in Civil Code section 1354, intended for recorded use restrictions in common interest developments to be treated as equitable servitudes and afforded a presumption of validity.
    - The change in statutory language from "where reasonable" to "unless unreasonable" shifts the burden of proof to the challenger to demonstrate unreasonableness.
- The stability and predictability provided by recorded use restrictions are crucial to the success of condominiums and other common interest residential developments.
    - This encourages the development of shared ownership housing and protects buyers who rely on a particular restrictive scheme.
    - It also provides assurance to prospective purchasers and protects owners from unanticipated increases in association fees to defend legal challenges.
- Enforcing restrictions uniformly, rather than on a case-by-case basis, promotes harmony within the development and avoids costly and divisive litigation.
    - Homeowners associations are ill-equipped to make fact-intensive determinations about individual animals or owners.
- The pet restriction is not arbitrary because it is rationally related to legitimate health, sanitation, and noise concerns in a high-density condominium project.
    - Nahrstedt's complaint focused on her individual circumstances (cats kept indoors, noiseless, no nuisance) rather than the effect on the development as a whole, which is legally insufficient to overcome the presumption of validity.
- There is no fundamental public policy, constitutional right, or statute in California that guarantees condominium owners the right to keep household pets.
    - The state constitutional right to privacy does not encompass an unbridled right to personal freedom, and no sources of positive law support a fundamental public policy favoring pets in condominiums.
    - *(dicta)* Exceptions exist for disabled individuals needing service animals and elderly persons in publicly funded housing, but these are not applicable here.

**Dissent (Arabian)**
- The pet restriction is patently arbitrary and unreasonable because the substantial benefits of pet ownership outweigh the meager utility of the restriction when pets are maintained without creating a nuisance or disturbing others.
- The majority's analysis gives scant acknowledgment to the historical and cultural association between humans and pets, and the value pets impart.
- When pets are kept out of sight, make no noise, generate no odors, and do not create a nuisance, there is no corresponding benefit to the community from prohibiting them.
- The inconsistency of allowing fish and birds (which can also create disturbances or health concerns) while banning other pets like cats highlights the unreasonableness and discriminatory impact of the restriction.
- The majority's approach encourages disregard for the privacy interests of law-abiding property owners and sacrifices individual freedom to the "tyranny of the commonality."

**NOTES:**
1. This case is important for understanding the enforceability of covenants, conditions, and restrictions (CC&Rs) in common interest developments, particularly how courts interpret "unreasonable" in this context. It contrasts with cases like *Shelley v. Kraemer* by upholding a restriction, but both cases deal with the limits of enforcing private agreements when they conflict with public policy or individual rights. It also touches on the tension between individual property rights and the collective good in a shared living environment, a theme seen in nuisance cases like *Jost v. Dairyland Power Cooperative*.
2. What if the CC&R explicitly allowed for an exception process for pets that could be demonstrated not to cause a nuisance? Would that change the court's analysis regarding the burden on the Association or the "unreasonableness" of the restriction?

**Takeaway:** Recorded use restrictions in common interest developments are strongly presumed valid and will be enforced unless they are arbitrary, violate fundamental public policy, or impose burdens that substantially outweigh benefits to the development as a whole, with the focus on the development, not individual circumstances.

---

## Kiekel v. Four Colonies Homes Association (Kansas Court of Appeals, 2007)

**Facts**
- In 1971, the Four Colonies subdivision was created as a planned unit development, subject to a Declaration of Covenants, Conditions, and Restrictions (Declaration) and Bylaws.
- The Declaration could be amended by 75% of lot owners, while the Bylaws could be amended by a majority vote of owners present at a meeting.
- The Declaration included various restrictive covenants but did not expressly prohibit or permit renting. It did refer to "lessees and tenants."
- Since 1971, lot owners in Four Colonies have rented their properties, with 100-115 lots rented as of 2005.
- In 1997, Four Colonies proposed a bylaw amendment to restrict renting but withdrew it, determining it conflicted with the Declaration.
- James A. Kiekel and Margaret G. Kiekel (Kiekels) owned eight rental properties in the subdivision since 1988.
- Four Colonies received complaints about tenants' conduct and property maintenance, including from the Kiekels' tenants.
- In October 2004, Four Colonies proposed a new bylaw amendment to impose various rental restrictions, including prohibitions on future rentals, approval of lease agreements, and authority for the Association to terminate leases and evict tenants.
- At a special meeting, 51.34% of the 372 attending lot owners voted to approve the 2004 bylaw amendment.
- Four Colonies sent the Kiekels a letter requesting tenant information based on the new bylaw.
- The Kiekels filed a petition for declaratory judgment, arguing the 2004 bylaw amendment was void.
- Four Colonies filed a counterclaim for injunctive relief, seeking to prevent the Kiekels from renting their properties, alleging violations of the Declaration's commercial use and noxious activity restrictions.

**Procedural History**
- The district court denied the Kiekels' petition for declaratory judgment, finding the bylaw enforceable and not in conflict with the Declaration.
- The district court denied Four Colonies' counterclaim for injunctive relief.
- The Kiekels appealed the denial of their petition for declaratory judgment.
- Four Colonies cross-appealed the denial of its request for injunctive relief.

**Issue**
Can a homeowners association impose new property use restrictions, such as rental limitations, through a bylaw amendment when its Declaration of Covenants, Conditions, and Restrictions, which defines fundamental property rights, requires a super-majority vote for amendment and does not explicitly grant such authority to bylaws?

**Holding**
The Kiekels (appellants) win. A homeowners association cannot impose new property use restrictions through a bylaw amendment when the Declaration, which governs fundamental property rights, requires a super-majority for amendment and does not delegate such authority to the bylaws. Reversed in part and Affirmed in part.

**Rules**
- **Interpretation of Restrictive Covenants.** Covenants and agreements restricting the free use of property are strictly construed against limitations upon such use, and doubts will be resolved in favor of the unrestricted use of property.
- **Declaration vs. Bylaws.** A declaration is the enabling document or "constitution" of a community association, containing the plan of development and essentials of ownership, while bylaws set forth procedures for internal government and operation.
- **Amendment Authority.** Fundamental provisions dealing with ownership and property rights are typically in the declaration, and any attempt to restrict property use as authorized in the declaration is void and unenforceable unless the declaration is properly amended.
- **Rejected: Bylaws can expand, interpret, or define Declaration.** The court rejected the argument that a bylaw provision stating bylaws "expand, interpret or specifically define certain portions of the Articles of Incorporation... and the Declaration" granted authority to impose new property use restrictions.

**Reasoning**
- The Declaration is the "enabling document" or "constitution" of the association, defining fundamental ownership rights.
- The Bylaws typically contain governance and operational provisions, functioning like corporate bylaws.
- The Declaration's amendment procedure requires a 75% super-majority vote, while the Bylaws require only a simple majority of those present, indicating that amending the Declaration is intended to be more difficult due to its impact on property rights.
- The Declaration did not expressly prohibit or permit renting, but it did refer to "lessees and tenants," implying that renting was contemplated.
- The 2004 bylaw amendment imposed extensive property use restrictions concerning renting, effectively eliminating the right to rent for many owners.
- Such extensive restrictions on property use can only be accomplished through an amendment to the Declaration, not the Bylaws.
- The Declaration did not delegate authority to the Board of Directors to impose property use restrictions through bylaws, even though it allowed delegation of common area enjoyment rights to tenants "in accordance with the Bylaws." This specific delegation did not extend to general rental restrictions.
- The Kiekels had notice that their property rights could be restricted by an amendment to the Declaration, but not by a bylaw amendment.
- Four Colonies' prior conduct (withdrawing a similar 1997 bylaw amendment because it conflicted with the Declaration) supports the conclusion that such restrictions require a Declaration amendment.
- Regarding injunctive relief, the Kiekels' renting did not violate the Declaration's commercial use restriction because earning rental income is not the same as conducting a commercial business on the property, as long as lessees use it as a residence.
- The district court did not abuse its discretion in denying injunctive relief based on the noxious activity restriction, as Four Colonies failed to prove the Kiekels' tenants were uniquely problematic or that the Kiekels' maintenance issues were worse than others.
- Four Colonies had an adequate legal remedy for maintenance issues through its right to restoration and the ability to sue for unpaid assessments.

**Dissent ([Judge])** / **Concurrence ([Judge])**
None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case highlights the distinction between a Declaration of Covenants, Conditions, and Restrictions and Bylaws in a homeowners association context. It reinforces the principle that fundamental property rights, especially those restricting use, are primarily governed by the Declaration and require strict adherence to its amendment procedures. This connects to cases like Nahrstedt v. Lakeside Village Condominium Association, which also deals with the enforceability and interpretation of restrictive covenants in common interest communities, emphasizing the importance of the founding documents.
2. [CHECK: Is there a tension between the court's sympathy for the HOA's desire to maintain the neighborhood and its strict application of the rules regarding amendments? Could the court have found a way to balance these interests, or is the strict construction of covenants paramount?]

**Takeaway:** Homeowners associations must follow the proper amendment procedures for their governing documents, particularly the Declaration, when imposing new restrictions on fundamental property rights like the right to rent.

---

## In re Estate of Rothko (New York Court of Appeals, 1977)

**Facts**
- Mark Rothko, a famous abstract expressionist painter, died testate on February 25, 1970, leaving 798 paintings.
- Rothko's will was admitted to probate on April 27, 1970, and Bernard J. Reis, Theodores Stamos, and Morton Levine were appointed executors.
- Within three weeks, on May 21, 1970, the executors entered into two contracts:
    - A contract to sell 100 paintings to Marlborough AG (MAG), a Liechtenstein corporation, for $1.8 million, with $200,000 down and the balance in 12 interest-free installments over 12 years.
    - A contract to consign approximately 700 paintings to Marlborough Gallery, Inc. (MNY), a domestic corporation, with MNY receiving a 50% commission on sales (40% for sales to other dealers).
- Reis was a director, secretary, and treasurer of MNY.
- Stamos was an artist under contract with Marlborough.
- Kate Rothko, decedent's daughter, petitioned to remove the executors, enjoin MNY and MAG from disposing of paintings, rescind the agreements, return the paintings, and seek damages.
- The Attorney-General, representing the ultimate charitable beneficiaries of the Mark Rothko Foundation, joined in seeking similar relief.
- On June 26, 1972, a temporary restraining order was issued, and on September 26, 1972, a preliminary injunction was issued, preventing MAG, MNY, and the executors from selling or disposing of the paintings without court permission.
- MNY, MAG, and Lloyd (who controlled both Marlborough corporations) sold 57 paintings in violation of the court orders.

**Procedural History**
- The Surrogate's Court found the executors breached their duties, removed them, rescinded the contracts, found MNY, MAG, and Lloyd in contempt, and awarded damages, including "appreciation damages" against Reis, Stamos, MNY, and MAG.
- The Appellate Division modified the Surrogate's order by deleting an option for the new fiduciary to specify which paintings to accept, and otherwise affirmed.
- Reis, Stamos, Levine, MNY, MAG, and Lloyd appealed to the New York Court of Appeals.

**Issue**
Can executors who breach their duty of loyalty by selling estate property to entities with which they have conflicts of interest be held liable for the appreciated value of the property at the time of the court's decree, rather than its value at the time of sale?

**Holding**
The petitioners win. Executors who breach their duty of loyalty by engaging in self-interested transactions are liable for appreciation damages, representing the value of the property at the time of the decree. Affirmed.

**Rules**
- **Duty of Loyalty.** A fiduciary must act with undivided loyalty to the beneficiaries and avoid conflicts of interest.
- **No Further Inquiry Rule.** When a fiduciary engages in self-dealing or has a conflict of interest, the transaction is voidable, and the court will not inquire into its fairness.
- **Executor's Duty of Prudence.** An executor must employ such diligence and prudence in managing estate assets as prudent persons of discretion and intelligence would.
- **Liability of Co-Executor.** An executor who knows a co-executor is committing breaches of trust and fails to prevent them is legally accountable.
- **Liability of Third Parties.** A third party who takes property with notice of a fiduciary's breach of trust is liable for the breach.
- **Appreciation Damages.** Where a breach of trust involves a serious conflict of interest or a duty to retain property, damages may be measured by the value of the property at the time of the decree (appreciation damages), rather than merely the difference between the sale price and the fair market value at the time of sale.
- **Contempt of Court.** Disposing of property in violation of a court order constitutes contempt, regardless of how sales are interpreted under the Uniform Commercial Code.

**Reasoning**
- The Surrogate and Appellate Division properly found that Reis and Stamos had serious conflicts of interest.
    - Reis was a director and officer of MNY and benefited financially and in status from Marlborough.
    - Stamos was an artist under contract with Marlborough and received personal benefits.
    - These conflicts violated the duty of loyalty, which prevents a fiduciary from accepting employment or benefits from a third party transacting with the trust.
- The contracts were neither fair nor in the best interests of the estate, as demonstrated by the inadequate commissions and sale prices.
- Levine, though not acting in self-interest, failed to exercise ordinary prudence by ignoring the obvious conflicts of his co-executors and the potential loss to the estate. He cannot shelter behind advice of counsel when his duty was to act prudently in a business capacity.
- MNY and MAG are chargeable with notice of the executors' breach of duty.
- Appreciation damages are appropriate because the breach involved more than merely selling for too low a price; it involved serious conflicts of interest and inherently wrongful transfers.
    - The policy reason for limiting damages to date-of-sale value (to encourage trustees to sell when authorized) does not apply when the trustee violates an integral condition of the trust through disloyalty.
    - The goal is to make the injured party whole, placing the estate in the position it would have been in had the breach not occurred.
    - *Menzel v. List* supports this measure of damages, as both cases aim to make the injured party whole when property cannot be returned.
- The Surrogate's calculation of appreciation damages was reasonable, given the difficulty in precise valuation due to the wrongdoers' conduct.
- The 1969 agreements between Rothko and Marlborough were abandoned by the parties' conduct, including the creation of the new 1970 agreements.
- MNY, MAG, and Lloyd violated the court's restraining order and injunction by disposing of paintings, regardless of their interpretation of sales under the UCC or art market custom.

**Dissent ([Judge])**
None. (Justices Capozzoli and Nunez dissented in part at the Appellate Division regarding the measure of damages, arguing for value as of May 1970.)

**NOTES:**
1. This case is a foundational example of the **duty of loyalty** owed by fiduciaries (here, executors) and the strict consequences of breaching that duty. It connects to the broader concept of **owner sovereignty** by showing how the law protects the integrity of property management, even after death, against those entrusted with its care. The court's strong stance against self-dealing echoes principles seen in cases like *Meinhard v. Salmon* (though not a property case, it establishes a high standard for fiduciaries). The discussion of appreciation damages is particularly important, as it goes beyond simple compensatory damages to deter disloyal conduct and fully restore the estate.
2. What if the paintings had depreciated significantly after the wrongful sale? Would the executors still be liable for the original sale price, or would the estate bear the loss? This raises questions about the limits of "making the estate whole" when market conditions change adversely.

**Takeaway:** Fiduciaries owe an undivided duty of loyalty, and breaches involving self-interest will result in severe penalties, including liability for the appreciated value of wrongfully transferred property.

---

## 40 West 67th Street v. Pullman (New York Court of Appeals, 2003)

**Facts**
- In 1998, David Pullman (defendant) bought into the 40 West 67th Street cooperative and acquired shares for apartment 7B.
- Pullman began complaining about his elderly upstairs neighbors, accusing them of noise and running an illegal business.
- The cooperative's Board investigated Pullman's claims and found them to be false.
- Hostilities escalated, including a physical altercation between Pullman and the upstairs neighbor.
- Pullman distributed flyers to residents, calling the neighbor a "psychopath" and making other inflammatory accusations.
- Pullman made unauthorized alterations to his apartment and had weekend construction work done in violation of house rules.
- Pullman commenced four lawsuits against the upstairs couple, the cooperative president, and management, and attempted to start three more.
- The cooperative called a special meeting to determine if Pullman's conduct was "objectionable" under the lease agreement.
- Shareholders, by a vote of 2,048 to 0, passed a resolution declaring Pullman's conduct "objectionable" and directed the Board to terminate his proprietary lease and cancel his shares.
- The Board sent Pullman a notice of termination, requiring him to vacate his apartment.
- Pullman ignored the notice and remained in the apartment.

**Procedural History**
- The cooperative sued Pullman for possession and ejectment, cancellation of his stock, and a money judgment.
- Supreme Court denied the cooperative's motion for summary judgment, holding that the cooperative must prove objectionable conduct by competent evidence to the court's satisfaction, and declined to apply the business judgment rule.
- The Appellate Division, in a divided decision, granted the cooperative summary judgment, applying the business judgment rule.
- Pullman appealed to the New York Court of Appeals.

**Issue**
Can a residential cooperative terminate a shareholder-tenant's lease for objectionable conduct based on a shareholder vote, reviewed under the business judgment rule, or must a court independently evaluate the reasonableness of the cooperative's action?

**Holding**
The cooperative (plaintiff) wins. The business judgment rule governs a cooperative's decision to terminate a tenancy in accordance with the terms of the parties' agreement. Affirmed.

**Rules**
- **Business Judgment Rule (for cooperatives).** A court should defer to a cooperative board's determination so long as the board acts for the purposes of the cooperative, within the scope of its authority, and in good faith.
- **RPAPL 711 (1).** A proceeding to recover possession of real property based on a lease termination for objectionable conduct requires the landlord to establish by competent evidence to the satisfaction of the court that the tenant is objectionable.
- **Rejected: Independent Judicial Review.** A court is not required to make its own independent evaluation of the reasonableness of a cooperative's action to terminate a tenancy for objectionable conduct.

**Reasoning**
- The **business judgment rule** best balances the individual and collective interests in a residential cooperative setting, protecting the interests of the entire community.
- The rule protects cooperative decisions against "undue court involvement and judicial second-guessing."
- The business judgment rule can be applied consistently with **RPAPL 711 (1)**; the competent evidence required by the statute is satisfied by the application of the business judgment rule to the shareholder vote and findings.
- The cooperative acted within the **scope of its authority** by following the procedures in the lease agreement for termination, which Pullman voluntarily entered into.
- The cooperative's action legitimately furthered the **corporate purpose** by terminating a tenancy after shareholders found Pullman's behavior intolerable, as the Board has a fiduciary duty to further the collective interests.
- Pullman failed to show any **bad faith, arbitrariness, favoritism, discrimination, or malice** on the cooperative's part.
- While the business judgment rule is deferential, courts must exercise "heightened vigilance" in examining tenancy terminations to ensure the board's action meets the Levandusky test and is not a "rubber stamp" for abuse.
- Cooperative living entails voluntary, shared control, and shareholder-tenants agree to submit to the authority of the board, which "may significantly restrict the bundle of rights a property owner normally enjoys."

**Dissent ([Judge])** / **Concurrence ([Judge])**
None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case builds on *Levandusky v. One Fifth Avenue Apartment Corp.* by extending the business judgment rule to tenancy terminations in cooperatives, which is a significant limitation on a tenant's property rights. It highlights the unique nature of cooperative ownership compared to traditional landlord-tenant relationships.
2. [CHECK: Is there a tension between the "heightened vigilance" language and the overall deferential standard?]

**Takeaway:** The business judgment rule generally protects decisions made by cooperative boards, including tenancy terminations, as long as they act within their authority, in good faith, and for a legitimate corporate purpose, though courts will apply heightened scrutiny to terminations.

---

## Levandusky v. One Fifth Avenue Apartment Corp. (New York Court of Appeals, 1990)

**Facts**
- In 1987, Ronald Levandusky (respondent) decided to enlarge his kitchen and "jog" a steam riser in his apartment at One Fifth Avenue.
- Levandusky, then president of the cooperative's board, claimed the cooperative's architect, Elliot Glass, orally approved the alteration. Glass denied specific approval.
- Levandusky's proprietary lease required prior written consent for alterations to water, gas, or steam risers.
- Levandusky's architect prepared plans, approved by Glass, and submitted them to the board. The plans did not show a change to the steam riser.
- The board approved Levandusky's plans on March 14, 1988.
- On March 15, 1988, Levandusky executed an "Alteration Agreement" incorporating "Renovation Guidelines" he largely drafted, which required advance written approval for changes to the heating system.
- In late spring 1988, the building's managing agent learned Levandusky intended to move the steam riser and informed the board.
- Both Levandusky and the board consulted engineer John Flynn, who advised that relocating risers was technically feasible but risky and should be avoided.
- At a June 13, 1988 meeting, which Levandusky attended, the board resolved to "reaffirm the policy — no relocation of risers."
- At a June 23, 1988 meeting, the board denied Levandusky a variance to move his riser and conditioned approval of his renovation plans on an acceptable redesign.
- Levandusky hired a contractor who severed and jogged the kitchen steam riser.
- In August 1988, the board issued a "stop work" order.
- Levandusky installed air-conditioning units that allegedly did not conform to Landmarks Preservation Commission requirements.

**Procedural History**
- Levandusky commenced an Article 78 proceeding to set aside the stop work order.
- The cooperative cross-petitioned to compel Levandusky to restore the riser and remove the air conditioners.
- Supreme Court initially granted Levandusky's petition, annulled the stop work order, and dismissed the counterclaims, finding the board's decision arbitrary and capricious.
- On reargument, Supreme Court withdrew its decision, dismissed Levandusky's petition, and ordered him to restore the riser, applying the **business judgment rule**. It adhered to its ruling on the air conditioners.
- The Appellate Division modified the judgment, affirming the dismissal of the air conditioner claim but reversing on the stop work order, agreeing with Supreme Court's initial decision that the board's action was arbitrary and capricious. Two justices dissented, arguing the board's action was within its business judgment.
- The cooperative appealed to the Court of Appeals.

**Issue**
What standard of review should courts apply when a cooperative corporation's board of directors seeks to enforce building policy against a tenant-shareholder?

**Holding**
The appellant (One Fifth Avenue Apartment Corp.) wins. The **business judgment rule** furnishes the correct standard of review for decisions made by cooperative boards. The order of the Appellate Division is modified.

**Rules**
- **Business Judgment Rule.** Courts will not inquire into actions of corporate directors taken in good faith and in the exercise of honest judgment in the lawful and legitimate furtherance of corporate purposes, so long as they have not breached their fiduciary obligation.
- **Cooperative Board Authority.** A cooperative board's decision will be upheld if it acts for the purposes of the cooperative, within the scope of its authority, and in good faith.
- Rejected: **Reasonableness Standard.** This standard requires the board to demonstrate its decision was reasonable and permits the court to evaluate the merits or wisdom of the board's decision.

**Reasoning**
- Cooperative and condominium associations are "quasi-governments" where owners consent to be governed by a board.
- Boards have broad powers, including approving structural alterations, which can restrict property rights.
- However, agreement to submit to board authority is voluntary, and community control offers economic and social benefits.
- The standard of review must balance protecting individual residents from abuse with not undermining the community's collective interests.
- The **business judgment rule** best balances these interests because:
    - A cooperative corporation is a corporation, subject to the Business Corporation Law.
    - Courts are ill-equipped to evaluate business judgments; directors have unique capabilities and experience.
    - Board members possess experience with their building's peculiar needs not shared by courts.
    - Allowing full judicial review of every board decision threatens the stability of the common living arrangement and hampers the board's effectiveness.
    - The rule protects board decisions from indiscriminate attack while permitting review of improper decisions (e.g., no legitimate relationship to welfare, singles out individuals, taken without notice, beyond scope of authority).
- Levandusky failed to show the board breached its duty.
    - He did not comply with the Alteration Agreement or Renovation Guidelines requiring explicit written notice for heating system changes.
    - The board promptly consulted its engineer and notified Levandusky of its policy against moving pipes.
    - His claims of personal animosity and disparate treatment are conclusory and unrefuted by evidence from the board.
    - The board's decision to adhere to a uniform policy regarding the piping system, based on the engineer's advice, falls within the rule.

**Concurrence (Titone)**
- Agrees with the majority's result but disagrees with applying the **business judgment rule**.
- Argues that the shareholder's challenge was an Article 78 proceeding, which should be governed by CPLR 7803's "arbitrary and capricious" standard.
- The **business judgment rule** is suited for challenges to management's business decisions (e.g., investment choices, waste), focusing on honesty and integrity, not for administration of bylaws governing shareholder rights.
- Courts are well-suited to review the rationality or arbitrariness of board decisions regarding renovations, unlike complex business judgments.
- The "arbitrary and capricious" standard, properly applied, does not inquire into the "reasonableness" or wisdom of the decision but rather whether a rational basis exists.
- Focusing on board members' ulterior personal motives (as the majority's application of the business judgment rule might allow) is impractical and undesirable, leading to more litigation and judicial entanglement in petty grievances.
- The board's concern about the risk of unforeseen problems with the old pipe system, articulated by the engineer, provides a rational basis for its decision, which would satisfy the "arbitrary and capricious" standard.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is important for understanding the limits of individual property rights within a communal living arrangement, specifically in the context of cooperative apartments. It connects to the broader theme of **owner sovereignty and its limits** by showing how an individual owner's right to use their property is constrained by the collective governance structure they voluntarily joined. It contrasts with cases like *Jacque v. Steenberg Homes*, where the owner's right to exclude was paramount, by demonstrating a context where individual autonomy is significantly curtailed for the common good. It also relates to *Nahrstedt v. Lakeside Village Condominium Association* in its discussion of the deference given to community association rules, though *Nahrstedt* applied a "reasonableness" standard to recorded use restrictions.
2. A tension exists between the majority's desire to protect boards from "undue court involvement" and the concurrence's concern that the **business judgment rule** might actually lead to more litigation by inviting inquiry into "malevolent conduct" or "personal motives."

**Takeaway:** Courts will generally defer to the decisions of cooperative boards under the **business judgment rule**, upholding their actions if taken in good faith, within their authority, and for the cooperative's purposes, placing a high burden on the challenging tenant-shareholder.

---

## Murphy v. Financial Development Corp. (New Hampshire Supreme Court, 1985)

**Facts**
- In 1966, the Murphys bought a house with a mortgage.
- In March 1980, the Murphys refinanced their loan with Financial Development Corporation, executing a new promissory note and power of sale mortgage, which was later assigned to Colonial Deposit Company (collectively, "lenders").
- In February 1981, Richard Murphy became unemployed.
- By September 1981, the Murphys were seven months behind on mortgage payments and owed utility and tax assessments.
- After unsuccessful discussions about payment revisions, the lenders gave notice of intent to foreclose on October 6, 1981.
- The Murphys paid the seven months of mortgage arrearage but did not pay $643.18 in foreclosure costs and legal fees.
- The lenders scheduled the foreclosure sale for November 10, 1981.
