- At the Murphys' request, the lenders postponed the sale to December 15, 1981, advising it would cost an additional $100 and the sale would proceed unless $743.18 plus current mortgage payments were received by December 15.
- Notice of postponement was posted on the property, at Nashua City Hall, and the Post Office on November 10. No prospective bidders were present for the original sale date.
- In late November, the Murphys paid the October mortgage payment but made no further payments.
- An attempt by lenders to arrange new financing for the Murphys failed when the Murphys refused to pay for a new appraisal.
- On the morning of December 15, 1981, the Murphys tried to get another postponement, but the lenders refused unless costs and fees were paid.
- The sale proceeded at 10:00 a.m. on December 15. Only the Murphys, a lenders' representative, and an attorney conducting the sale were present.
- The lenders' representative made the only bid of $27,000, roughly the amount owed, which was accepted.
- Later that day, the attorney conducting the sale informed William Dube, a representative of Southern New Hampshire Home Traders, Inc., about the sale.
- Dube offered to buy the property for $27,000, which the lenders rejected, counter-offering $40,000.
- Within two days, Dube and the lenders agreed on a purchase price of $38,000, and the sale was completed.

**Procedural History**
- The Murphys sued to set aside the sale or for damages.
- The trial court (Master) found for the Murphys, awarding $27,000 against the lenders, finding they failed to exercise good faith and due diligence. The Master also ruled Southern was a bona fide purchaser. The Master awarded attorney's fees.
- The lenders appealed.

**Issue**
- Can a mortgagor sue a mortgagee for failing to obtain a fair price at a foreclosure sale if the mortgagor did not seek an injunction before the sale?
- Did the lenders fulfill their duty of good faith and due diligence in conducting the foreclosure sale?

**Holding**
- The Murphys (plaintiffs) win on the issue of liability for due diligence, but the damages calculation and attorney's fees are reversed. A mortgagor can sue after a foreclosure sale for unfairness occurring during the sale itself, even without a prior injunction. The lenders failed to exercise due diligence in obtaining a fair price. Reversed in part; affirmed in part; remanded.

**Rules**
- **Statutory Bar to Action (RSA 479:25, II).** A statute barring actions based on foreclosure validity if an injunction is not sought prior to sale only applies to facts the mortgagor knew or should have known in time to seek an injunction.
- **Mortgagee's Duty in Power of Sale.** A mortgagee executing a power of sale must comply with statutory procedural requirements and has a duty to protect the mortgagor's interests through good faith and due diligence.
- **Mortgagee's Fiduciary Duty.** In its role as a seller, a mortgagee's duty of good faith and due diligence is essentially that of a fiduciary.
- **Due Diligence in Foreclosure Sale.** A mortgagee must exert every reasonable effort to obtain a "fair and reasonable price under the circumstances," which may include adjourning the sale or establishing an upset price.
- **Inadequacy of Price.** Inadequacy of price alone is not sufficient to demonstrate bad faith unless the price is so low as to shock the judicial conscience.
- **Bad Faith.** To constitute bad faith, there must be an intentional disregard of duty or a purpose to injure.
- **Lack of Due Diligence.** Lack of due diligence is determined by whether a reasonable person in the mortgagee's place would have adjourned the sale or taken other measures to receive a fair price.
- **Damages for Bad Faith.** Where bad faith is found, damages are the difference between the fair market value and the price obtained at sale.
- **Damages for Lack of Due Diligence.** Where only lack of due diligence is found, damages are the difference between a fair price for the property and the price obtained at the foreclosure sale.

**Reasoning**
- The statutory bar (RSA 479:25, II) does not apply because the alleged unfairness (sale price) occurred during the sale itself, meaning the Murphys could not have known about it in time to seek an injunction.
- The lenders did not act in bad faith because there was no evidence of intentional disregard of duty or purpose to injure (e.g., discouraging other buyers).
- The lenders failed to exercise due diligence:
    - They did not reappraise the house despite knowing the Murphys had substantial equity ($19,000 based on a 1980 appraisal).
    - They were the only bidders and their bid only covered the debt, providing no return of the Murphys' equity.
    - They had reason to know they could make a substantial profit on a quick turnaround sale, evidenced by their immediate offer to sell for $40,000 and actual sale for $38,000 shortly after buying it for $27,000.
    - They should have taken more steps to ensure a higher price, such as establishing an upset price or commercially advertising the postponed sale.
    - Their statutory notice efforts were insufficient to demonstrate due diligence, as no one else appeared at the sale.
- The proper measure of damages for lack of due diligence is the difference between a "fair price" (not necessarily fair market value) and the price obtained at sale.
- Attorney's fees are not warranted because there was no finding of bad faith or oppressive conduct.

**Dissent (Brock, J.)**
- Agrees with the majority on the mortgagee's fiduciary duty and its definition.
- Disagrees that the record supports the master's finding of lack of due diligence.
- The master made no findings about what a voluntary seller would have done that the lenders did not.
- There is no evidence that a voluntary seller would have reappraised or set an upset price, nor what an appropriate upset price would have been.
- The lenders' subsequent offer to sell for $40,000 does not, by itself, show what a reasonable person would have done to ensure a fair price at the foreclosure sale.
- The master's implication that the lenders should have made a higher bid is not supported by authority, as the mortgagee's duty as seller ends once reasonable efforts are made to obtain a fair price.
- The sale price was not "so low as to shock the judicial conscience."

**NOTES:**
1. This case illustrates the limits on a mortgagee's power of sale, imposing a duty of "due diligence" beyond mere statutory compliance. It connects to **owner sovereignty and its limits** by showing that even a party with a contractual right to sell another's property (the mortgagee) is constrained by equitable duties. It contrasts with cases like *Williams v. Ford Motor Credit Co.* (though not directly cited), which might focus more on the strict terms of self-help repossession, by emphasizing the fiduciary-like duties in a foreclosure context.
2. What if the lenders had waited a month to sell the property to Dube? Would that change the court's finding that they "had reason to know" they could make a quick profit?

**Takeaway:** A mortgagee conducting a foreclosure sale has a fiduciary-like duty of due diligence to obtain a fair price for the property, even if statutory notice requirements are met, and failure to do so can result in damages.

## Skendzel v. Marshall (Supreme Court of Indiana, 1973)

**Facts**
- In December 1958, Mary Burkowski (vendor) entered into a land sale contract with Charles P. and Agnes P. Marshall (vendees) for $36,000.
- The contract required an initial payment of $500, another $500 by December 25, 1958, and $2,500 or more by January 15 of each year thereafter until paid.
- The contract included a prepayment clause allowing prepayments to be applied to future principal payments.
- The contract contained a forfeiture clause stating that if any default continued for 30 days, all prior payments would be forfeited and retained by the vendor as liquidated damages, and the contract would terminate.
- The vendor, Mary Burkowski, died in 1963.
- The plaintiffs (Skendzel, Legowski, Wysocki) are assignees of Burkowski's interests in the contract, receiving their assignment on June 27, 1968.
- The Marshalls made payments totaling $21,000 by February 15, 1965.
- No payments were made after February 15, 1965, leaving $15,000 unpaid on the original contract price.
- One year after receiving their assignment, the plaintiffs filed suit alleging default and seeking to enforce the forfeiture provision.

**Procedural History**
- Plaintiffs (vendor-assignees) sued to enforce a forfeiture clause in a land sale contract.
- The trial court issued a negative judgment against the plaintiffs.
- The Court of Appeals reversed, finding the defendants (vendees) had breached and the plaintiffs had not waived their right to enforce forfeiture.
- Defendants (vendees) petitioned for transfer to the Supreme Court of Indiana.
- The Supreme Court of Indiana granted transfer, reversed the Court of Appeals, and remanded with instructions.

**Issue**
Can a vendor enforce a forfeiture clause in a land sale contract, allowing them to retain all prior payments as liquidated damages and regain possession, when the vendee has paid a substantial portion of the purchase price?

**Holding**
The vendees (Marshalls) win. A vendor cannot enforce a forfeiture clause in a land sale contract when the vendee has paid a substantial amount, as such a forfeiture would be an unconscionable penalty; instead, the vendor must pursue a judicial foreclosure. Reversed and remanded.

**Rules**
- **Equity abhors forfeitures.** Equity has jurisdiction to grant relief against the enforcement of forfeitures when the loss or injury occasioned by the default can be exactly compensated.
- **Equitable interference with penalties and forfeitures.** Equity will relieve against a penalty or forfeiture if it is merely used to secure payment of a debt or performance of an act, and compensation can be adequately made for the breach.
- **Liquidated damages vs. penalty.** "Reasonable" liquidated damage provisions are permitted, but if damages are unreasonable or disproportionate to the actual loss, they are characterized as penal.
- **Land sale contract as a secured transaction.** A conditional land sale contract is in the nature of a secured transaction, creating a vendor's lien on the property to secure the unpaid balance, analogous to a mortgage.
- **Foreclosure of vendor's lien.** A vendor's lien under a land sale contract should be enforced through judicial foreclosure proceedings, similar to a mortgage, including redemption rights.
- **Rejection of strict foreclosure.** American jurisdictions have largely rejected strict foreclosure in favor of foreclosure by judicial sale, which is more equitable.
- **Appropriate circumstances for forfeiture.** Forfeiture may be appropriate for an abandoning or absconding vendee, or where the vendee has paid a minimal amount and the vendor is bearing the costs of upkeep.

**Reasoning**
- The forfeiture of $21,000 (well over half the original contract price) is clearly excessive and inconsistent with principles of fairness and equity.
- The vendees acquired a substantial equitable interest in the property, and forfeiture would result in substantial injustice.
- Equity disfavors forfeitures, especially when the amount forfeited is disproportionate to the actual damages suffered by the vendor.
- The court views a conditional land contract as a sale with a security interest, where the vendor's retention of legal title is functionally equivalent to reserving a lien or mortgage.
- To treat a land contract differently from a mortgage would be to prioritize form over substance.
- Judicial foreclosure, which includes a period of redemption, is a more equitable remedy than forfeiture, allowing the vendee to protect their equity.
- Trial Rule 69(C) and the mortgage foreclosure statute apply to "all liens upon real estate," and the vendor's interest in a land contract constitutes such a lien.
- The trial court correctly refused to enforce the forfeiture but erred by denying all relief; it should have ordered a judicial foreclosure.

**Dissent ([Judge])** / **Concurrence (Prentice, J.)**
- The opinion might be seen as indifferent to contract vendors' rights, which is not the intent.
- Vendees seeking to avoid forfeiture should clearly show the inequity of enforcement.
- If the vendee has little or no equity, or has abandoned the premises, forfeiture should be allowed.
- If forfeiture is deemed unjust, the court should grant the vendor maximum equitable relief, treating the transaction as a note and mortgage.
- This would include provisions for increased interest during default, acceleration clauses, attorney's fees, and waiver of valuation and appraisement laws, which are typically found in mortgages but often omitted from land contracts.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is crucial for understanding how courts use equitable principles to limit the enforcement of contractual terms, particularly forfeiture clauses in land sale contracts. It connects to the concept of **equity** and the court's role in preventing unconscionable outcomes, similar to how courts might intervene in cases of **waste** or **restraints on alienation**. It also highlights the functional equivalence of certain property interests, comparing a land contract to a mortgage, which is a common theme in property law (e.g., the "sticks in the bundle" metaphor).
2. What if the vendee had only paid $1,000 on a $100,000 contract and then defaulted? Would the court still require foreclosure, or would forfeiture be deemed appropriate under the "minimal amount" exception mentioned in the reasoning?

**Takeaway:** Courts will generally treat a land sale contract as a mortgage, requiring judicial foreclosure rather than forfeiture, to protect a vendee's substantial equitable interest and prevent unconscionable results.

## Wood v. Donohue (Ohio Court of Appeals, 1999)

**Facts**
- In 1983, Donohue and Schroot entered into a land installment contract with Wood to purchase a house for $87,900.
- Donohue and Schroot made a $30,000 down payment and agreed to make monthly payments with a seven-year balloon payment.
- The house was near the Fernald uranium processing plant.
- In 1985, a class action was initiated against the processing plant.
- Wood, Donohue, and Schroot filed claims in the class action.
- The balance of the purchase price for the house was paid in full in 1990.
- The class action settled, with members receiving money for diminution in property value as of December 18, 1984.
- The Fernald trustees awarded $9,478 for the diminished value of the property and issued a check in 1993 to Donohue, Schroot, and Wood.
- The check was not cashed.
- Wood filed a complaint seeking a declaration that she was entitled to 65.41% of the settlement check.
- The Fernald trustees deposited a new check into an escrow account, and Wood dismissed them from the case.

**Procedural History**
- The trial court entered judgment for Wood, awarding her 65.41% of the settlement and Donohue the remainder, based on the portion of the purchase money Donohue had paid as of December 18, 1984.
- Donohue appealed the trial court's decision.

**Issue**
- Is the buyer in a land installment contract entitled to the full amount of a settlement for property diminution that occurred after the contract was signed but before the full purchase price was paid, under the doctrine of equitable conversion?

**Holding**
- Donohue (appellant) wins. The buyer, as equitable owner, is entitled to the full settlement amount for property diminution that occurred after the land installment contract was executed. Reversed.

**Rules**
- **Equitable Conversion.** Where land is contracted to be sold, equity treats the exchange as actually taking place when the contract becomes effective, passing equitable title to the buyer.
- **Vendor's Interest.** The seller's interest under a contract of purchase is a right to receive the balance of the purchase price, secured by retaining legal title.
- **Vendor's Lien.** A seller's retention of legal title in a land installment contract is analogous to a mortgage for the unpaid purchase price, with title kept as security for the debt.
- **Vendee's Equitable Estate.** A vendee obtains an equitable estate entitling them to all incidents of ownership, including the right to use the property free from vendor interference.
- **Equitable Owner.** One who is recognized in equity as the owner of property because the real and beneficial use and title belong to them, even if bare legal title is vested in another.
- **Land Installment Contract (Ohio R.C. 5313.01(A)).** An executory agreement where a vendee agrees to pay the purchase price in installments, while the vendor retains title to the property as security for the vendee’s obligation.

**Reasoning**
- The doctrine of equitable conversion applies to land installment contracts in Ohio.
- Upon execution of the land installment contract, equitable title transferred to Donohue, making him the equitable owner.
- As equitable owner, Donohue bore all losses and was entitled to all benefits that might accrue to the property.
- The court found no reason not to apply the equitable conversion analysis, typically used for insurance proceeds after accidental damage, to settlement proceeds for diminution in value.
- The land installment contract's terms indicated the parties intended Donohue to bear any loss, as he was responsible for insurance premiums, was a named insured, and had options regarding insurance proceeds for repair or application to the purchase price.
- Wood's retention of legal title was solely as security for the unpaid purchase money.
- Since Wood had received the full purchase price by the time the settlement money was paid, her security interest was no longer impaired.
- Awarding Wood a portion of the settlement would result in her unfair enrichment by recovering twice for the same property.

**Dissent ([Judge])** / **Concurrence ([Judge])**
- None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case illustrates the application of the **equitable conversion** doctrine, which shifts the risk of loss and the benefit of appreciation to the buyer upon contract formation, even before legal title passes. It connects to the concept of **owner sovereignty** by determining who holds the beneficial interest in the property at a given time, and thus who is entitled to compensation for its diminution. It also touches upon the idea of a vendor's lien, similar to a mortgage, which is a common security interest in property law.
2. [CHECK: Is there any tension between the court's statement that the buyer "bore all losses" and the contract provision that the seller maintain "hazard" insurance? How would this play out if the property was destroyed before the purchase price was paid in full?]

**Takeaway:** Under the doctrine of equitable conversion, a buyer in a land installment contract becomes the equitable owner upon contract formation and is entitled to compensation for property diminution, even if legal title has not yet passed.

## Kotis v. Nowlin Jewelry Inc. (Texas Courts of Appeals, 1992)

**Facts**
- On June 11, 1990, Steve Sitton acquired a gold ladies Rolex watch from Nowlin Jewelry by forging a check belonging to his brother and misrepresenting his brother's authorization.
- The next day, Sitton telephoned Eddie Kotis, a used car dealership owner, and asked if he was interested in buying a Rolex watch.
- Kotis purchased the watch from Sitton for $3,550.00.
- Kotis called Nowlin Jewelry that same day and spoke with Cherie Nowlin, asking if a gold President model Rolex watch had been sold about a month prior.
- Kotis initially refused to identify himself to Ms. Nowlin.
- Ms. Nowlin told Kotis that Sitton had purchased the watch the day before.
- Ms. Nowlin called Kotis back and informed him that Sitton's check had not yet cleared.
- Kotis told Ms. Nowlin that he did not have the watch and did not want the watch, and refused to state Sitton's asking price.
- On June 15, 1990, Nowlin's bookkeeper learned that Sitton's check would be dishonored.
- John Nowlin, president of Nowlin's, called Kotis, but Kotis refused to speak with him and referred him to his attorney.
- On June 25, 1990, Kotis' attorney suggested Nowlin could buy the watch back from Kotis, which Nowlin refused.
- Sitton was indicted for forgery and theft.
- The district court ordered Nowlin's Jewelry to hold the watch until ownership was adjudicated.
- Nowlin filed suit seeking a declaratory judgment of ownership.
- Kotis filed a counterclaim for a declaration that he was a good faith purchaser.

**Procedural History**
- Nowlin Jewelry, Inc. filed suit seeking a declaratory judgment of ownership of the watch.
- Kotis filed a counterclaim.
- After a bench trial, the trial court rendered judgment declaring Nowlin the sole owner of the watch and awarded attorney's fees to Nowlin.
- Kotis appealed the trial court's judgment.

**Issue**
- Can a buyer who purchases goods from a swindler with voidable title acquire good title if the buyer did not act in good faith?

**Holding**
- Defendant (Nowlin Jewelry, Inc.) wins. A buyer who purchases goods from a swindler with voidable title cannot acquire good title if the buyer did not act in good faith. Affirmed.

**Rules**
- **UCC § 2.403(a) (Power to Transfer; Good Faith Purchase of Goods; "Entrusting").** A purchaser of goods acquires all title which his transferor had or had power to transfer, and a person with voidable title has power to transfer good title to a good faith purchaser for value.
- **UCC § 1.201(32) ("Purchase").** A "purchase" is a taking by sale, discount, negotiation, mortgage, pledge, lien, issue or reissue, gift or any other voluntary transaction creating an interest in property.
- **Void Title.** A thief who wrongfully takes goods against the will of the owner acquires only void title.
- **Voidable Title.** A swindler who fraudulently induces the victim to deliver goods voluntarily acquires voidable title.
- **UCC § 2.403(a)(4).** A purchaser can obtain voidable title even if delivery was procured through fraud punishable as larcenous under criminal law.
- **UCC § 1.201(19) ("Good Faith").** "Good faith" means honesty in fact in the conduct or transaction concerned.
- **Test for Good Faith.** The test for good faith is the actual belief of the party, not the reasonableness of that belief.
- **Evidence of Lack of Good Faith.** An unreasonably low price for goods is evidence the buyer knows the goods are stolen.

**Reasoning**
- Sitton obtained voidable title to the watch from Nowlin Jewelry.
    - Nowlin voluntarily delivered the watch to Sitton, even though Sitton used a forged check.
    - Sitton fraudulently induced Nowlin to deliver the watch, rather than taking it against Nowlin's will.
    - Under UCC § 2.403(a)(4), delivery procured through fraud punishable as larcenous still results in voidable title.
- Kotis did not act in good faith when purchasing the watch from Sitton.
    - The trial court, as the trier of fact, was the sole judge of witness credibility.
    - Kotis's testimony that he had already purchased the watch before learning Sitton's story was false could have been disbelieved by the judge.
    - Other facts supported the finding of lack of good faith:
        - Kotis initially refused to identify himself to Cherie Nowlin.
        - Kotis told Ms. Nowlin he did not have the watch and did not want it, despite having already purchased it.
        - Kotis refused to divulge Sitton's asking price.
        - Kotis later refused to speak with John Nowlin and referred him to his attorney.
    - Even if Kotis had already purchased the watch when he called Nowlin's, the extremely low price ($3,550 for a watch worth $7,000-$8,000) was evidence that Kotis knew the transaction was unlawful.
    - While the test for good faith is actual belief, it does not sanction willful disregard of suspicious facts.
- Because Kotis was not a good faith purchaser, he could not acquire good title from Sitton, who had only voidable title.
- Therefore, Nowlin Jewelry retained title and was entitled to possession of the watch.

**Dissent ([Judge])** / **Concurrence ([Judge])**
- None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case illustrates the **good faith purchaser for value** doctrine under the UCC, specifically how it applies to goods obtained by fraud. It distinguishes between void and voidable title, a concept that is crucial for understanding the security of transactions involving personal property. It connects to the concept of **first possession** and how title can be transferred, even if imperfectly.
2. [CHECK: The opinion states that the test for good faith is "actual belief" but then says it "need not let this standard sanction willful disregard of suspicious facts." This seems to create a tension between a subjective and objective standard for good faith. How would a court reconcile these two statements in a different factual scenario?]

**Takeaway:** A buyer cannot claim good faith purchaser status if they willfully disregard suspicious facts surrounding the transaction, even if the seller had voidable title.

## Hauck v. Crawford (South Dakota Supreme Court, 1953)

**Facts**
- Plaintiff Hauck is a 44-year-old farmer with an 8th-grade education, owning two sections of land in South Dakota and North Dakota.
- On May 23, 1951, three men, including Mr. Crawford, approached Hauck at a neighbor's place to discuss leasing his land for oil and gas.
- Crawford offered 25 cents per acre for a lease, which Hauck agreed to.
- One of the men prepared papers on a typewriter in the back seat of a car, using printed forms with fine print.
- The man indicated where Hauck should sign, and after signing once, partially turned the sheet and asked Hauck to sign again, stating it was part of the lease.
- Hauck testified no mention was made of a mineral deed, and Crawford corroborated this.
- Separate instruments were prepared for land in each state.
- Hauck never received copies of the instruments he signed.
- Hauck had signed a mineral deed conveying one-half of the minerals in his land to D.W. Crawford.
- This deed was filed for record on June 1, 1951.
- On May 29, 1951, Crawford conveyed these mineral rights to defendants White and Duncan in Gainesville, Texas.

**Procedural History**
- The trial court entered judgment canceling the deeds based on alleged fraud.
- Defendants appealed.

**Issue**
- Can a deed obtained through "fraud in the execution" be canceled against a subsequent bona fide purchaser for value if the original grantor was negligent in signing the deed?

**Holding**
- The defendants (appellants) win. The judgment is reversed.
- A deed obtained by "fraud in the execution" is void as between the original parties, but if the original grantor was negligent in signing, the deed may be valid against a bona fide purchaser for value.
- Reversed.

**Rules**
- **Fraud in the Execution (Fraud in the Factum).** This type of fraud relates to misrepresentation of the contents of a document by which one is induced to sign a paper thinking that it is other than it really is.
- **Void vs. Voidable.** An instrument obtained by fraud in the execution is void, meaning "the contractual knot was never tied" and it has no legal effect. An instrument obtained by fraud in the inducement is voidable.
- **Negligence and Fraud.** As between the original parties, the perpetrator of fraud cannot avoid his acts by showing the victim was negligent.
- **Bona Fide Purchaser.** A bona fide purchaser for value without notice may acquire good title even if the original transaction was fraudulent, unless the original instrument was void and the true owner was not negligent or did not create an estoppel.
- **Estoppel.** An estoppel arises when, by conduct or acts, a party intentionally or through culpable neglect induces another to believe certain facts exist, and the other party relies and acts on that belief to their prejudice.
- **Culpable Negligence.** In civil actions, "culpable negligence" means the same as actionable negligence.
- **Forgery (Rejected).** The court rejects the minority view that obtaining a signature through fraud in the execution constitutes forgery.

**Reasoning**
- The court agrees with the trial court that Crawford's actions constituted "fraud in the execution" because he misrepresented the papers and tricked Hauck into signing a deed he believed was a lease.
- Under the rule from *Federal Land Bank v. Houck*, a deed obtained by fraud in the execution is void between the original parties, and the grantor's negligence does not neutralize this fraud.
- Since the deed was void, Crawford had no title to convey to White and Duncan.
- However, the court notes that even if a deed is void, if the plaintiff was negligent or created an estoppel, they should bear the burden rather than a bona fide purchaser.
- The trial court did not make a specific finding on whether Hauck was negligent when he signed the deed.
- The question of whether Hauck acted as a person of reasonable and ordinary care, given his capacity and intelligence, is a question of fact for the trial court to determine.
- The court rejects the idea that obtaining a signature through fraud in the execution constitutes forgery, preferring its established rule regarding fraud in the execution.

**Dissent (Judge)**
- None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case builds on the concept of "void" versus "voidable" instruments, which is crucial for understanding how property interests can be transferred and protected. It connects to cases like *Johnson v. M'Intosh* in establishing the validity of title, but here, the focus is on the *transfer* of title and the protection of subsequent purchasers. The distinction between void and voidable is critical for the **title_transfer_land_transactions_good_faith_purchasers_recording** unit.
2. What level of "culpable negligence" would be sufficient to create an estoppel against Hauck? Is it reasonable to expect an 8th-grade educated farmer to scrutinize fine print when presented with documents by someone he trusts, especially when told it's just a lease?

**Takeaway:** A deed obtained through fraud in the execution is void between the original parties, but the original grantor's negligence may prevent them from recovering the property from a bona fide purchaser for value.

## Schwab v. Timmons (Wisconsin Supreme Court, 1999)

**Facts**
- Before 1854, the United States owned Lots 2, 3, and 4, which were located between Green Bay and a bluff.
- In 1854, the United States granted Lot 4 to Ingebret Torgerson but retained Lots 2 and 3. At this time, the lots extended to a public roadway above the bluff.
- In 1882, the United States granted Lots 2 and 3 to Halvor Anderson.
- At some point, Lots 2, 3, and 4 were subdivided into parcels.
- The Schwabs' parents purchased their parcels in Lot 2 in the 1940s. These parcels extended from Green Bay to above the bluff, with access to a public roadway.
- In 1965 and 1974, the Schwabs inherited these parcels.
- After 1974, the Schwabs conveyed the property above the bluff to relatives, retaining the parcel below the bluff.
- McCormick inherited her parcel, which also originally included land above and below the bluff with highway access. She conveyed the property above the bluff to a third party, retaining the parcel below.
- The petitioners' parcels are now bordered by water on the west and a bluff on the east, making them landlocked.
- A private road runs north from Hobler's parcel across the respondents' properties, terminating on the Lenz parcel.
- Negotiations for the petitioners to extend this private road to their properties failed.
- In 1988, the petitioners requested the Village of Ephraim to extend a public road to their properties, but the request was denied.

**Procedural History**
- Petitioners (Schwabs and McCormick) brought a declaratory judgment action seeking an easement by necessity or implication.
- The circuit court granted the respondents' motions to dismiss, concluding that the historical circumstances did not fit typical situations for implied easements and that any easement would not have survived due to lack of recording or notice.
- The court of appeals summarily affirmed the circuit court's decision.
- Petitioners appealed to the Wisconsin Supreme Court.

**Issue**
Can landowners claim an easement by necessity or implication over neighboring properties when their parcels became landlocked due to their own actions in conveying away their highway access, or due to geographical barriers and historical conveyances by the federal government?

**Holding**
Defendant (respondents) wins. The petitioners are not entitled to an easement by implication or necessity because their parcels were not landlocked at the time of the original conveyances by the United States, and they created their own landlocked parcels by conveying away their highway access. Affirmed.

**Rules**
- **Easement by Implication.** An easement by implication arises when there has been a separation of title, a use before separation that was so obvious and continuous as to show it was meant to be permanent, and the easement is necessary for the beneficial enjoyment of the land granted or retained.
- **Necessity for Implied Easement.** The necessity for an implied easement must be "so clear and absolute that without the easement the grantee cannot enjoy the use of the property granted to him for the purposes to which similar property is customarily devoted."
- **Easement by Necessity.** An easement of necessity arises where an owner severs a landlocked portion of their property by conveying such parcel to another.
- **Elements of Easement by Necessity.** To establish an easement by necessity, a party must show common ownership of the two parcels prior to severance of the landlocked parcel, and that the owner of the now landlocked parcel cannot access a public roadway from their own property.
- **Location of Easement by Necessity.** If established, an easement by necessity will be implied over the land retained by the grantor.
- **Rejected: Geographical Barriers for Easement by Necessity.** Wisconsin courts have not recognized geographical barriers alone as circumstances warranting an easement by necessity.
- **Way of Necessity is Not Merely Convenience.** A way of necessity is not merely one of convenience, and the law will not imply such a way where another method for obtaining access at a reasonable expense exists.
- **Grantor Cannot Claim Easement by Necessity Over Grantee.** An easement by necessity exists where an owner sells a landlocked parcel to another, recognizing a way of necessity in the grantee over the land retained by the grantor.
- **Public Policy Against Implied Covenants.** Public policy in Wisconsin strongly opposes the implication of covenants of conveyance, including easements.
- **Reliance on Public Records.** A buyer of land may rely on public records as information of all conveyances and on the words of instruments for all rights thereunder.
- **Sources of Information for Land Rights.** A purchaser of real estate has three sources of information: reviewing the chain of title, searching other public records, and inspecting the land itself.
- **Notice of Easement.** A purchaser of land without knowledge or actual or constructive notice of an easement takes title relieved of the burden of the easement.

**Reasoning**
- The petitioners failed to establish an easement by implication because they did not allege that any use by the United States was so obvious, manifest, or continuous as to show it was meant to be permanent.
- The petitioners failed to establish an easement by necessity because the parcels were not landlocked at the time of the original conveyances by the United States.
    - At the time the United States conveyed Lot 4 in 1854, the eastern boundary of the lots extended to a public roadway above the bluff, providing access.
    - The United States never severed a landlocked portion of its property that was inaccessible from a public roadway.
- The court rejected the argument that geographical barriers alone warrant an easement by necessity.
    - A way of necessity is not merely for convenience; other methods of access, even if less convenient or more expensive, preclude an easement by necessity.
    - The petitioners had access to a public road, which they sold off, creating their own landlocked situation.
- The petitioners are grantors, not grantees, of the conveyances that resulted in their landlocked property, which is contrary to the principle of an easement by necessity being implied over the grantor's retained land.
- Granting an easement in this case would be contrary to Wisconsin's public policy against encumbrances and the reliance on recording and conveyance statutes.
    - It would sanction "hidden easements" not apparent from public records or inspection.
    - The respondents had no actual or constructive notice of any potential easement from the 1854 conveyance.

**Dissent ([Judge])**
None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case reinforces the **strong presumption against implied easements** and the importance of the **recording acts** in Wisconsin. It connects to cases like *Miller v. Hoeschler* and *Backhausen v. Mayer* in its emphasis on the disfavor of implied easements and the need for clear notice. It also highlights the principle that a party cannot create their own necessity and then demand an easement, similar to how a party cannot claim necessity if they have other reasonable access, as seen in *Ludke v. Egan*.
2. What if the original conveyance by the United States had explicitly reserved an easement for future access to Lots 2 and 3, but this reservation was not properly recorded? Would the current landowners be bound by it, or would the lack of recording defeat the claim?

**Takeaway:** Courts are reluctant to grant easements by necessity or implication, especially when the necessity is self-created or when it would undermine the principles of notice and reliance on public records.

---

## Warsaw v. Chicago Metallic Ceilings Inc. (Supreme Court of California, 1984)

**Facts**
- In 1972, plaintiffs and defendant acquired contiguous, unimproved parcels of land from a common owner in Vernon, California.
- Plaintiffs constructed a large commercial building on their parcel, covering almost the entire property, with a 40-foot wide paved driveway along the northern edge for access to loading docks.
- Defendant constructed a smaller building on its parcel, leaving a 150-foot wide vacant strip of ground along the side abutting plaintiffs' property.
- Plaintiffs' 40-foot driveway was inadequate for large trucks to turn and position themselves at the loading docks without driving onto defendant's property.
- The possibility of creating an easement over defendant's property was considered and rejected during the original negotiations between the seller, plaintiffs, and defendant.
- From 1972 to 1979, trucks servicing plaintiffs' facility used a portion of the vacant ground on defendant's property to enter, turn, park, and leave the loading dock area.
- On at least two occasions during this period, plaintiffs unsuccessfully tried to acquire an easement from defendant or create mutual easements.
- In 1979, defendant planned to build a warehouse on the southerly portion of its property, including the area used by plaintiffs.
- Defendant raised a dirt pad along its southerly property line, approximately five feet from the property line, which blocked plaintiffs' use of the area.
- Plaintiffs filed an action for injunctive and declaratory relief.
- The trial court denied plaintiffs' request for a preliminary injunction.
- Defendant proceeded to erect a building on the contested area while the litigation was pending.

**Procedural History**
- The trial court found that plaintiffs had acquired a 25-foot wide prescriptive easement over defendant's property and ordered defendant to remove the portion of its building interfering with the easement. The trial court also reserved jurisdiction to award damages if defendant failed to comply.
- Defendant appealed the trial court's judgment.
- The Court of Appeal affirmed the trial court's finding of a prescriptive easement and the mandatory injunction.
- The Supreme Court of California granted a hearing to consider compensation issues.

**Issue**
Can a property owner who acquires a valid prescriptive easement be required to compensate the underlying property owner for the fair market value of the easement or the cost of removing encroaching structures?

**Holding**
Plaintiff (easement owner) wins. One who acquires a valid prescriptive easement cannot be required to compensate the underlying property owner for the fair market value of the easement, nor for the cost of removing encroaching structures if the encroachment was willful. Affirmed.

**Rules**
- **Elements of Prescriptive Easement.** The party claiming a prescriptive easement must show use of the property that has been open, notorious, continuous, and adverse for an uninterrupted period of five years.
- **Definite and Certain Line of Travel.** The existence of a prescriptive easement must be shown by a definite and certain line of travel for the statutory period, though slight deviations from the accustomed route will not defeat it.
- **Presumption of Adverse Use.** Continuous use of an easement over a long period of time without the landowner's interference is presumptive evidence of its existence and, in the absence of evidence of mere permissive use, is sufficient to sustain a judgment.
- **Mandatory Injunction for Easement Protection.** A court of equity may issue a mandatory injunction for the protection and preservation of an easement, including an order for removal of an obstruction already erected.
- **Balancing Hardships for Encroachments.** A court has discretion to balance the hardships and deny removal of an encroachment if it was innocently made, does not irreparably injure the plaintiff, and the cost of removal would greatly exceed the inconvenience to the plaintiff.
- **Willful Encroachment.** If an encroaching structure was willfully erected with knowledge of the claimed easement, a mandatory injunction for its removal may issue even if the cost of removal is great.
- **No Compensation for Prescriptive Easement.** The statutory procedure for acquiring an easement by prescription retains the traditional common law rule that such an easement may be obtained without incurring any liability to the underlying property owner.
- **Legislative Prerogative for Compensation.** Any decision to alter the system of acquiring an interest in land by prescription by requiring the payment of compensation is a matter for the Legislature.
- **Rejected: Equitable Power to Require Compensation.** Rejected: Courts acting in equity have the power to require payment of fair market value for a prescriptive easement.

**Reasoning**
- The trial court's findings that plaintiffs established the elements of a prescriptive easement were supported by substantial evidence.
    - The use was open and notorious, as trucks regularly used the area.
    - The use was continuous for more than five years.
    - The use was adverse, as defendant's refusal to negotiate an express easement indicated no permission was given.
    - The line of travel was sufficiently definite, despite slight variations by different truck drivers, as they all used the parcel for the same purpose.
- The mandatory injunction ordering removal of defendant's building was appropriate because defendant erected the structure after plaintiffs filed suit and while litigation was pending, indicating a willful encroachment.
    - Defendant "gambled on the outcome of the action and lost."
    - Courts have ordered removal of willful encroachments despite disproportionate hardship to the defendant.
- Requiring plaintiffs to compensate defendant for the fair market value of the easement is not authorized by statute or common law.
    - Civil Code section 1007 states that occupancy for the statutory period confers "title by prescription," which is "sufficient against all."
    - Requiring compensation would defeat the policies underlying adverse possession and prescription, which aim to reduce litigation and stabilize long-continued property uses.
    - The "lost grant" theory of prescription justifies its continuance based on functional utility in terminating controversies and stabilizing property uses.
    - Altering this system to require compensation is a matter for the Legislature, not the courts.
- Requiring plaintiffs to contribute to the cost of removing the encroaching structure is not equitable.
    - Defendant erected the structure with prior notice of plaintiffs' claim and while the suit was pending.
    - It would be inequitable to charge plaintiffs for removing a structure willfully erected by defendant.

**Dissent (Reynoso, J.)**
- The majority's decision to deny compensation for the fair market value of the easement is unjust.
- While the declaration of a prescriptive easement is an action at law, the protection of that right is an action in equity, giving courts power to impose conditions.
- Civil Code section 1007 merely codifies the general concept of prescriptive easements and does not remove the courts' traditional equitable power to ensure fairness.
- The court in equity should exercise its power to require plaintiffs to pay fair market value for the property interest acquired, especially since plaintiffs are "trespassers" acquiring valuable property rights "free of charge."
- The policy reasons cited by the majority (reducing litigation, protecting possession, favoring use over disuse) are unconvincing in this case.
    - Denying compensation does not reduce litigation; it forces owners to sue when trespass occurs.
    - While possession is protected, plaintiffs should pay for it.
    - Modern society prefers planned use over ad hoc use by a trespasser, especially in urban settings.
- The court's role is to apply a "conception of justice in accordance with the prevailing reason and conscience of the time," and the majority's decision contravenes modern notions of fairness.
- The case should be remanded to fix reasonable compensation (fair market value) to be paid by plaintiffs to defendant.

**Concurrence (Grodin, J.)**
- Agrees with the policy criticisms in the dissenting opinion regarding the lack of compensation for prescriptive easements in modern society.
- Believes that any change to this area of law should come from the Legislature, not the courts.
- Notes that the Legislature has already addressed the harshness of prescriptive easements by enacting Civil Code section 1008, which allows property owners to prevent acquisition of an easement by posting a sign. This legislative attention suggests the courts should defer to the Legislature for further changes.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is important for understanding the **prescriptive easement** doctrine, particularly the lack of a compensation requirement for the owner whose land is burdened. It connects to **adverse possession** (as the court discusses the underlying policies) by showing that the law favors long-standing use and stability of property interests, even if acquired without permission. It also touches on the equitable powers of courts, similar to cases like *Pile v. Pedrick* or *Golden Press Inc. v. Rylands*, regarding mandatory injunctions for encroachments, but here the willfulness of the encroacher is key.
2. The tension between the majority's strict application of the statutory and common law rule for prescriptive easements and the dissent's call for equitable compensation highlights a fundamental debate in property law: should courts prioritize established legal rules and stability, or should they adapt to modern notions of fairness and prevent "unjust enrichment"? This raises a hypo: What if the defendant's encroachment was truly innocent and the cost of removal was astronomical compared to the benefit to the easement holder? Would the court still order removal without compensation?

**Takeaway:** A party who establishes a prescriptive easement is generally not required to pay compensation to the burdened landowner, and a willful encroacher may be forced to remove their structure without contribution from the easement holder.

---

## Holbrook v. Taylor (Supreme Court of Kentucky, 1976)

**Facts**
- In 1942, the Holbrooks (appellants) purchased property.
- In 1944, the Holbrooks gave permission for a haul road to be cut on their property to move coal from a new mine, and they were paid a royalty for its use until the mine closed in 1949.
- In 1957, the Holbrooks built a tenant house on their property, and they and their tenant used the roadway.
- In 1961, the tenant house burned down and was not replaced.
- In 1964, the Taylors (appellees) bought a three-acre building site adjoining the Holbrooks' property.
- In 1965, the Taylors built their residence on their property.
- During the construction of the Taylors' home, they used the roadway for ingress and egress for workmen, hauling machinery and materials, and making general improvements, with the Holbrooks' consent.
- The Taylors widened the roadway, put in a culvert, and graveled part of it with "red dog" at a cost of approximately $100.
- After the $25,000 residence was completed, the Taylors continued to use the roadway regularly.
- In the fall of 1970, a dispute arose between the parties regarding the use of the roadway.
- The Holbrooks erected a steel cable across the roadway and put up "no-trespassing" signs.

**Procedural History**
- The Taylors filed suit to require the removal of the obstruction and to declare their right to use the roadway.
- The trial court found that a right to use the roadway by prescription had not been established, but that it had been established by estoppel.
- The Holbrooks appealed.

**Issue**
Can a license to use a roadway over another's land become irrevocable by estoppel if the licensee makes substantial expenditures and improvements on the faith of that license?

**Holding**
The appellees (Taylors) win. A license to use a roadway becomes irrevocable by estoppel when the licensee has exercised the privilege and made substantial expenditures and improvements on the faith of the license, with the licensor's knowledge and acquiescence. Affirmed.

**Rules**
- **Easement Creation.** An easement may be created by express written grant, by implication, by prescription, or by estoppel.
- **Easement by Prescription.** An easement, such as a right of way, is created when the owner of a tenement to which the right is claimed, or those under whom he claims title, have openly, peaceably, continuously, and under a claim of right adverse to the owner of the soil, and with his knowledge and acquiescence, used a way over the lands of another for as much as 15 years.
- **Irrevocable License by Estoppel.** Where a license is not a bare right of entry, but includes the right to erect structures and acquire an interest in the land in the nature of an easement by the construction of improvements thereon, the licensor may not revoke the license and restore his premises to their former condition after the licensee has exercised the privilege given by the license and erected the improvements at considerable expense.
- **Rejected: Revocable License.** Many courts hold that a licensee is conclusively presumed as a matter of law to know that a license is revocable at the pleasure of the licensor, and if he expends money in connection with his entry upon the land of the latter, he does so at his peril. (This rule is explicitly rejected in Kentucky for licenses involving improvements).

**Reasoning**
- The trial court was justified in finding no prescriptive easement because the use of the road prior to 1965 was by permission and there was no evidence of adverse, continuous, or uninterrupted use.
- The use of the roadway by the Taylors for construction, general improvements, and maintenance, and their construction of a $25,000 residence, all with the Holbrooks' actual consent or tacit approval, demonstrates that the license to use the roadway cannot be revoked.
- The court cites prior cases establishing that a license becomes irrevocable when the licensee makes substantial expenditures or improvements on the faith of the license.
    - In *Lashley Telephone Co. v. Durbin*, the court held that a licensor cannot revoke a license after the licensee has erected structures and made improvements at considerable expense.
    - In *Gibbs v. Anderson*, the court reversed a denial of a claim for a roadway by estoppel where the landowner agreed to the passway and watched the claimant expend money on its construction.
    - In *McCoy v. Hoffman*, the court affirmed a right to use a road by estoppel, noting that where a licensee makes substantial expenditures on the faith of a license, it becomes irrevocable.
    - In *Akers v. Moore*, the court found a right to use a passway by estoppel where landowners acquiesced in changes and allowed money to be spent on improvements, stating it would be unconscionable to permit revocation.
- There is no other reasonable location for a roadway to provide access to the Taylors' property.
- The evidence supports the trial court's finding that the right to use the roadway was established by estoppel.

**Dissent (STEPHENSON, J.)**
None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case illustrates the doctrine of **irrevocable license by estoppel**, which acts as an exception to the general rule that licenses are revocable at will. It connects to the broader theme of how property rights can be created or altered by reliance and equity, even without a formal grant. It contrasts with cases like *Wood v. Leadbitter*, which strictly adheres to the revocability of licenses.
2. [CHECK: The opinion mentions a dissent by Stephenson, J., but provides no reasoning. Is it important to note that the reasoning is unavailable?]

**Takeaway:** A license to use land can become an irrevocable easement if the licensee makes substantial investments in reliance on the license, preventing the licensor from revoking it due to equitable estoppel.

---

## Fontainebleau Hotel Corp. v. Forty-Five Twenty-Five Inc. (Florida District Court of Appeal, 1959)

**Facts**
- The Eden Roc Hotel was built in 1955, adjoining the Fontainebleau Hotel to the north.
- The Fontainebleau Hotel began constructing a 14-story addition 20 feet from its north property line.
- The addition, when completed, would cast a shadow over the cabana, swimming pool, and sunbathing areas of the Eden Roc during winter afternoons.
- The Eden Roc Hotel alleged the construction was motivated by malice and ill will.
- The City of Miami Beach enacted a "Shadow Ordinance" at the Eden Roc's behest, which was later held invalid.
- The Fontainebleau's construction was proceeding under a permit issued by the city, authorizing a 76-foot setback from the ocean bulkhead line.
- The Eden Roc's objection to the setback distance was raised almost a year after construction began, when the addition was approximately eight stories high.

**Procedural History**
- The Eden Roc Hotel (plaintiff) sought an injunction to stop the construction.
- The trial court granted a temporary injunction.
- The Fontainebleau Hotel (defendant) appealed the temporary injunction.

**Issue**
Can a landowner be enjoined from constructing a building on their property that casts a shadow on an adjoining landowner's property, in the absence of a contractual or statutory obligation, or an easement?

**Holding**
The defendant (Fontainebleau Hotel Corp.) wins. A landowner has no legal right to the free flow of light and air across an adjoining landowner's property, and a structure serving a useful purpose does not constitute a nuisance even if it causes injury by blocking light and air. Reversed with directions to dismiss the complaint.

**Rules**
- **Sic utere tuo ut alienum non laedas.** One must use their property so as not to injure the lawful rights of another.
- **Rejected: English doctrine of "ancient lights."** This doctrine, which grants a legal right to unobstructed light and air after uninterrupted use for a period of 20 years, has been unanimously repudiated in the United States.
- **Lawful use of property.** A property owner may put their own property to any reasonable and lawful use, so long as they do not deprive an adjoining landowner of any right of enjoyment of their property recognized and protected by law, and so long as their use is not a nuisance.
- **Right to light and air.** In the absence of a contractual or statutory obligation, or an easement, a landowner has no legal right to the free flow of light and air across the adjoining land of a neighbor.
- **Spite structures.** Where a structure serves a useful and beneficial purpose, it does not give rise to a cause of action, even if it causes injury by cutting off light and air and interfering with a view, and regardless of the fact that the structure may have been erected partly for spite.
- **Judicial legislation.** Changing the universal rule that adjoining landowners have an equal right to build to the line of their respective tracts and to such a height as desired (in the absence of restrictions) amounts to judicial legislation.
- **Violation of ordinance.** Even if a building ordinance is violated, a plaintiff may not have a cause of action if the violation would make no appreciable difference to the alleged injury.

**Reasoning**
- The maxim *sic utere tuo ut alienum non laedas* means one must use their property so as not to injure the lawful rights of another, not that one must never use their property in a way that causes any injury to a neighbor.
- There is no legal right to the free flow of light and air from adjoining land in the absence of a contract, statute, or easement.
- The English doctrine of "ancient lights" has been repudiated in the United States.
- A structure serving a useful purpose does not create a cause of action, even if it blocks light and air and was partly motivated by spite.
- To change this established rule would be judicial legislation; such changes should be made through comprehensive planning and zoning ordinances.
- There is no statutory basis, express or implied easement, or prescriptive right for the plaintiff's claim to light and air.
- Even if the setback ordinance was violated, the plaintiff would not have a cause of action because the violation would not significantly alter the shadow problem, and the construction was proceeding under a valid permit.
- The plaintiff's objection to the setback was made late in the construction process, after significant expenditure by the defendant.

**Dissent ([Judge])** / **Concurrence ([Judge])**
None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is a foundational example of the **owner autonomy** principle in property law, emphasizing the right of a landowner to use their property as they see fit, even if it negatively impacts a neighbor, as long as no "lawful right" of the neighbor is infringed. It directly rejects the English "ancient lights" doctrine, which is a key distinction in American property law. This case contrasts with cases like *Jost v. Dairyland Power Cooperative* or *Campbell v. Seaman*, which deal with nuisance where there is a direct physical invasion or interference with the use and enjoyment of land, rather than merely blocking light and air.
2. This case raises the question of whether there are any limits to a landowner's right to build on their property, particularly when the intent is malicious, and the harm is significant. What if the primary purpose of the addition was *solely* to cast a shadow, with no other beneficial use?

**Takeaway:** American property law generally does not recognize a right to light and air, prioritizing a landowner's right to develop their property over a neighbor's access to sunlight.

---

## Neponsit Property Owners' Association v. Emigrant Industrial Savings Bank (New York Court of Appeals, 1938)

**Facts**
- In January 1911, Neponsit Realty Company (grantor) filed a map for a residential development in Queens County.
- In 1917, Neponsit Realty Company conveyed a lot to Robert Oldner Deyer and his wife (predecessors in title to defendant).
- The deed contained a covenant requiring an annual charge, not exceeding $4 per lot, for the maintenance of roads, paths, parks, beach, sewers, and other public purposes.
- The covenant stated that the charge would become a lien on the land and could be collected by a Property Owners' Association, which might be organized later.
- The covenant also stated it would "run with the land" until January 31, 1940.
- Every subsequent deed in the chain of title, including the referee's deed to the defendant, conveyed the property subject to the covenants and restrictions of former deeds of record.
- The Neponsit Property Owners' Association, Inc. (plaintiff) was organized to receive and expend these sums for the benefit of property owners.
- The defendant, Emigrant Industrial Savings Bank, purchased the land at a judicial sale.

**Procedural History**
- Plaintiff (Neponsit Property Owners' Association) brought an action to foreclose a lien on the defendant's land, based on the covenant.
- Defendant moved for judgment on the pleadings, dismissing the complaint.
- Plaintiff moved to dismiss defendant's counterclaim and strike out affirmative defenses.
- The Special Term granted plaintiff's motion and denied defendant's motion.
- The Appellate Division unanimously affirmed the Special Term's order and granted leave to appeal to the Court of Appeals upon certified questions.

**Issue**
Can a property owners' association enforce an affirmative covenant to pay money for the maintenance of common areas against a subsequent purchaser, even if the association does not own property benefiting from the covenant and there is no strict privity of estate?

**Holding**
Plaintiff (Neponsit Property Owners' Association) wins. The covenant is enforceable against the defendant because it "touches and concerns" the land in substance, and the corporate plaintiff, acting as a representative of the property owners, satisfies the requirement of privity of estate in substance. Affirmed.

**Rules**
- **Requirements for a Real Covenant to Run with the Land.** A covenant will run with the land and be enforceable against a subsequent purchaser if: (1) grantor and grantee intended it to run with the land; (2) it "touches" or "concerns" the land; and (3) there is "privity of estate" between the promisee and promisor.
- **"Touching and Concerning" the Land.** This test is vague and requires the court to determine, in its best judgment, whether the covenant affects the legal relations (advantages and burdens) of the parties as owners of particular parcels of land, rather than merely as members of the community.
- **Affirmative Covenants.** While traditionally, a covenant to do an affirmative act (as opposed to a negative restriction) does not run with the land, exceptions exist, especially where the covenant to pay money is for something done in connection with the promisor's land and substantially alters the legal rights flowing from ownership.
- **Privity of Estate.** This requirement, in the context of covenants running with the land, can be satisfied in substance, even if not in technical form, when a corporate entity acts as a representative or agent for the property owners for whose benefit the covenant was created.
- **Rejected: Strict Privity of Estate for Corporate Associations.** The court rejects a blind adherence to the ancient formula of privity of estate when a corporate property owners' association is formed as a medium for the enjoyment of common rights by property owners.

**Reasoning**
- The covenant clearly shows the grantor's intent for it to run with the land and be enforceable by a property owners' association.
- The covenant "touches and concerns" the land because it requires payment for the maintenance of common areas (roads, parks, etc.) that benefit the defendant's property and other properties in the development.
    - The payment is for something done in connection with the land, not disconnected from its use.
    - It imposes a burden on the land that increases the value of the land by ensuring the maintenance of appurtenant easements and rights of common enjoyment.
    - To distinguish this covenant based on it being an affirmative act to pay money, rather than a restriction on use, would be to prioritize form over substance.
    - The court looks to the "substantial effect" of the covenant on the legal rights of the parties as landowners, rather than technical distinctions.
- While the plaintiff association does not own property in the tract, it was formed as a convenient instrument for the property owners to advance their common interests.
    - Ignoring the corporate form, the association acts as an agent or representative of the property owners.
    - To deny enforcement based on a lack of technical privity of estate would be "blind adherence to an ancient formula" that does not fit the modern context of property owners' associations.
    - In substance, there is privity of estate between the plaintiff (representing the property owners) and the defendant.
- The defense that the lien is unenforceable under Real Property Law sections 242 and 259 is insufficient.

**Dissent ([Judge])** / **Concurrence ([Judge])**
None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is crucial for understanding the evolution of **covenants running with the land**, particularly the "touch and concern" and "privity of estate" requirements. It demonstrates a move away from rigid, formalistic interpretations towards a more flexible, substantive approach, especially in the context of modern residential developments and property owners' associations. It connects to cases like *Tulk v. Moxhay* by showing how courts adapt equitable principles to enforce restrictions, even if not strictly meeting common law requirements. It also contrasts with older, more rigid applications of the "affirmative covenant" rule, as seen in *Miller v. Clary*, by finding an exception based on the covenant's practical effect.
2. The court explicitly states that the "touch and concern" test is "too vague to be of much assistance." This raises the question of how much discretion courts have in applying this test and whether a more predictable standard is desirable.

**Takeaway:** Courts may interpret traditional requirements for covenants running with the land flexibly to uphold the substance and intent of agreements that benefit a community, especially in modern residential developments.

---

## Sanborn v. McLean (Michigan Supreme Court, 1925)

**Facts**
- In 1891, the Green Lawn subdivision was platted with 91 lots on Collingwood Avenue, designed for residence purposes.
- On December 28, 1892, Robert J. and Joseph R. McLaughlin, common owners of the lots, deeded lots 37-41 and 58-62 with restrictions allowing only residences costing at least $2,500 and set back 20 feet from the street.
- On July 24, 1893, the McLaughlins conveyed lots 17-21, 78-82, and lot 98 with the same restrictions.
- On September 7, 1893, the McLaughlins conveyed lot 86 (the defendants' lot) without express restrictions in the deed.
- Subsequent deeds to other lots were executed by the McLaughlins, some with restrictions and some without.
- For over 30 years, all lots on Collingwood Avenue were used strictly for residences, with expensive dwellings built on them.
- In 1910 or 1911, Mr. McLean purchased lot 86 on contract, which had a partly built dwelling, and he completed and occupied it.
- Defendants John A. McLean and Christina McLean (owners of lot 86) began to erect a gasoline filling station at the rear of their lot.
- Plaintiffs, owners of adjoining and nearby land in the subdivision, sought to enjoin the construction of the gasoline station.

**Procedural History**
- Plaintiffs filed a bill to enjoin the violation of building restrictions.
- The trial court issued a decree enjoining the defendants and ordering the work done on the building to be torn down.
- Defendants appealed.

**Issue**
Can a reciprocal negative easement be enforced against a property owner whose deed does not contain express restrictions, if the owner had constructive notice of a general residential plan for the subdivision?

**Holding**
Plaintiffs win. A reciprocal negative easement can be enforced against a property owner whose deed does not contain express restrictions, if the owner had constructive notice of a general residential plan for the subdivision. Modified and affirmed.

**Rules**
- **Reciprocal Negative Easement.** If the owner of two or more lots, so situated as to bear the relation, sells one with restrictions of benefit to the land retained, the servitude becomes mutual, and, during the period of restraint, the owner of the lot or lots retained may do nothing forbidden to the owner of the lot sold; they being subject to a reciprocal negative easement.
- **Running with the Land.** A reciprocal negative easement runs with the land sold, is not personal to owners, but is operative upon the use of the land by any owner having actual or constructive notice thereof, passing its benefits and carrying its obligations to all purchasers of land subject to its affirmative or negative mandates.
- **Origin of Reciprocal Negative Easements.** Reciprocal negative easements are never retroactive, but arise, if at all, out of a benefit accorded land retained by restrictions imposed upon neighboring land sold by a common owner, and may not arise and fasten upon one lot by reason of other lot owners conforming to a general plan.
- **Notice.** A purchaser of land is bound by a reciprocal negative easement if they have actual or constructive notice of it.
- **Constructive Notice (Recording Acts).** Under recording acts, if an abstract of title shows that a lot, while in the hands of a common owner, was burdened with a reciprocal negative easement, the current owners and their predecessors are bound by constructive notice thereof.
- **Constructive Notice (Character of Neighborhood).** Where the purchaser of a lot in a residential district could not avoid noticing the strictly uniform residence character given the lots by the expensive dwellings thereon, clearly indicating a general plan, he was put to inquiry beyond merely asking his grantor whether there were restrictions.

**Reasoning**
- The subdivision was planned strictly for residence purposes, and the McLaughlins, as common owners, imposed restrictions on many lots sold to carry out this residential scheme.
- When the common owners sold lots with restrictions beneficial to the retained land, a reciprocal negative easement attached to the retained lots, including lot 86.
- This easement was created before the sale of lot 86 to defendants' predecessors in title, even though the deed for lot 86 did not explicitly mention restrictions.
- The defendants had constructive notice of this easement through two means:
    - **Recording Acts:** The abstract of title showed the common owners and the record of deeds for other lots in the plat restricted to carry out the general plan. This provided constructive notice under the recording acts.
    - **Character of the Neighborhood:** Mr. McLean, when he purchased, could not avoid noticing the uniform residential character of the street with expensive dwellings, which clearly indicated a general plan. This put him on inquiry, and a reasonable inquiry would have revealed the reciprocal negative easement.
- The fact that some lots were sold without express restrictions does not defeat the general plan, as purchasers of those lots consistently observed the restrictions.
- The court modified the trial court's decree to allow the constructed portion of the building to remain if it can be utilized for a purpose within the restrictions, rather than requiring its destruction.

**Dissent ([Judge])** / **Concurrence ([Judge])**
None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is a foundational example of how **equitable servitudes** can be implied even without express language in a deed, based on a common scheme and notice. It connects to the concept of **covenants running with the land** but applies in equity where the technical requirements for a real covenant might not be met. It highlights the importance of notice (both record and inquiry) in property law, similar to how **Howard v. Kunto** deals with the importance of possession and tacking for adverse possession.
2. What if the neighborhood had been mixed-use for a long time before the defendants purchased their lot? Would the "character of the neighborhood" still provide constructive notice of a residential-only restriction?

**Takeaway:** A reciprocal negative easement can be implied and enforced against a property owner if there was a common scheme of development and the owner had actual or constructive notice of the restrictions, even if their deed does not explicitly state them.

---

## Shelley v. Kraemer (Supreme Court of the United States, 1948)

**Facts**
- In 1911, 30 out of 39 property owners on Labadie Avenue in St. Louis signed a restrictive covenant, recorded, stating that for 50 years, the property could not be occupied by anyone not of the Caucasian race.
- At the time the agreement was signed, five parcels in the restricted district were owned by Negroes, one since 1882.
- In 1945, petitioners Shelley, who are Negroes, purchased a parcel in the restricted area from Fitzgerald via a warranty deed.
- The Shelleys had no actual knowledge of the restrictive agreement at the time of purchase.
- In 1945, respondents Kraemer, owners of other property subject to the covenant, sued to prevent the Shelleys from taking possession and to divest their title.
- In a separate case in Detroit, in 1934, Ferguson and his wife executed a similar restrictive covenant for their property, stating it could not be used or occupied by anyone except those of the Caucasian race, effective if 80% of properties in the block were similarly restricted.
- Similar agreements were executed for 80% of the lots in that block.
- In 1944, petitioners, who were Negroes, acquired title to the Detroit property and occupied it.
- In 1945, respondents, owners of property subject to the Detroit covenant, sued to force petitioners to move and enjoined them from future occupancy.

**Procedural History**
- In the Missouri case, the trial court denied relief, finding the covenant invalid because not all property owners had signed it.
- The Supreme Court of Missouri reversed, holding the agreement effective and enforceable, and directed the trial court to grant the requested relief. Petitioners appealed.
- In the Michigan case, the trial court ordered petitioners to move and enjoined future occupancy.
- The Supreme Court of Michigan affirmed, rejecting petitioners' Fourteenth Amendment claims. Petitioners appealed.
- The U.S. Supreme Court granted certiorari for both cases.

**Issue**
Can state courts enforce private restrictive covenants that prevent people of a designated race from owning or occupying real property without violating the Fourteenth Amendment's Equal Protection Clause?

**Holding**
Petitioners (Shelley and others) win. State court enforcement of racially restrictive covenants constitutes state action that denies equal protection of the laws guaranteed by the Fourteenth Amendment. Reversed.

**Rules**
- **State Action Doctrine.** The Fourteenth Amendment applies only to state action, not to purely private conduct.
- **Judicial Action as State Action.** The action of state courts and judicial officers in their official capacities is considered action of the State within the meaning of the Fourteenth Amendment.
- **Equal Protection Clause.** The Fourteenth Amendment guarantees equal protection of the laws, which includes the right to acquire, enjoy, own, and dispose of property free from state-sponsored racial discrimination.
- **Rejected: Private Agreements.** Restrictive agreements, standing alone, are not violative of the Fourteenth Amendment if their purposes are effectuated by voluntary adherence.

**Reasoning**
- The Fourteenth Amendment's prohibitions extend to all action of the State, whether by its legislative, executive, or judicial authorities.
- Judicial enforcement of a common-law rule, even if procedurally fair, can violate the Fourteenth Amendment if it results in the denial of constitutional rights.
- In these cases, the state courts actively intervened to enforce the restrictive covenants, using the full coercive power of government.
- Without judicial enforcement, petitioners would have been free to occupy the properties.
- The state courts' actions denied petitioners the enjoyment of property rights solely because of their race or color.
- The argument that denying access to courts for covenant enforcement would deny equal protection to covenant parties is without merit, as the Constitution does not grant a right to state action that denies equal protection to others.
- The power of the State to create and enforce property interests must be exercised within the boundaries of the Fourteenth Amendment.
- The primary concern of the Fourteenth Amendment's framers was to establish equality in the enjoyment of basic civil and political rights and to preserve those rights from discriminatory state action based on race or color.

**Dissent ([Judge])**
None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is crucial for understanding the **state action doctrine** and its application to private agreements in property law. It connects to the broader theme of limits on owner sovereignty, showing that even private property rights can be constrained when their enforcement implicates constitutional protections. It builds on cases like *Buchanan v. Warley*, which struck down racially discriminatory zoning ordinances, by extending the prohibition against racial discrimination to judicial enforcement of private covenants.
2. The case leaves open the question of whether a state court's enforcement of a restrictive covenant that excludes white persons would also be unconstitutional, though the Court suggests "indiscriminate imposition of inequalities" is not equal protection.

**Takeaway:** State court enforcement of private racially restrictive covenants constitutes unconstitutional state action under the Fourteenth Amendment's Equal Protection Clause.

---

## Fair Housing Council of San Fernando Valley v. Roommate.com LLC (United States Court of Appeals, Ninth Circuit, 2012)

**Facts**
- Roommate.com, LLC (Roommate) operates a website that helps people find roommates.
- Users create profiles by answering questions about their sex, sexual orientation, and whether children will live with them.
- Users also list preferences for roommate characteristics, including sex, sexual orientation, and familial status.
- Roommate matches users and provides lists based on these profiles and preferences.
- The Fair Housing Councils of San Fernando Valley and San Diego (FHCs) sued Roommate, alleging its website's practices violated the Fair Housing Act (FHA) and the California Fair Employment and Housing Act (FEHA).
- Prior to litigation, the FHCs investigated Roommate's alleged violations and started new education and outreach campaigns targeting discriminatory roommate advertising.

**Procedural History**
- The district court initially dismissed the claims, finding Roommate immune under the Communications Decency Act (CDA).
