- The Ninth Circuit reversed, holding Roommate was not immune for posting questionnaires requiring disclosure of protected characteristics, limiting searches based on preferences, and matching users based on those preferences.
- On remand, the district court held Roommate's activities violated the FHA and FEHA and issued a permanent injunction.
- The district court also awarded the FHCs attorney's fees.
- Roommate appealed the summary judgment, injunction, and attorney's fees.
- The FHCs cross-appealed the amount of attorney's fees.

**Issue**
Does the Fair Housing Act (FHA) apply to the selection of roommates in shared living situations?

**Holding**
Defendant (Roommate.com) wins. The FHA does not apply to the selection of roommates in shared living situations. Reversed.

**Rules**
- **Fair Housing Act (FHA) Scope.** The FHA prohibits discrimination in the "sale or rental of a dwelling" based on protected characteristics.
- **FHA Definition of Dwelling.** A "dwelling" is defined as "any building, structure, or portion thereof which is occupied as, or designed or intended for occupancy as, a residence by one or more families."
- **Right to Intimate Association.** The freedom to enter into and carry on certain intimate or private relationships is a fundamental element of liberty protected by the Bill of Rights.
- **Canon of Constitutional Avoidance.** Where an otherwise acceptable construction of a statute would raise serious constitutional problems, courts will construe the statute to avoid such problems unless such construction is plainly contrary to the intent of Congress.
- **California Fair Employment and Housing Act (FEHA) Definition of Housing Accommodation.** FEHA defines "housing accommodation" similarly to the FHA's definition of "dwelling."
- **Rejected: Subsequent Legislative History.** The acts of a subsequent legislature tell nothing definitive about the meaning of laws adopted by an earlier legislature.

**Reasoning**
- The FHA's reach depends on the meaning of "dwelling."
    - Interpreting "dwelling" as an independent living unit and stopping the FHA at the front door makes practical sense.
    - Applying the FHA to sub-parts of a home or apartment leads to awkward results.
    - There is no indication Congress intended to interfere with personal relationships inside the home.
    - Congress aimed to address discrimination by landlords in the sale and rental of housing, which is different from arrangements between people sharing living space.
    - Applying the FHA to roommate selection would lead to results that defy mores prevalent when the statute was passed (e.g., requiring women to accept men as roommates).
- Constitutional concerns provide strong countervailing considerations.
    - The right to intimate association protects highly personal relationships, including the roommate relationship, which involves few people, selectivity, and exclusion of non-roommates from critical aspects.
    - Choosing a roommate implicates significant privacy and safety considerations due to unfettered access to the home, which is the center of private lives.
        - Roommates observe personal habits, see each other in various stages of undress, and learn intimate details.
        - Roommates have access to physical belongings and person, making individuals vulnerable.
        - Individuals are exposed to a roommate's belongings, activities, and habits, which could be offensive, dangerous, or annoying.
        - Government regulation of roommate selection would intrude into the home and restrict the ability to choose compatible roommates, seriously invading privacy, autonomy, and security.
    - Examples illustrate these concerns: women seeking female roommates for modesty/security, orthodox Jews seeking roommates with similar beliefs/dietary restrictions.
    - HUD has previously deferred to constitutional considerations in similar roommate advertising cases.
- The FHA can reasonably be read to either include or exclude shared living arrangements.
    - Because applying the FHA to shared living units raises substantial constitutional concerns, the court must choose the construction that avoids these problems.
    - Reading "dwelling" to mean an independent housing unit is a fair interpretation consistent with congressional intent and avoids constitutional issues.
- Since the FHA does not apply to the sharing of living units, Roommate's facilitation of roommate searches is not unlawful under the FHA.
    - Roommate has standing to raise the constitutional claims of its users.
- The same constitutional concerns apply to the FEHA.
    - The FEHA's definition of "housing accommodation" is ambiguous, allowing the application of the canon of constitutional avoidance.
    - The court interprets FEHA to exclude the sharing of living units to avoid constitutional problems.
    - A 1995 FEHA amendment regarding sex-specific advertisements for shared living areas does not definitively indicate the original intent of the statute.
    - The court rejects the argument that a FEHC decision clarifies FEHA's ambiguity, as the FEHC did not consider constitutional avoidance.

**Dissent (Ikuta)**
- Disagrees with the majority's standing analysis, arguing that the FHCs did not suffer an "injury in fact" under Supreme Court precedent (Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife). The FHCs' voluntary redirection of resources to combat online discrimination was adaptive management, not an injury.
- Dissents from the majority's application of constitutional avoidance to the FEHA. FEHA's language, particularly the 1995 amendment allowing sex-specific advertisements for shared living units but not other protected characteristics, makes it unambiguous that it applies to shared living arrangements for characteristics like race or religion. The California Fair Employment and Housing Commission's decision in Larrick confirms this interpretation.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is important for understanding the limits of anti-discrimination laws in the context of private living arrangements. It connects to the concept of **owner autonomy** and the **right to exclude** within one's home, which is a core theme in property law. It also highlights the tension between public policy goals (anti-discrimination) and individual constitutional rights (intimate association, privacy). This case could be compared with cases like *Village of Euclid v. Ambler Realty Co.* or *Shelley v. Kraemer* to discuss the extent to which the government can regulate property use and private agreements.
2. The dissent raises an interesting point about the 1995 FEHA amendment. If the amendment explicitly carved out an exception for sex-based discrimination in shared living, does that imply that other forms of discrimination (e.g., race) are still covered? This creates a potential open question regarding the scope of FEHA for other protected classes in roommate situations.

**Takeaway:** Anti-discrimination laws like the FHA and FEHA do not apply to the selection of roommates in shared living situations due to constitutional concerns about the right to intimate association and privacy within the home.

---

## Village of Euclid v. Ambler Realty Co. (Supreme Court of the United States, 1926)

**Facts**
- Ambler Realty Co. owned a 68-acre tract of undeveloped land in the Village of Euclid, Ohio.
- The land was located in the path of industrial development from the nearby city of Cleveland.
- On November 13, 1922, the Village Council adopted a comprehensive zoning ordinance.
- The ordinance divided the village into six classes of use districts (U-1 to U-6), three classes of height districts (H-1 to H-3), and four classes of area districts (A-1 to A-4).
- Ambler Realty Co.'s land was classified into U-2 (two-family dwellings), U-3 (apartment houses, hotels, etc.), and U-6 (industrial uses).
- The zoning restrictions significantly reduced the market value of Ambler Realty Co.'s land.

**Procedural History**
- Ambler Realty Co. sued the Village of Euclid in federal district court, seeking an injunction to prevent the enforcement of the zoning ordinance.
- The district court held the ordinance unconstitutional and void, enjoining its enforcement.
- The Village of Euclid appealed to the Supreme Court of the United States.

**Issue**
Can a municipal zoning ordinance that restricts the use of private property, thereby reducing its value, be a valid exercise of the police power under the Fourteenth Amendment?

**Holding**
The Village of Euclid wins. A comprehensive zoning ordinance is a valid exercise of police power if its provisions are not clearly arbitrary and unreasonable, and have a substantial relation to public health, safety, morals, or general welfare. Reversed.

**Rules**
- **Police Power.** The scope of the application of constitutional guarantees must expand or contract to meet new and different conditions.
- **Police Power and Nuisance.** The question of whether the power exists to forbid a particular building or use, like the question of whether a particular thing is a nuisance, is determined by considering it in connection with the circumstances and locality, not abstractly.
- **Legislative Judgment.** If the validity of legislative classification for zoning purposes is fairly debatable, the legislative judgment must control.
- **Zoning and Public Welfare.** The exclusion of buildings devoted to business, trade, etc., from residential districts bears a rational relation to the health and safety of the community.
- **Judicial Scrutiny of Ordinances.** When an injunction is sought against an ordinance on the broad ground that its mere existence and threatened enforcement constitute an irreparable injury, and the court finds the ordinance in its general scope and dominant features valid, the court will not scrutinize its provisions sentence by sentence to find minor unconstitutional parts.

**Reasoning**
- The meaning of constitutional guarantees never varies, but their application must adapt to new conditions.
- Urban life has become more complex, requiring additional restrictions on land use. Regulations that were once considered arbitrary may now be necessary and valid.
- Zoning ordinances find their justification in the police power, asserted for the public welfare.
- The line between legitimate and illegitimate exercise of police power is not precise and varies with circumstances.
- The principle of *sic utere tuo ut alienum non laedas* (use your own property in such a manner as not to injure that of another) and the law of nuisances provide helpful analogies for understanding the scope of police power.
- The exclusion of industrial establishments from residential areas, even those that are not inherently offensive, can be justified as a reasonable margin to ensure effective enforcement and because the distinction between "good" and "bad" industries can be difficult to draw.
- The Village of Euclid, as a separate municipality, has the authority to govern itself and direct the course of industrial development within its borders, even if it diverts development from what would otherwise be its natural course.
- The exclusion of apartment houses, business houses, and retail stores from residential districts is a serious question, but many state courts have upheld such power.
- Reasons for upholding such exclusions include:
    - Promotion of health and safety for children by separating dwellings from trade/industry.
    - Suppression of disorder and facilitation of fire extinguishment and traffic regulation.
    - Easier and less expensive street construction and repair by concentrating heavy traffic.
    - Apartment houses can be parasitic in detached house sections, interfering with air circulation, sunlight, and bringing increased traffic and noise, ultimately destroying the residential character.
- These reasons are sufficiently cogent to prevent the Court from declaring the ordinance clearly arbitrary and unreasonable, or lacking a substantial relation to public health, safety, morals, or general welfare.
- The lawsuit attacked the ordinance as an entirety, claiming its mere existence caused irreparable injury. The Court will not engage in a piecemeal dissection of every provision when the general scope is valid.
- Specific complaints about particular provisions or applications should be addressed as they arise.

**Dissent (Van Devanter, McReynolds, Butler)**
- None. (The opinion states "Mr. Justice Van Devanter, Mr. Justice McReynolds and Mr. Justice Butler, dissent." but provides no reasoning.)

**NOTES:**
1. This case is foundational for understanding **zoning** as a legitimate exercise of the state's police power. It establishes the constitutionality of comprehensive land-use planning, moving beyond the traditional nuisance framework. It connects to cases like *Pennsylvania Coal Co. v. Mahon* by further defining the limits of government regulation on private property, but here, the regulation is upheld as a valid exercise of police power rather than a taking.
2. The Court explicitly states that some provisions, if attacked separately, might not withstand constitutional scrutiny. This leaves open the question of "as-applied" challenges to zoning ordinances, even if the general scheme is valid.

**Takeaway:** Zoning ordinances are generally constitutional if they bear a rational relation to public health, safety, morals, or general welfare, even if they diminish property value.

---

## Harbison v. City of Buffalo (New York Court of Appeals, 1958)

**Facts**
- In 1924, Andrew Harbison, Sr. purchased property at 35 Cumberland Avenue in Buffalo, NY.
- Shortly after, he built a 30x40 foot frame building and started a cooperage business, reconditioning barrels.
- At that time, the area was unpaved, had a city dump, and a glue factory nearby.
- By 1926, the area was zoned for residential use, though Harbison's business was an existing nonconforming use.
- From 1936 to 1956, Harbison obtained annual licenses to operate his business, which the city classified as a "junk dealer."
- In 1950, Harbison installed a special sewage system for $2,000 and a boiler for $700 at the city's insistence.
- On July 30, 1953, Buffalo amended its ordinances to require certain nonconforming uses in residential districts, including "junk yards," to cease or change to a conforming use within three years.
- On November 27, 1956, the city's director of licenses notified Harbison to discontinue his junk yard operation.
- The city refused Harbison's subsequent applications for a wholesale junk license and a drum reconditioning license, citing the residential zoning and the prohibition of outside storage of used materials.

**Procedural History**
- Petitioners (Harbison) brought an article 78 proceeding in the nature of mandamus, seeking an order directing the city to issue a license.
- The lower courts sustained the petitioners.
- The City of Buffalo appealed.

**Issue**
Can a city ordinance constitutionally require the termination of a pre-existing nonconforming use after a reasonable amortization period?

**Holding**
The City of Buffalo wins. A city ordinance requiring the termination of a pre-existing nonconforming use after a reasonable amortization period is a valid exercise of police power, provided the period is reasonable considering the specific facts. Reversed and remanded.

**Rules**
- **Vested Rights in Nonconforming Uses.** Owners of property with existing nonconforming uses or structures have a constitutional right to continue such use, but this right is not perpetual.
- **Police Power and Public Benefit.** Where the benefit to the public is greater than the detriment to the property owner, the prohibition of continuation of prior nonconforming uses may be sustained.
- **Amortization Period.** A zoning ordinance may provide for the termination of a nonconforming use after a period sufficient to allow the property owner to amortize their investment and make other plans.
- **Reasonableness Test for Amortization.** The reasonableness of an amortization period must be determined by balancing the social harm and private injury, considering factors such as the nature of the business, improvements on the land, character of the neighborhood, and detriment to the property owner.
- Rejected: **Perpetual Right to Nonconforming Use.** A property owner has a perpetual right to continue a prior nonconforming use, regardless of changes in the neighborhood or the existence of an amortization period.

**Reasoning**
- The court acknowledges the general rule that nonconforming uses are constitutionally protected and can continue.
- However, this right has never been held to continue in perpetuity.
- The policy of zoning embraces the ultimate elimination of nonconforming uses, and courts favor reasonable restrictions on them.
- Many other states and text writers support the constitutionality of ordinances that require termination after a reasonable amortization period.
- Such regulations are akin to those restricting the enlargement, extension, rebuilding, or alteration of nonconforming structures.
- The court distinguishes *Town of Somers v. Camarco*, where a one-year notice period for a sand and gravel business was found unconstitutional due to substantial investment and an unreasonably short termination period.
- To hold that a nonconforming use vests an owner with a perpetual right would be an unwarranted restriction on the legislature in dealing with urban development.
- The court cannot determine the reasonableness of the three-year period without more facts, as the lower courts did not consider this question.
- Material issues of fact remain regarding the value and condition of improvements, relocation costs, and potential for continued business operation without outside storage.

**Dissent (Van Voobhis, J.)**
- The decision marks the beginning of the end of constitutional protection for pre-existing nonconforming uses.
- The traditional rule is that nonconforming uses are constitutionally protected and will be permitted to continue.
- The city's action is confiscatory, especially given Harbison's recent investment at the city's insistence.
- Zoning relates to future development, not retroactive reconstruction; confiscation of property for public use requires just compensation, similar to slum clearance.
- The "amortization" theory is a "catch phrase" without real meaning, as the periods vary widely and do not relate to the useful life of structures.
- This theory encourages owners to let nonconforming uses decay, leading to slums.
- The decision creates confusion, instability, and an avalanche of litigation by departing from established precedent.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is significant for introducing and validating the concept of "amortization" as a tool for municipalities to eliminate nonconforming uses, which was a departure from the traditional "vested rights" approach. It connects to *Village of Euclid v. Ambler Realty Co.* by showing how zoning regulations, initially upheld for prospective application, can be extended to affect existing property rights. It also contrasts with cases like *Pennsylvania Coal Co. v. Mahon* by suggesting that some diminution of property value due to regulation is permissible without compensation, provided there's a reasonable period for the owner to recoup their investment.
2. The dissent raises a crucial point about the potential for "amortization" to be applied arbitrarily or to encourage neglect of property. A hypo could be: What if the city had required a 10-year amortization period for Harbison's business, and he had made significant improvements in year 9? Would that still be considered reasonable?

**Takeaway:** While nonconforming uses are generally protected, municipalities can require their termination after a reasonable amortization period, balancing public benefit against private injury.

---

## Southern Burlington County NAACP v. Township of Mount Laurel (Supreme Court of New Jersey, 1975)

**Facts**
- In 1950, Mount Laurel Township was a rural agricultural area with a population of 2817.
- After 1950, the township experienced significant population growth, reaching 5249 by 1960 and 11,221 by 1970.
- The township adopted a general zoning ordinance in 1964, which was similar to earlier, less restrictive enactments.
- This ordinance zoned 29.2% of the land for industry, 1.2% for retail business, and the remainder for residential use.
- The residential zones (R-1, R-1D, R-2, R-3) permitted only single-family, detached dwellings.
- The ordinance required minimum lot sizes of 9,375 sq ft (R-1), 11,000 sq ft (R-2), and 20,000 sq ft (R-3), and minimum dwelling floor areas of 1,100 sq ft (one-story) or 1,300 sq ft (one-and-a-half stories or higher).
- The R-1D zone, a "cluster" zone created in 1968, reduced minimum lot size to 10,000 sq ft but maintained similar dwelling floor area requirements and an overall density limit.
- The township approved four Planned Unit Development (PUD) projects, which included multi-family housing but were designed for middle and upper-income residents, with restrictions on the number of bedrooms and school-age children.
- In 1972, the township created an R-4 zone for Planned Adult Retirement Communities (PARC), with extensive development requirements making it unsuitable for low and moderate-income retirees and restrictions on age and number of residents.
- The township's 1969 Master Plan Report recognized the need for positive action regarding substandard housing in the Springville section (R-3 zone), but official policy was to wait for dilapidation and then forbid occupancy.
- A 1968 private non-profit effort to build subsidized multi-family housing in Springville was thwarted by municipal requirements for single-family detached dwellings on 20,000 sq ft lots.
- The township's land use regulations were intended to result in economic discrimination and exclusion of substantial segments of the area population to keep down local property taxes, particularly school costs.
- Plaintiffs, including minority group poor, challenged the township's land use regulations.

**Procedural History**
- The trial court found Mount Laurel's zoning ordinance invalid and ordered the township to study housing needs and present a plan for affirmative public action.
- The township appealed to the Appellate Division.
- Plaintiffs cross-appealed, arguing the plan should consider regional housing needs beyond those connected to the township.
- The Supreme Court of New Jersey certified the appeals on its own motion.

**Issue**
Can a developing municipality use land use regulations to make it physically and economically impossible to provide low and moderate-income housing, thereby excluding people based on their income and resources?

**Holding**
Plaintiff (NAACP) wins. A developing municipality cannot use land use regulations to prevent the opportunity for low and moderate-income housing. Modified and Affirmed.

**Rules**
- **Police Power and General Welfare.** All police power enactments, including zoning regulations, must conform to state constitutional requirements of substantive due process and equal protection, promoting public health, safety, morals, or the general welfare.
- **Regional General Welfare.** When land use regulation has a substantial external impact, the general welfare of the state's citizens beyond the borders of the particular municipality cannot be disregarded and must be recognized and served.
- **Housing as Basic Need.** Proper provision for adequate housing for all categories of people is an absolute essential in promoting the general welfare required in all local land use regulation.
- **Presumptive Obligation for Developing Municipalities.** A developing municipality has a presumptive obligation to plan and provide, by its land use regulations, the reasonable opportunity for an appropriate variety and choice of housing, including low and moderate-cost housing, to meet the needs, desires, and resources of all categories of people who may desire to live within its boundaries.
- **Negative Obligation.** A developing municipality may not adopt regulations or policies which thwart or preclude the opportunity for low and moderate-income housing.
- **Affirmative Obligation.** A developing municipality must affirmatively afford the opportunity for low and moderate-income housing, at least to the extent of the municipality's fair share of the present and prospective regional need.
- **Burden of Proof (Exclusionary Zoning).** When a developing municipality's land use regulations have not made realistically possible a variety and choice of housing, including adequate provision for low and moderate-income housing, or have expressly prescribed requirements or restrictions that preclude or substantially hinder it, a facial showing of violation of substantive due process or equal protection under the state constitution is made, and the burden shifts to the municipality to establish a valid basis for its action or non-action.
- **Fiscal Reasons for Exclusion (Rejected).** No municipality may exclude or limit categories of housing for the purpose of keeping down local taxes.
- **Ecological/Environmental Reasons for Exclusion (Limited).** While land use regulations should consider ecological or environmental factors, these cannot be used as a pretext to limit housing to single-family dwellings on large lots if the area is amenable to utility installations.

**Reasoning**
- Mount Laurel's zoning ordinance permits only single-family detached dwellings, prohibiting multi-family housing, townhouses, and mobile homes, which are essential for low and moderate-income families.
- The PUDs allowed multi-family housing but were designed for affluent residents, with restrictions on bedroom numbers and school-age children, clearly intended to limit families with children and reduce educational costs.
- The minimum lot area, lot frontage, and building size requirements in Mount Laurel's residential zones are so restrictive that they preclude single-family housing for moderate-income families, significantly increasing housing costs.
- The township zoned almost 30% of its area for industrial uses, far exceeding the realistic potential for industrial development, thereby removing an unreasonable amount of land from possible residential development, seemingly for local fiscal reasons.
- The court finds that Mount Laurel's zoning ordinance is presumptively contrary to the general welfare and outside the intended scope of the zoning power because it makes it impossible to provide low and moderate-income housing.
- The court rejects the municipality's primary justification of fiscal benefits, stating that relief from the tax system must come from other branches of government, not by restricting housing types through zoning.
- The court also rejects environmental concerns as a primary justification for large-lot zoning in this case, noting that the flat land is amenable to utility installations.
- The court emphasizes that the "general welfare" in land use regulation extends beyond municipal boundaries to the regional need for housing, especially for low and moderate-income families.
- The court modifies the trial court's remedy, invalidating the ordinance only in the specified particulars and giving the township 90 days to amend it, rather than nullifying the entire ordinance.
- The court clarifies that its role is not to build housing but to ensure the municipality provides the opportunity through appropriate land use regulations.

**Dissent (None.)**

**Concurrence (Mountain, J.)**
- Agrees with the conclusions but would base the decision on an interpretation of the statutory term "general welfare" in N.J.S.A. 40:55-32, rather than on state constitutional grounds.

**Concurrence (Pashman, J.)**
- Joins the majority but believes the court should go farther and faster, extending the principles to all municipalities in the state (developing, developed, and rural) and establishing a policy of active judicial enforcement of both negative and affirmative obligations.
- Highlights the widespread nature of exclusionary zoning, its motivations (fiscal concerns and prejudice), and its various forms (minimum house/lot size, prohibition of multi-family housing, bedroom restrictions, prohibition of mobile homes, overzoning for nonresidential uses).
- Emphasizes the acute national housing shortage, the burden on employees due to job migration to suburbs, and the risk of prolonged judicial inaction allowing exclusionary practices to become permanent.
- Details the affirmative obligations of developing municipalities, including considering regional housing needs in all planning activities, cooperating in regional planning efforts, and encouraging private and public efforts to provide low and moderate-income housing.
- Notes limitations on affirmative obligations, such as not requiring a municipality to assume more than its fair share of regional needs and allowing control over the pace and sequence of development, provided regulations are reasonable and least exclusionary.
- Argues that developed suburban municipalities also have negative and affirmative obligations, especially when land becomes available for development or redevelopment.
- States that even rural municipalities should not establish inherently exclusionary land use controls that bear no substantial relationship to legitimate zoning purposes.
- Proposes a four-step judicial process for enforcement: identify the relevant region, determine regional housing needs, allocate needs among municipalities, and shape a suitable remedial order.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is foundational for understanding the **Mount Laurel doctrine** and the concept of **exclusionary zoning**. It significantly expands the interpretation of the "general welfare" clause in zoning to include regional housing needs, especially for low and moderate-income individuals. It connects to *Village of Euclid v. Ambler Realty Co.* by acknowledging the evolving nature of zoning law and the need for judicial intervention when conditions change, as warned in *Pierro v. Baxendale*. It also contrasts with cases like *Lionshead Lake, Inc. v. Township of Wayne* and *Fischer v. Township of Bedminster* which previously sanctioned broad restrictive municipal decisions.
2. A tension exists between the court's reluctance to act as a "super planning board" and the need for judicial oversight to ensure compliance. How far should courts go in dictating specific zoning changes or housing types to municipalities?

**Takeaway:** Developing municipalities have a constitutional obligation under state law to provide a realistic opportunity for a fair share of regional low and moderate-income housing, and cannot use zoning to exclude such housing for fiscal reasons.

---

## Charles River Bridge v. Warren Bridge (Supreme Court of the United States, 1837)

**Facts**
- In 1650, the Massachusetts legislature granted Harvard College the right to operate a ferry between Charlestown and Boston.
- Harvard College operated this ferry through lessees and received profits until 1785.
- In 1785, the Massachusetts legislature incorporated the Proprietors of the Charles River Bridge to build a bridge where the ferry was located.
- The Charles River Bridge company was granted tolls for 40 years (later extended to 70 years) and was required to pay Harvard College 200 pounds annually.
- The Charles River Bridge was built and operated, with the company paying Harvard College and maintaining the bridge.
- In 1828, the Massachusetts legislature incorporated the Proprietors of the Warren Bridge to build another bridge over the Charles River, very close to the Charles River Bridge.
- The Warren Bridge was allowed to take tolls until its construction costs were reimbursed, after which it would become free.
- The Warren Bridge was built and eventually became free, diverting traffic and tolls from the Charles River Bridge.

**Procedural History**
- The Proprietors of the Charles River Bridge filed a bill in the Supreme Judicial Court of Massachusetts against the Proprietors of the Warren Bridge, seeking an injunction to prevent the erection of the bridge and for general relief.
- The Charles River Bridge company argued that the act authorizing the Warren Bridge impaired the obligation of a contract, violating the U.S. Constitution.
- The Supreme Judicial Court of Massachusetts dismissed the bill.
- The Charles River Bridge company appealed to the Supreme Court of the United States by writ of error.
- The Supreme Court of the United States affirmed the judgment of the Massachusetts court.

**Issue**
Can a state legislature authorize the construction of a new public work that competes with and diminishes the value of an existing chartered public work, without violating the Contract Clause of the U.S. Constitution, when the original charter does not explicitly grant an exclusive privilege?

**Holding**
Defendant (Warren Bridge) wins. A state legislature can authorize the construction of a new public work that competes with an existing chartered public work, even if it diminishes the value of the existing work, if the original charter does not explicitly grant an exclusive privilege. Affirmed.

**Rules**
- **Strict Construction of Public Grants.** Public grants, especially those to private corporations, are to be construed strictly against the grantee and in favor of the public. Nothing passes by implication in such grants.
- **Contract Clause.** A state law may be retrospective and divest vested rights without violating the U.S. Constitution, unless it also impairs the obligation of a contract.
- **State Sovereignty.** The government's power to promote the happiness and prosperity of the community, including providing necessary channels of communication, should not be presumed to be surrendered or diminished by implication.
- Rejected: **Implied Exclusive Privilege.** An exclusive privilege or a covenant not to authorize competition will not be implied in a public grant unless explicitly stated.

**Reasoning**
- The Court must interpret the charter of the Charles River Bridge strictly, as it is a public grant to a private corporation concerning public interest.
- Nothing in the Charles River Bridge charter explicitly granted an exclusive privilege over the Charles River or prohibited the state from authorizing another bridge.
- To imply such an exclusive right would diminish the state's power to provide for the public good and would be contrary to the public interest in a growing and enterprising country.
- The object of government is to promote community happiness and prosperity, which often requires new infrastructure and improvements.
- If implied contracts were recognized in such cases, it would jeopardize investments in new technologies (like railroads) and hinder progress.
- The ferry right of Harvard College was extinguished by the building of the Charles River Bridge, with compensation provided, and the bridge charter is a separate grant.
- The argument that the state cannot diminish the value of a granted franchise without impairing a contract is rejected; the power of the state, like the taxing power, is not presumed to be abandoned.
- The Court's role is to interpret the contract as written, not to create implied terms that were not the deliberate purpose of the state.

**Dissent (Story, J.)**
- The grant of a franchise, like a ferry or bridge, inherently includes an exclusive right to prevent ruinous competition within a reasonable distance, as established by common law.
- The state's grant to the Charles River Bridge company was a contract, and the common law understanding of such grants at the time implied an exclusive right to protect the franchise's value.
- The act incorporating the Warren Bridge effectively destroyed the value of the Charles River Bridge franchise, thus impairing the obligation of the contract.
- The state should not be allowed to take private property (the franchise) for public use without compensation, even if it is for public convenience.
- The strict construction rule should not be applied to defeat the clear intent and beneficial interest conveyed by a public grant, especially when there was a valuable consideration.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is crucial for understanding the **Contract Clause** and the **strict construction of public grants**. It contrasts with cases like *Fletcher v. Peck* and *Dartmouth College v. Woodward*, where the Court protected contracts against state impairment, by emphasizing the public interest and limiting implied rights in corporate charters. It also touches on the concept of **eminent domain** and the need for compensation, though the majority found no contract impairment here.
2. The tension between protecting private property rights (especially those derived from state charters) and allowing states to promote public welfare and economic development is a key theme. How might the outcome have differed if the Charles River Bridge charter had explicitly included an exclusivity clause?

**Takeaway:** Public grants are to be construed strictly against the grantee, and exclusive privileges will not be implied, prioritizing the state's power to promote public welfare over the potential for private monopolies.

---

## Kelo v. City of New London (Supreme Court of the United States, 2005)

**Facts**
- In 1990, New London, Connecticut, was designated a "distressed municipality" due to economic decline.
- In 1996, a naval facility employing over 1,500 people closed in the Fort Trumbull area of New London.
- In 1998, the city's unemployment rate was nearly double the state average, and its population was at its lowest since 1920.
- The New London Development Corporation (NLDC), a private nonprofit entity, was reactivated to plan economic development for the city.
- The State of Connecticut authorized bond issues to support NLDC's planning and the creation of a state park.
- Pharmaceutical company Pfizer Inc. announced in February 1998 that it would build a research facility adjacent to Fort Trumbull.
- The NLDC developed an integrated development plan for 90 acres of the Fort Trumbull area, intending to capitalize on Pfizer's arrival, create jobs, increase tax revenue, and revitalize the area.
- The plan included parcels for a waterfront hotel, residences, a Coast Guard Museum, research and office space, parking, and retail.
- In January 2000, the New London City Council approved the development plan and designated the NLDC as its development agent, authorizing it to acquire property by purchase or eminent domain.
- The NLDC purchased most of the real estate but failed to negotiate purchases with the petitioners.
- In November 2000, the NLDC initiated condemnation proceedings against the petitioners' properties.
- Petitioners owned 15 properties in the Fort Trumbull area, some occupied by owners and some investment properties, none of which were blighted or in poor condition.

**Procedural History**
- Petitioners sued the City of New London in the New London Superior Court, claiming the takings violated the Fifth Amendment's "public use" restriction.
- The Superior Court granted a permanent restraining order prohibiting the taking of properties in Parcel 4A (park or marina support) but denied relief for properties in Parcel 3 (office space).
- Both sides appealed to the Supreme Court of Connecticut.
- The Supreme Court of Connecticut held that all proposed takings were valid, finding that economic development qualified as a valid public use under both federal and state constitutions.
- The Supreme Court of Connecticut reversed the trial court's decision regarding Parcel 4A, finding its intended use sufficiently definite.
- Petitioners sought certiorari from the U.S. Supreme Court.

**Issue**
Can a city take private property for the purpose of economic development, transferring it to private parties, if the taking is part of a comprehensive plan to revitalize an economically distressed area and is expected to generate public benefits like jobs and increased tax revenue?

**Holding**
Respondent (City of New London) wins. A city's taking of private property for the purpose of economic development, as part of a comprehensive plan to revitalize an economically distressed area, satisfies the "public use" requirement of the Fifth Amendment, even if the property is transferred to private parties. Affirmed.

**Rules**
- **Fifth Amendment Takings Clause.** Private property shall not be taken for public use, without just compensation.
- **Public Use Doctrine (Broad Interpretation).** The concept of "public use" in the Fifth Amendment is broadly interpreted to mean "public purpose," reflecting deference to legislative judgments in this field.
- **Deference to Legislative Judgment.** Courts should afford legislatures broad latitude in determining what public needs justify the use of the takings power.
- **Public Purpose Justification.** A taking is for a public use if it serves a public purpose, even if the property is subsequently transferred to private parties.
- **Rejected: Literal "Use by the Public" Test.** The Court has long rejected any literal requirement that condemned property be put into use for the general public.
- **Rejected: "Purely Private Taking" Prohibition.** A taking for the sole purpose of transferring property from one private party to another, to confer a private benefit, is forbidden.
- **Rejected: Pretextual Public Purpose.** A taking under the mere pretext of a public purpose, when its actual purpose is to bestow a private benefit, is forbidden.
- **Rejected: Heightened Scrutiny for Economic Development Takings.** There is no basis for a new bright-line rule that economic development does not qualify as a public use, nor for requiring "reasonable certainty" that expected public benefits will accrue.

**Reasoning**
- The Court's jurisprudence has consistently defined "public use" broadly as "public purpose," reflecting deference to legislative judgments.
- The City's development plan was a "carefully considered" plan to revitalize an economically distressed city, projected to create jobs, increase tax revenue, and improve the area.
- The Court has previously upheld takings where the property was transferred to private parties for public purposes, such as eliminating blight (Berman v. Parker) or breaking up a land oligopoly (Hawaii Housing Authority v. Midkiff).
- The fact that private parties will benefit from the development does not negate the public character of the taking, as the government's pursuit of a public purpose often benefits private parties.
- The Court rejected petitioners' argument for a bright-line rule against economic development takings, stating that promoting economic development is a traditional government function and there's no principled way to distinguish it from other recognized public purposes.
- The Court also rejected the argument for requiring "reasonable certainty" of public benefits, as this would involve courts in empirical debates over the wisdom of legislative initiatives, which is contrary to precedent.
- The Court emphasized that its role is not to second-guess the City's judgments about the efficacy of its plan or the specific lands needed, but only to determine if the takings are for a "public use."
- States are free to impose stricter "public use" requirements than the federal baseline.

**Concurrence (Justice Kennedy)**
- Agrees with the Court's opinion but emphasizes that transfers intended to confer benefits on particular, favored private entities, with only incidental or pretextual public benefits, are forbidden.
- A court should seriously review plausible accusations of impermissible favoritism, though with a presumption of government reasonableness and public purpose.
- The trial court's extensive inquiry in this case found no evidence of illicit favoritism, and the plan was a comprehensive effort to address a serious citywide depression with substantial public benefits.
- A more stringent standard of review might be appropriate for narrowly drawn categories of takings where the risk of undetected impermissible favoritism is acute, but this case does not present such circumstances.

**Dissent (Justice O’Connor)**
- Argues that the Court abandons a basic limitation on government power, making all private property vulnerable to being taken for transfer to another private owner if it might be "upgraded" for more beneficial use.
- Contends that equating incidental public benefits from private use with "public use" effectively deletes the words "for public use" from the Fifth Amendment.
- Distinguishes Berman and Midkiff by arguing that those cases involved the elimination of existing harmful property uses (blight, land oligopoly), whereas Kelo's homes were well-maintained and not harmful.
- Believes the "public purpose" interpretation of "public use" is theoretically flawed and leads to an unprincipled application, as nearly any lawful use of private property generates some incidental public benefit.
- Expresses concern that the decision will disproportionately harm politically powerless communities, allowing property to be transferred from those with fewer resources to those with more.
- Criticizes the Court's reliance on the idea that states can impose stricter limits, calling it an abdication of federal responsibility to enforce the Constitution.

**Dissent (Justice Thomas)**
- Argues that the Court replaces the "Public Use" Clause with a "Public Purpose" Clause, effectively erasing it from the Constitution.
- Believes the original meaning of "public use" requires that the government or the public have a legal right to use the property, not merely that the taking serves any public purpose.
- Points to the common law distinction between nuisance law (which requires no compensation) and eminent domain (which requires compensation) to support a narrower reading of "public use."
- Criticizes the Court's precedents (Bradley, Clark, Strickley) for adopting the "public purpose" test with little analysis of original meaning and for affording undue deference to legislative judgments.
- Argues that the "public purpose" standard is not susceptible to principled application and that the "actual use" test is easier to administer.
- Predicts that the decision will have harmful consequences, exacerbating the displacement of poor and minority communities for the benefit of powerful private interests.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is a landmark decision regarding the **Takings Clause** of the Fifth Amendment, specifically the "public use" requirement. It significantly broadens the interpretation of "public use" to include economic development, even when property is transferred to private entities. It connects to **Pennsylvania Coal Co. v. Mahon** and **Penn Central Transportation Co. v. New York City** by further defining the limits of government power over private property, but in the context of eminent domain rather than regulatory takings. It also contrasts with cases like **Jacque v. Steenberg Homes** and **Intel Corp. v. Hamidi** which emphasize the right to exclude, as here the government is compelling exclusion for a broader public purpose.
2. A key tension is between the majority's broad, deferential interpretation of "public use" and the dissents' call for a narrower, originalist interpretation. A hypo could be: If a city wants to condemn a successful, family-owned restaurant to build a larger, chain restaurant that promises more jobs and tax revenue, would this be a valid public use under Kelo?

**Takeaway:** The Supreme Court held that economic development qualifies as a "public use" under the Fifth Amendment's Takings Clause, allowing governments to condemn private property for transfer to other private entities as part of a comprehensive revitalization plan.

---

## United States v. Miller (Supreme Court of the United States, 1943)

**Facts**
- For many years, a proposal for state reclamation works in California had been before the people of the state.
- In 1932, the people of California voted approval and authorization of the project.
- The United States subsequently adopted the project as a federal project.
- On April 6, 1934, the Chief of Engineers of the Army recommended that the Government contribute twelve million dollars towards the project.
- In 1935, Congress authorized the appropriation.
- On December 22, 1935, the President approved construction of the entire improvement.
- In 1936 and 1937, Congress appropriated funds for the project.
- In August 1937, the project was again authorized by Congress.
- In his report for the fiscal year ending June 30, 1937, the Secretary of the Interior stated that Shasta, California, had been selected for the site of the Sacramento River dam, which involved relocating about thirty miles of railroad line.
- Alternate routes for the railroad relocation were surveyed by March 1936 and staked.
- Prior to the project's authorization, the area including respondents' tracts was largely uncleared brush land.
- In 1936 and 1937, certain parcels were purchased with the intention of subdividing them, and in 1937, subdivisions were plotted, leading to the settlement of Boomtown, where respondents' lands are located.
- By December 1938, Boomtown had been built up for business and residential purposes.
- On December 14, 1938, the United States filed a complaint in eminent domain against the respondents and others whose lands were needed for the railroad relocation.
- On the same day, the Government filed a declaration of taking, estimating just compensation for a tract belonging to three respondents at $2,550 and depositing that sum in court.
- The court directed the Clerk to pay each of these three owners one-third of the deposit ($850) on account of compensation.
- At trial, the government objected to respondents' witnesses testifying to market value as of December 14, 1938, without excluding any increment of value due to the project's authorization after August 26, 1937.
- The trial court sustained the objection and required witnesses to exclude value increments after August 26, 1937, due to the project.
- The jury rendered verdicts, and the three respondents who had received $850 each were awarded less than the total paid them.
- The trial court entered judgment for the United States for the amounts these three respondents had received in excess of the verdicts, with interest.
- The three respondents moved to set aside the money judgments against them, which was overruled.

**Procedural History**
- The District Court entered judgment for the United States, including money judgments against three respondents for overpayments.
- All respondents appealed to the Circuit Court of Appeals.
- The Circuit Court of Appeals reversed the judgment, holding that the trial judge erred in his rulings and charge regarding valuation, and that the District Court lacked jurisdiction to award the United States a judgment for overpaid amounts.
- The United States appealed to the Supreme Court.

**Issue**
- When the government condemns land for a public project, should the "just compensation" include any increase in the land's market value that resulted from the government's own project?
- Can a district court order repayment of estimated compensation paid to landowners that exceeds the final jury award in an eminent domain case?

**Holding**
- The United States wins. Just compensation for condemned land should not include any increase in value attributable to the government's project if the land was probably within the scope of the project from the time the government committed to it. A district court has jurisdiction to order repayment of estimated compensation that exceeds the final award. Reversed.

**Rules**
- **Just Compensation.** The Fifth Amendment requires "just compensation" for private property taken for public use, meaning the full and perfect equivalent in money of the property taken, putting the owner in as good a pecuniary position as if the property had not been taken.
- **Market Value.** Courts use market value as a practical standard for just compensation, defined as what a willing buyer would pay in cash to a willing seller.
- **Exclusion of Special Value to Condemnor.** The market value of property taken must exclude its special value to the condemnor, as distinguished from others who may or may not possess the power to condemn.
- **Exclusion of Project-Enhanced Value.** If lands were probably within the scope of a public project from the time the government was committed to it, any increase in value arising from the known fact that the lands probably would be condemned should not be included in just compensation.
- **Jurisdiction over Deposits.** A district court has jurisdiction over funds deposited in its chancery in an eminent domain proceeding and can order repayment of any excess amounts paid to owners over the final award.

**Reasoning**
- The purpose of just compensation is to indemnify the owner for their loss, not to allow them to gain from the taker's need.
- The market value standard aims to provide a practical measure, but it must be applied fairly in condemnation cases.
- Elements that affect market value but must be eliminated in condemnation include special adaptability to the owner's use and special value to the condemnor.
- Allowing an owner to benefit from an increase in value due to the government's project, when their land was likely to be taken for that project, would be allowing them to gain by speculating on the government's activities.
- The project, from its final authorization in August 1937, included the railroad relocation, and one probable route was over the respondents' lands.
- Therefore, it was proper to instruct the jury to exclude any value increase after August 1937 due to the likelihood of their property being taken.
- The federal statutes governing condemnation proceedings require federal courts to adopt state procedural forms and methods, but they do not affect substantive rights like the measure of compensation, which is grounded in the U.S. Constitution.
- The Act of February 26, 1931, which allows for declarations of taking and deposits of estimated compensation, intends for these payments to be provisional, "on account of" compensation, not a final settlement.
- To hold otherwise would defeat the statute's policy, encourage underestimation by federal officials, and deny owners immediate cash.
- The District Court, having the funds in its chancery, retained jurisdiction to manage their disbursement and repayment as justice required, even after releasing funds to respondents.
- Respondents had due process because they had full opportunity to argue against the restitution judgment through their motions to set it aside.

**Dissent ([Judge])**
- None.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is crucial for understanding the limits of "just compensation" under the Fifth Amendment, particularly how courts determine market value in eminent domain proceedings. It connects to the broader theme of government power over private property and the balance between public good and individual rights, similar to cases like *Kelo v. City of New London* regarding the definition of "public use" and *Pennsylvania Coal Co. v. Mahon* regarding regulatory takings. It also highlights the court's role in defining the scope of property rights when they conflict with public projects.
2. What if the government's project was not clearly defined at the outset, and the "scope of the project" was ambiguous? How would a court determine if the land was "probably within the scope of the project"?

**Takeaway:** Just compensation in eminent domain excludes value increases caused by the government's project if the property was likely to be taken for that project.

---

## Pennsylvania Coal Co. v. Mahon (Supreme Court of the United States, 1922)

**Facts**
- In 1878, Pennsylvania Coal Company (PCC) conveyed the surface rights of a property to Mahon's predecessor in title.
- The deed expressly reserved PCC's right to remove all coal beneath the surface, and the grantee waived all claims for damages from such mining.
- Mahon acquired the surface rights to the property.
- In 1921, Pennsylvania enacted the Kohler Act, which made it unlawful to mine anthracite coal in a way that caused subsidence of, among other things, any structure used as a human habitation.
- PCC intended to mine under Mahon's property, which would cause subsidence of Mahon's house.
- PCC gave timely notice of its intent to mine.

**Procedural History**
- Mahon sued PCC in equity to enjoin mining that would cause subsidence.
- The Court of Common Pleas denied the injunction, finding the Kohler Act unconstitutional if applied to this case.
- The Supreme Court of Pennsylvania reversed, holding the statute was a legitimate exercise of police power.
- PCC appealed to the U.S. Supreme Court.

**Issue**
- Can a state law prohibit coal mining that causes surface subsidence, even when the coal company previously reserved the right to mine and the surface owner waived damages, without compensating the coal company?

**Holding**
- Pennsylvania Coal Company wins. The Kohler Act, as applied, is an unconstitutional taking of property without compensation. Reversed.

**Rules**
- **Police Power Limits.** While property may be regulated to a certain extent under the police power, if regulation goes too far, it will be recognized as a taking requiring compensation.
- **Taking.** What makes the right to mine coal valuable is that it can be exercised with profit; to make it commercially impracticable to mine certain coal has very nearly the same effect for constitutional purposes as appropriating or destroying it.
- **Public Interest.** A strong public desire to improve the public condition is not enough to warrant achieving the desire by a shorter cut than the constitutional way of paying for the change.
- **Reciprocity of Advantage.** (Implicitly applied) Regulations that secure an "average reciprocity of advantage" among affected parties are more likely to be upheld.

**Reasoning**
- The Kohler Act destroys previously existing property and contract rights.
- While government can diminish property values to some extent without compensation, there are limits to this implied limitation on property rights.
    - The extent of diminution is a key factor; when it reaches a certain magnitude, it becomes a taking.
- This case involves a single private house, and the public interest in preventing damage to it is not sufficient to warrant such an extensive destruction of PCC's rights.
    - The damage is not a public nuisance because it is not common or public.
    - The statute's limited scope (not applying when the coal owner also owns the surface) suggests a limited public interest.
    - Protection of personal safety could be provided by notice, which PCC gave.
- The extent of the taking is great, abolishing a valuable estate in land (the right to mine coal) and a binding contract.
- The Act cannot be sustained as an exercise of police power where the right to mine coal has been reserved, because it makes mining commercially impracticable, which is equivalent to appropriating or destroying the coal.
- The public's rights in a street are only those it has paid for; if surface rights were acquired without support rights, the state cannot supply the latter without compensation.
- The Rent Cases (e.g., *Block v. Hirsh*) are distinguishable because they involved temporary emergencies and provided for reasonable compensation.

**Dissent (Brandeis)**
- The Kohler Act is a legitimate exercise of police power to prevent a noxious use of property.
- Restriction imposed to protect public health, safety, or morals from danger is not a taking; the state merely prevents a use that interferes with paramount public rights.
- The purpose of the restriction is public, even if private persons incidentally benefit.
- Keeping coal in place is an appropriate means to prevent subsidence, and it does not become inappropriate just because it deprives the owner of the only profitable use.
- The value of the restricted coal should be compared to the value of the whole property, not just the coal itself. Dividing property interests (surface and subsoil) does not increase the owner's rights against the public.
- If public safety is imperiled, neither grant nor contract can prevail against the police power.
- Reciprocity of advantage is not necessary when the police power is exercised to protect the public from detriment and danger, as opposed to conferring benefits.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is foundational for understanding the **Takings Clause** of the Fifth Amendment (applied to states via the Fourteenth Amendment). It establishes the concept of a "regulatory taking," where a government regulation goes "too far" and effectively takes private property, even without formally condemning it. This contrasts with earlier understandings where a physical invasion or direct appropriation was generally required for a taking. It sets up the tension between the state's police power and private property rights, a theme explored in cases like *Village of Euclid v. Ambler Realty Co.* and *Penn Central Transportation Co. v. New York City*.
2. The "too far" test is notoriously vague. What constitutes "too far"? Is it a percentage of value lost, or the destruction of a "distinct investment-backed expectation"? The majority focuses on the diminution of value of the *mining rights* specifically, while the dissent argues for considering the value of the *entire property*. This difference in the "denominator" for the taking fraction is a persistent issue in takings jurisprudence.

**Takeaway:** A government regulation that goes "too far" in diminishing the value or use of private property will be considered a taking requiring just compensation, even if it does not involve a physical appropriation.

---

## Penn Central Transportation Co. v. New York City (Supreme Court of the United States, 1978)

**Facts**
- Over the past 50 years, states and municipalities enacted laws to preserve historic landmarks and districts.
- In 1965, New York City adopted its Landmarks Preservation Law to protect historic landmarks and neighborhoods.
- The Landmarks Preservation Commission (Commission) was established to identify and designate landmarks.
- The law requires landmark owners to keep exterior features in good repair and obtain Commission approval for alterations or new construction.
- The law also provides for transferable development rights (TDRs) for landmark owners who have not developed their property to the full extent permitted by zoning laws.
- In 1967, the Commission designated Grand Central Terminal (Terminal), owned by Penn Central Transportation Co. (Penn Central), as a landmark.
- Penn Central did not seek judicial review of the designation.
- In 1968, Penn Central leased the airspace above the Terminal to UGP Properties, Inc. (UGP) to construct a multistory office building.
- UGP and Penn Central submitted two plans (Breuer I and Breuer II Revised) to the Commission for approval to construct an office building atop the Terminal.
- The Commission denied a certificate of no exterior effect and subsequently denied a certificate of appropriateness for both plans.
- Penn Central did not seek judicial review of the denial of either certificate.
- Penn Central filed suit in New York Supreme Court, claiming the Landmarks Preservation Law constituted a "taking" without just compensation and deprived them of property without due process.

**Procedural History**
- The trial court granted injunctive and declaratory relief to Penn Central.
- The New York Supreme Court, Appellate Division, reversed, holding that the restrictions were necessary for a legitimate public purpose and Penn Central failed to prove deprivation of all reasonable beneficial use.
- The New York Court of Appeals affirmed, finding no "taking" because the law did not transfer control of the property and Penn Central failed to show denial of a reasonable return.
- Penn Central appealed to the U.S. Supreme Court.

**Issue**
Does New York City's Landmarks Preservation Law, by restricting the development of Grand Central Terminal, constitute a "taking" of private property for public use without just compensation in violation of the Fifth and Fourteenth Amendments?

**Holding**
Defendant (New York City) wins. The application of New York City's Landmarks Preservation Law to Grand Central Terminal does not constitute a "taking" of appellants' property. Affirmed.

**Rules**
- **Takings Clause (Fifth Amendment).** The Fifth Amendment prohibits the government from taking private property for public use without just compensation, applicable to states via the Fourteenth Amendment.
- **Ad Hoc Factual Inquiries.** Whether a particular restriction constitutes a "taking" depends largely on the specific circumstances of the case, involving essentially ad hoc, factual inquiries.
- **Factors for a Taking.** Key factors in determining if a taking has occurred include:
    - The economic impact of the regulation on the claimant.
    - The extent to which the regulation has interfered with distinct investment-backed expectations.
    - The character of the governmental action.
- **Physical Invasion vs. Public Program.** A "taking" is more readily found when governmental interference is a physical invasion than when it arises from a public program adjusting economic benefits and burdens for the common good.
- **Diminution in Value.** Diminution in property value, standing alone, does not establish a "taking" if the regulation is reasonably related to the promotion of the general welfare.
- **Single Parcel Rule.** "Taking" jurisprudence does not divide a single parcel into discrete segments to determine if rights in a particular segment have been entirely abrogated; rather, it focuses on the parcel as a whole.
- **Legitimate Public Purpose.** Land-use regulations that promote the "health, safety, morals, or general welfare" are generally upheld, even if they prohibit the most beneficial use of the property.
- **Transferable Development Rights (TDRs).** TDRs, while not necessarily "just compensation" if a taking occurred, can mitigate financial burdens and are considered when assessing the impact of regulation.

**Reasoning**
- The Court recognized that states and cities may enact land-use restrictions to enhance the quality of life by preserving historic and aesthetic features.
- Appellants did not dispute that historic preservation is a permissible governmental goal or that the restrictions were appropriate means to achieve it.
- The Court rejected the argument that denying the ability to exploit "air rights" above the Terminal automatically constitutes a taking, emphasizing that takings jurisprudence considers the parcel as a whole, not discrete segments.
- The Court found that the Landmarks Law did not interfere with Penn Central's present use of the Terminal as a railroad station with office space and concessions, which was its primary expectation.
- The Court concluded that Penn Central could still obtain a "reasonable return" on its investment, as found by the New York courts.
- The Court noted that the Commission had not prohibited all construction above the Terminal, only the specific large-scale plans submitted, and Penn Central had not sought approval for smaller structures.
- The transferable development rights (TDRs) afforded to Penn Central, while not necessarily "just compensation" for a taking, were found to be valuable and mitigated the financial burdens of the law.
- The Court distinguished this case from *Pennsylvania Coal Co. v. Mahon* because the Landmarks Law did not make it commercially impracticable to use the property and did not have the same effect as destroying rights.
- The governmental action was characterized as a public program promoting the general welfare, not a physical invasion or appropriation for a uniquely public function.
- The law applies to a large number of structures, providing assurances against arbitrariness, and the benefits of landmark preservation accrue to all citizens, including the property owners.

**Dissent (Rehnquist)**
- The dissent argued that the landmark designation imposed a substantial cost on Penn Central with little or no offsetting benefit, unlike typical zoning where there is "an average reciprocity of advantage."
- It contended that the law imposed an affirmative duty on Penn Central to maintain the Terminal at its own expense and severely restricted its use, subjecting it to a nonconsensual servitude.
- The dissent asserted that the city was not prohibiting a "noxious use" of property, but rather preventing a beneficial use (construction of an office building) for aesthetic reasons.
- It believed that the government was forcing a few individuals to bear public burdens that should be borne by the public as a whole, which the Fifth Amendment prohibits.
- The dissent questioned whether TDRs constituted "just compensation," noting the New York Court of Appeals' acknowledgment of their defects and speculative market value, and would have remanded for a determination on this point.
- It warned that the Court's decision allowed the city to achieve its desire to improve the public condition by a "shorter cut than the constitutional way of paying for the change," echoing Justice Holmes in *Pennsylvania Coal Co. v. Mahon*.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is a landmark (pun intended) in regulatory takings jurisprudence, establishing the **ad hoc factual inquiry** test. It is often contrasted with *Pennsylvania Coal Co. v. Mahon*, which found a taking, to illustrate the difficulty in drawing the line between a legitimate regulation and a compensable taking. It also introduces the concept of **transferable development rights (TDRs)** as a mitigating factor in takings analysis.
2. The dissent raises an important question about the "parcel as a whole" rule and how to define the relevant property interest when air rights are restricted. It also highlights the "average reciprocity of advantage" concept from *Pennsylvania Coal Co. v. Mahon* as a key distinction between general zoning and specific landmark designations.

**Takeaway:** A government regulation that restricts the use of private property for a legitimate public purpose, such as historic preservation, does not constitute a compensable taking if it allows the owner a reasonable beneficial use of the property as a whole and does not interfere with distinct investment-backed expectations.

---

## Lucas v. South Carolina Coastal Council (Supreme Court of the United States, 1992)

**Facts**
- In 1986, David Lucas purchased two beachfront lots on the Isle of Palms, South Carolina, for $976,000, intending to build single-family homes.
- At the time of purchase, the lots were zoned for single-family residential construction, and there were no restrictions on such use.
- In 1988, the South Carolina Legislature enacted the Beachfront Management Act.
- The Act established a "baseline" landward of Lucas's parcels and prohibited the construction of permanent habitable structures seaward of a line 20 feet landward of this baseline.
- The Act's prohibition rendered Lucas's parcels "valueless" according to a state trial court.

**Procedural History**
- Lucas sued in the South Carolina Court of Common Pleas, arguing the Act constituted a taking without just compensation.
- The trial court found a taking and ordered compensation of $1,232,387.50.
- The South Carolina Supreme Court reversed, finding that the Act was designed to prevent serious public harm, and thus no compensation was due.
- Lucas appealed to the U.S. Supreme Court, which granted certiorari.

**Issue**
Does a state regulation that deprives a landowner of all economically beneficial use of their property constitute a taking requiring just compensation, even if the regulation is intended to prevent serious public harm?

**Holding**
Lucas (petitioner) wins. A state regulation that deprives a landowner of all economically beneficial use of their property constitutes a taking requiring just compensation, unless the proscribed use was already prohibited by background principles of the state's law of property and nuisance. Reversed and remanded.

**Rules**
- **Categorical Regulatory Taking.** When a regulation deprives land of all economically beneficial or productive use, it is a categorical taking requiring just compensation.
- **Physical Invasion Rule.** Regulations that compel a property owner to suffer a physical invasion of their property are categorical takings.
- **Noxious Use/Harm-Preventing Principle (Rejected as sole justification).** The government may not avoid compensation for a total taking by simply asserting that the regulation prevents a "harmful or noxious use" of property, as the distinction between harm-preventing and benefit-conferring regulation is often subjective.
- **Background Principles of Nuisance and Property Law Exception.** A state may resist compensation for a regulation that deprives land of all economically beneficial use only if the proscribed use interests were not part of the owner's title to begin with, meaning they were already prohibited by background principles of the state's law of property and nuisance.
- **Legislative Declaration Insufficient.** A state cannot avoid compensation for a total taking merely by declaring that the prohibited uses are inconsistent with the public interest or violate a common-law maxim; it must identify specific background principles of nuisance and property law that prohibit the intended uses.

**Reasoning**
- The Court has recognized two categories of regulatory action that are compensable without a case-specific inquiry into the public interest: physical invasions and regulations that deny all economically beneficial or productive use of land.
- Total deprivation of beneficial use is, from the landowner's perspective, equivalent to a physical appropriation.
- Regulations that leave land economically idle carry a heightened risk that private property is being pressed into public service under the guise of mitigating public harm.
- The "harmful or noxious uses" principle was an early attempt to explain why government could affect property values without compensation, but the distinction between "harm-preventing" and "benefit-conferring" regulation is often subjective.
- The legislature's declaration that a use is harmful cannot be the sole basis for departing from the categorical rule that total regulatory takings must be compensated.
- To avoid compensation for a total taking, the state must show that the proscribed use was never part of the owner's title, based on background principles of state property and nuisance law.
- This inquiry involves analyzing the degree of harm to public resources or adjacent property, the social value of the activities, their suitability to the locality, and the ease of avoiding harm.
- It is unlikely that common-law principles would prevent the erection of any habitable or productive improvements on Lucas's land, as they rarely prohibit the "essential use" of land.
- The case is ripe for review because the South Carolina Supreme Court decided the case on the merits, and Lucas had no reason to pursue a "temporary taking" theory at trial when the Act was permanent.

**Dissent (Blackmun)**
- The Court should not have reviewed the case because Lucas's claim was not ripe, as he had not sought a special permit under the amended Act or challenged the administrative determination of his property's status.
- The trial court's finding that the property was "valueless" was erroneous, as Lucas still retained other attributes of ownership (e.g., right to exclude, recreational uses, right to alienate).
- The Court improperly shifted the burden of proof to the state to justify its legislative judgments, departing from the traditional presumption of constitutionality.
- The new categorical rule for total takings is inconsistent with prior precedent, which consistently weighed public and private interests even when regulations had significant economic effects.
- The "background principles of nuisance and property law" exception is unworkable because the distinction between harm-preventing and benefit-conferring is subjective, and common-law nuisance principles are vague and manipulable.
- The Court's historical compact argument is flawed; early American law often permitted extensive regulation of property for public benefit without compensation, and the Takings Clause was not initially understood to apply to regulations.

**Dissent (Stevens)**
- The Court should have exercised judicial restraint and avoided premature adjudication of a constitutional question, as Lucas had not exhausted administrative remedies under the amended Act.
- The categorical rule for total regulatory takings is unsound and unwise, as it is arbitrary (e.g., 95% diminution gets nothing, 100% gets full value) and will lead to manipulation of property definitions by developers.
- The Court's justification for the categorical rule is weak; the landowner's perspective of total deprivation is not sufficient, the rarity of total takings does not justify a per se rule, and the risk of "singling out" is not necessarily correlated with total takings.
- The nuisance exception is too rigid and narrow, effectively freezing the state's common law and denying legislatures their traditional power to adapt law to changing circumstances (e.g., environmental concerns).
- The Court's approach conflicts with the ad hoc, factual inquiry required for takings analysis, which involves weighing fairness and justice and balancing private and public interests.
- The generality of the Beachfront Management Act, which regulated the entire coastline and affected developed and undeveloped land alike, indicates it was not an effort to expropriate specific owners.
- The compelling purpose of the Act to protect life and property from storm damage, combined with the generality of the regulation and the inherent risks of beachfront investment, means the Act should not be considered a taking.

**Statement (Souter)**
- The writ of certiorari should be dismissed as improvidently granted because the unreviewable assumption that the property was rendered valueless is questionable and frustrates the Court's ability to clarify the legal premises.
- The concept of "total deprivation" is uncertain under existing law, and the Court's indirect approach to defining it through exceptions is problematic.
- Nuisance law typically focuses on conduct and usually leaves property owners with other reasonable uses, making it unlikely that nuisance abatement would result in a complete deprivation of all beneficial land use.

**NOTES:**
1. This case is a landmark in **regulatory takings** jurisprudence, establishing a bright-line rule for when a regulation goes "too far" and requires compensation. It builds on Pennsylvania Coal Co. v. Mahon by defining a specific scenario where a regulation is a per se taking, rather than relying solely on the ad hoc balancing test. It contrasts with Penn Central Transportation Co. v. New York City, which articulated the multi-factor balancing test for partial regulatory takings, by creating a categorical rule for total deprivations.
2. The "background principles of nuisance and property law" exception is a key area for discussion. How broadly or narrowly should courts interpret these "background principles"? Could a state legislature effectively codify new "nuisance" laws to avoid compensation for future regulations?
